Spectral Clustering with Side Information
Hendrik Fichtenberger Google Research Michael Kapralov EPFL Ekaterina Kochetkova EPFL Silvio Lattanzi Google Research Davide Mazzali EPFL Weronika Wrzos-Kaminska EPFL

Abstract
In the graph clustering problem with a planted solution, the input is a graph on n n vertices partitioned into k k clusters, and the task is to infer the clusters from graph structure. A standard assumption is that clusters induce well-connected subgraphs (i.e. Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) \Omega(1) -expanders), and connections between clusters are sparse (i.e. clusters form ϵ \epsilon -sparse cuts). Such a graph defines the clustering uniquely up to ≈ ϵ \approx\epsilon misclassification rate, and efficient algorithms for achieving this rate are known. While this vanilla version of graph clustering is extremely well studied, in the practice of graph analysis vertices of the graph are typically equipped with labels, or features, that provide additional information on cluster ids of the vertices. For example, each vertex could be equipped with a cluster label that is corrupted independently with probability δ \delta . Using either of the two sources of information separately leads to misclassification rate min ⁡ { ϵ , δ } \min\{\epsilon,\delta\} , but can one combine the two to achieve misclassification rate ≈ ϵ ​ δ \approx\epsilon\delta ? In this paper, we give an affirmative answer to this question, and furthermore show that such a misclassification rate can be achieved in sublinear time in the number of vertices n n . Our key algorithmic insight is a new observation on “spectrally ambiguous” vertices in a well-clusterable graph. While our sublinear-time classifier achieves the nearly optimal ≈ O ~ ​ ( ϵ ​ δ ) \approx\widetilde{O}(\epsilon\delta) misclassification rate, the approximate clusters that it outputs do not necessarily induce expanders in the graph G G . In our second result, we give a polynomial-time algorithm for reweighting edges of the input graph from the original ( k , ϵ , Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) ) (k,\epsilon,\Omega(1)) -clusterable instance to a ( k , O ~ ​ ( ϵ ​ δ ) , Ω ​ ( 1 ) ) (k,\widetilde{O}(\epsilon\delta),\Omega(1)) -clusterable instance (for constant k k ), improving sparsity of cuts nearly optimally and preserving expansion properties of the communities – an algorithm for refining community structure of the input graph.



1
Introduction

In the clustering problem one is given
n
data items and the task is to partition them into similar groups, or clusters. The clustering is sometimes based on individual feature embeddings of the data items, such as in the
k
-means problem and its variants, or pairwise similarity information, such as in graph clustering (or its signed version, namely correlation clustering). In the latter case, the algorithm is given a graph
G=(V,E)
and is tasked with partitioning vertices of
G
into
k
densely connected subgraphs, or “communities”, with few edges crossing from one community to another. This vanilla graph clustering problem has received considerable attention in the literature

[
21
,
36
,
27
,
23
,
25
,
12
,
22
,
13
,
6
]
, but in the practice of graph analysis one very often has access not just to the graph, but also to feature vectors associated with the vertices. One example is the
node property prediction
problem: see
[
17
]
for the problem formulation and
[
38
,
24
]
for some of the best clustering approaches. In the node property prediction problem one would like to cluster vertices based on their features as well as the structure of a graph that they belong to. Examples include predicting the conference that a paper appeared at given its abstract and the citation graph, predicting category of an Amazon product given the product description and the co-purchasing graph, or predict the presence of protein functions (i.e. labels on individual proteins) given a set of edges representing interactions between proteins, each equipped with a feature vector. Thus, an exciting broad direction is the following.

How does one optimally cluster a graph equipped with vertex features,

i.e. perform clustering with side information?

This question has been nicely answered in the literature on the stochastic block model
[
10
,
28
,
26
]
, where the graph with planted cluster structure is sampled and presented to the algorithm together with labels on vertices that represent cluster ids, but are independently corrupted with some small probability. Recently, also local clustering algorithms have been proposed for clustering problems with side information
[
37
]
based on a general contextual random graph model, which generalize both the stochastic block model and classic random models for planted cluster model. In contrast, our work considers a worst-case model of graphs, where the notion of “communities” is based on conductance.

Definition 1.1

(Conductance)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph, and let
\emptyset\neq S\subseteq V
. We define the conductance of
S
in
G
, denoted
\Phi_{G}(S)
, as the ratio between the number of edges
|E(S,V\setminus S)|
across
S
and the volume of the smaller side
\min\{\mathrm{vol}(S),\mathrm{vol}(V\setminus S)\}
1
1

1

The notation
\mathrm{vol}(S)
denotes the sum of degree of vertices in
S
.
. Furthermore, we define the conductance of
G
, denoted
\Phi(G)
, as the minimum
\Phi_{G}(S)
over all such cuts
S
.

Specifically, we work with worst-case graphs with a planted clustering as per the following definition.

Definition 1.2

(
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph, let
k\geq 2
be an integer, and let
\epsilon,\phi\in(0,1)
. We say that a partitioning
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
of
V
is a
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering of
G
if for every
i\in[k]
one has
\Phi_{G}(C_{i})\leq\epsilon
and
2
2

2

The notation
G\{C_{i}\}
refers to the subgraph of
G
induces
C_{i}
where we measure volumes with respect to the degrees in
G
.
\Phi(G\{C_{i}\})\geq\phi
. Furthermore, we say that
G
is
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clusterable, or that it admits a
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering, if there exists such a partitioning
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
of
V
.

The problem of clustering such graphs has received a lot of attention in the literature
[
7
,
5
,
13
]
. A given
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clusterable graph can admit multiple
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clusterings, even though all such clusterings of a given graph must be
\epsilon/\mathrm{poly}(\phi)
-close to each other (see
Figure

1
and discussion at the end of
Section

2
for mode details). Thus, existing work has focused on efficiently recovering a clustering that is
\epsilon/\mathrm{poly}(\phi)
-accurate
[
7
,
5
,
13
]
. In this paper we ask a different question.

Can one recover a much better than
\epsilon/\mathrm{poly}(\phi)
-approximation to a
target clustering

C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{k}
of an input
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clusterable graph given
side information
in the form of vertex labels perturbed with probability
\delta\ll 1
?

Specifically, we consider a setting where we are given a
d
-regular
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clusterable graph together with a labeling of vertices that provides a hint as to which cluster each vertex belongs to in the target clustering
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{k}
, where these hints are wrong with probability
\delta>0
for every given vertex, independently. More formally, we work in the following model:

Input model (IM)
.

Let
V
be a set of
n
elements, let
k\geq 2
be an integer, let
\epsilon,\phi\in(0,1)
be the conductance parameters, let
\eta\geq 1
be the balance parameter, and let
\delta\in(0,1)
be the label perturbation parameter. An adversary performs the following steps:

1.

it picks a partitioning
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
of
V
such that
|C_{i}|/|C_{j}|\leq\eta
for all
i,j\in[k]
;

2.

it picks an integer
d\geq 3
and a
d
-regular graph
G=(V,E)
such that
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
is a
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering of
G
as per
Definition

1.2
;

3.

for every
u\in V
, it picks a distribution
p(u)
over
[k]
so that the probability of
i\sim p(u)
being equal to the id of the cluster
u
belongs to, i.e.
u\in C_{i}
, is at least
1-\delta
;

4.

independently for every vertex
u\in V
, it samples a label
\sigma(u)
from the distribution
p(u)
.

Then, an algorithm is given
G,\sigma
, and the parameters
n,d,k,\epsilon,\phi,\eta
as input.

Remark 1.3
.

For simplicity of the presentation, we only consider
d
-regular graphs in our input model. The algorithms which we develop in this paper, however, can be applied to
d
-bounded degree graphs, with the notion of conductance (
Definition

1.1
) normalised by
d|S|
rather than
\mathrm{vol}(S)
, via a standard reduction. More specifically, we convert a
d
-bounded degree graph into a
d
-regular graph by adding
d-\deg(x)
self-loops to each vertex
x\in V
and apply the algorithms to the resulting graph.

The goal is to design an algorithm that, given a clusterable graph and labeling of its vertices as above, determines which cluster every vertex belongs to as accurately as possible. As mentioned above, the graph alone already determines the clustering up to misclassification rate
3
3

3

The misclassification rate is the fraction of vertices for which the inferred cluster id does not correspond to the target clustering (see the algorithmic goal
(AG)
).
of
\epsilon/\mathrm{poly}(\phi)
, i.e. incorrectly assigns at most an
\epsilon/\mathrm{poly}(\phi)
fraction of vertices. The labels themselves can be used to guess the clustering up to an
O(\delta)
misclassification rate, and it is not hard to show that a misclassification rate smaller than the product of the two, i.e.
\epsilon\delta/\mathrm{poly}(\phi)
, is not possible – see
Section

2
. Thus, our goal is to obtain an algorithm with misclassification rate
\gamma
as close to the product
\epsilon\delta/\mathrm{poly}(\phi)
as possible. Formally, we seek to do the following:

Algorithmic goal (AG)
.

Given
G
,
\sigma
, and the parameters
k,\phi,\eta
as per the input model above, output a labeling
\alpha:V\to[k]
that with good probability has misclassification rate at most
\gamma\in(0,1)
, i.e. the partitioning
\widehat{C}_{1}=\{u\in V:\,\alpha(u)=1\},\dots,\widehat{C}_{k}=\{u\in V:\,\alpha(u)=k\}
satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left|\widehat{C}_{i}\triangle C_{i}\right|\leq\gamma n\,.

The algorithm has access to two noisy observations derived from the target partitioning
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
: the graph
G
and the labels
\sigma
. The idea is that combining these two distinct observations of the same signal, the algorithm is able to approximately recover the target partitioning. For this intuition to make sense at all, we restrict ourselves to work in a parameter setting where each of these signals is somewhat useful in isolation.

Parameter setting (PS)
.

In Sections
1
and
2
, we restrict the input model above to
k,\eta=O(1)
,
\phi=\Omega(1)
, and
\epsilon,\delta\ll 1
for simplicity of presentation.

Under the above parameter setting, our central question is the following.

Can an efficient algorithm that sees both
G
and
\sigma
achieve misclassification rate
\gamma\approx{\epsilon\delta}
,
hence doing better than what can be done by looking at either graph structure or labels in isolation?

Our main result is an affirmative answer to the above question. In fact, we even show that such misclassification rate can be achieved by a sublinear time clustering oracle, i.e., a small space data structure that allows sublinear (in the number of vertices
n
) time access to the
\approx\epsilon\delta
-approximate clustering. The clusters that our algorithm returns, however, do not necessarily induce expanders, like in the original input instance. We then also ask the following.

Can an efficient algorithm that sees both
G
and
\sigma

refine
the original
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clusterable graph to a
(k,\epsilon^{\prime},\phi)
-clusterable graph for
\epsilon^{\prime}\approx\epsilon\delta
?

1.1
Our results

Spectral clustering oracles with side information

One might wonder why we are asking specifically about misclassification rate
\epsilon\delta
. The reason is that, even when we are given both signals, we cannot beat the product of what each of them can provide in isolation. In the next paragraph, we show an example supporting this claim.

Why
\epsilon\delta
is the best we can hope for.

To show this, let us fix
V
and
k=2
. We show a partitioning
C_{1},C_{2}
of
V
that is a
(2,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering of a graph
G=(V,E)
, but such graph
G
is completely uninformative about
\epsilon n
vertices. Since the labeling will be wrong on a
\delta
-fraction of these
\epsilon n
vertices, we are bound to misclassify an
\epsilon\delta
-fraction of vertices. We construct the graph
G
as follows (see
Figure

1
): we partition
V
into
A,B
and a set of middle vertices
M
, where
|A|,|B|\approx n/2
and
|M|\approx\epsilon n
; we define
G[A]
and
G[B]
to be
\phi
-expanders; we symmetrically connect every middle vertex in
M
to
d/2
neighbors in
A
and in
B
. One can see that any partitioning of
V
into
C_{1},C_{2}
such that
A\subseteq C_{1}
,
B\subseteq C_{2}
, and where
M
is equally split between
C_{1},C_{2}
, is a valid
(2,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering of
G
. In this sense,
G
is uninformative about the
\epsilon n
middle vertices
M
.

A
B
M
C_{1}
C_{2}
\widetilde{C}_{1}
\widetilde{C}_{2}
vertices in
M
may be in any cluster

Figure 1:
In the clustering
C_{1},C_{2}
(dashed circles), the vertices in
M
belong to
C_{1}
; in the clustering
\widetilde{C}_{1},\widetilde{C}_{2}
(dotted circles), the vertices in
M
belong to
\widetilde{C}_{2}
. Since both are valid
(2,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering of this graph, it is uninformative as to which cluster the vertices in
M
belong to.

Our main result is an algorithm that meets the optimal misclassification rate of
\epsilon\delta
up to a logarithmic factor in
1/\delta
, thus positively answering our question above. Moreover, this algorithm classifies any given vertex in sublinear time.

Theorem 1.4

(Classifier, see
6.1
)
.

There is a
d\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon)}\mathrm{poly}(\log(n/\delta))
-time
4
4

4

For the stated preprocessing time, we additionally require
\epsilon\geq 1/\mathrm{poly}(\log(n))
. For more details, see
6.1
and
Remark

6.2
.
algorithm that, given
G
and
\sigma
as per
(IM)
and respecting the parameter setting
(PS)
, prepares a data structure that features query time
d\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon)}\mathrm{poly}(\log(n/\delta))
such that with probability
0.9
over the internal randomness of the algorithm and the draw of
\sigma
, all but an
\widetilde{O}(\epsilon\delta)
5
5

5

Here, the notation
\widetilde{O}(\cdot)
hides a
\log(1/\delta)
factor.
fraction of queries
u\in V
are answered with a cluster id
i\in[k]
such that
u\in C_{i}
, i.e. it has misclassification rate
\widetilde{O}(\epsilon\delta)
.

Remark 1.5
.

Theorems
1.4
,
1.6
and
1.7
are informal versions of our main results which, in particular, apply to a more general setting of parameters.

Refining community structure with side information

Our
1.4
demonstrates that using the additional signal from
\sigma
can significantly enhance the recovery of the target clustering, far beyond what is achievable by analyzing
G
alone. Our second result answers our last question, showing that we can embed the signal from
\sigma
in
G
by reweighting its edges to refine its community structure. In other words, we use
\sigma
to morph
G
into a new graph that carries a much better signal of the target clustering compared to
G
.

Theorem 1.6

(Refining communities – see
7.1
)
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
G
and
\sigma
as per
(IM)
and respecting the parameter setting
(PS)
, outputs a reweighting of
G
that with probability
0.9
over
\sigma
admits a
(k,\widetilde{O}(\epsilon\delta),\Omega(1))
-clustering
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{k}^{\prime}
that is
\widetilde{O}(\epsilon\delta)
-close to the target clustering i.e.
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}^{\prime}\triangle C_{i}|=\widetilde{O}(\epsilon\delta)\cdot n
.

In fact, our result is more general: given any labeling which is correct on all but
\gamma\ll\epsilon
vertices, without any assumption on how it is generated, we can reweight
G
to boost the sparsity of cuts between clusters from
\epsilon
to
\approx\gamma
.

Theorem 1.7

(Refining communities, general – see
7.2
)
.

Let
d\geq 3
,
k=O(1)
,
\gamma\ll\epsilon\ll 1
, let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph which admits a
(k,\epsilon,\Omega(1))
-clustering
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
such that
|C_{i}|/|C_{j}|=O(1)
for all
i,j\in[k]
, and let
\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]
be a labeling such that
u\notin C_{\alpha(u)}
for all but a
\gamma
-fraction of
u\in V
. Then, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
G
and
\alpha
, outputs a reweighting of
G
that admits a
(k,O(\gamma),\Omega(1))
-clustering
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{k}^{\prime}
that is
O(\gamma)
-close to the target clustering, i.e.
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}^{\prime}\triangle C_{i}|=O(\gamma)\cdot n
.

One can see that
1.7
and
1.4
together readily give
1.6
: we can compute a label
\alpha(u)
for every vertex with the classifier from
1.4
, which is correct on all but
\widetilde{O}(\epsilon\delta)
vertices, and then run the algorithm from
1.7
on
G
and
\alpha
.

1.2
Related work

As we mentioned before, the problem of recovering the planted clustering in clusterable graphs has been well-studied in the literature. One of the first examples is the work of Goldreich and Ron
[
14
]
on expansion testing, which essentially considered the setting
k=1
. Follow-up works
[
20
,
29
,
14
,
8
,
19
]
resulted in clustering oracles that work for
k=2
and any small constant
\epsilon>0
, using similar collision counting primitives for approximating the spectral embeddings. Furthermore, the work of
[
7
]
was the first to consider the case of general
k
, but only for
\epsilon\ll 1/({\text{poly}(k)\log n})
. The case of general
\epsilon
and general
k
required new techniques. To extract more structure from random walk distributions,
[
5
,
13
]
introduced new linear algebraic post-processing techniques.

A relatively newer body of work examines stochastic block models (SBM) augmented by noisy vertex labels. For example,
[
10
,
28
,
26
]
focused on a version where nodes receive noisy labels (or feature vectors), and proposed computationally efficient algorithms achieving optimal recovery. Several works build on the idea of using the information coming from the noisy labels jointly with the information about the graph structure, such as
[
30
,
18
]
, which allows for accurate recovery algorithms even if the vertex labels are misleading.

2
Technical overview

In this section, we discuss the main ideas behind our results. In
Section

2.1
, we begin by describing a few natural attempts to design an algorithm that combines
G
and
\sigma
to achieve a misclassification rate of
\epsilon\delta
. In
Section

2.2
, we present the main technical tools used to achieve this rate, thereby proving
1.4
. Finally, in
Section

2.3
, we show how to reweight
G
to refine its community structure, as stated in
1.7
.

2.1
Some natural approaches and why they fail

We are interested in the setting where we are given a
d
-regular graph
G=(V,E)
and a labeling
\sigma:V\rightarrow[k]
with the following properties:
G
admits an unknown
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
,
\sigma
assigns the correct cluster id with probability
1-\delta
or a wrong cluster id with probability
\delta
independently for each vertex;
k=O(1)
,
\phi=\Omega(1)
,
\epsilon,\delta\ll 1
, and
|C_{i}|/|C_{j}|=O(1)
for all
i,j\in[k]
. The goal is to design an algorithm that given
G
and
\sigma
is able to recover
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
as accurately as possible. As mentioned above, the best one can hope for is a misclassification rate of
\epsilon\delta
. We begin our discussion by considering a few natural attempts at obtaining such an algorithm.

Majority-voting.

One might hope that a simple majority vote among a vertex’s neighbors would suffice to classify it. This fails even for
k=2
, for the following reason: a
(2,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering of
G
allows for
\epsilon n
problematic vertices in, say,
C_{1}
to have
1-\phi
fraction of their neighbors in
C_{2}
. Hence, this approach misclassifies at least an
\epsilon
-fraction of vertices. Even doing a majority vote among a vertex’s
t
-hop neighborhood suffers from the same barrier: most walks starting from a problematic vertex leak to
C_{2}
in just one step, so also their
t
-hop neighborhood mainly consists of vertices from
C_{2}
.

Majority-voting++.

Still in the simplified setting of
k=2
, there is more nuanced (but still very simple) variant of the vanilla majority-voting scheme discussed above that achieves misclassification rate
\approx\epsilon\delta
if
\delta\leq\epsilon
. This can be done by moving the critical threshold from
1/2
(i.e. the usual threshold for majority-voting) to, say,
2/3\cdot\phi
. However, it is not clear how this algorithm can be generalized to the general regime of
\delta
and
d
. We discuss in
Appendix

B
the correctness of this simple classifier and the obstacles in extending it to solve the problem in general.

Naive spectral clustering.

It is known that the spectrum of clusterable graphs is quite informative of the target partitioning. It is then natural to try to combine the information provided by the spectral properties of the graph with the information given by the labeling
\sigma
. Before discussing how one might go about this, we want to be more precise regarding the kind of signal given by the spectrum of
G
: consider the eigenvectors
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
of its normalized Laplacian, and let us define the
k
-dimensional embedding of a vertex
u
to be the vector
f_{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
whose
i
-th entry equals the value of
x_{i}
in
u
’s coordinate. Then, for every
i
we define the cluster mean of
C_{i}
as

\displaystyle\mu_{i}=\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}\sum_{u\in C_{i}}f_{u}\,.

(2.1)

It is known that the cluster means are far from each other and that the spectral embedding of most vertices tends to be close to their cluster mean. Formally, this is captured in the following lemma
[
7
,
13
]
(sometimes referred to as the
variance bound
as it bounds the directional variance of points in the spectral embedding around their cluster means; these cluster means
\mu_{i}
are nearly orthogonal):

Lemma 2.1

(Variance bound– see
Lemma

3.8
,
3.6
)
.

For any
k
-dimensional unit-norm vector
\alpha
, one has

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{u\in C_{i}}\langle f_{u}-\mu_{i},\alpha\rangle^{2}\lesssim\epsilon\,,

and moreover

\forall\,i\neq j\in[k],\,\,\,\left|\langle\mu_{i},\mu_{j}\rangle\right|\lesssim\sqrt{{\epsilon}}\cdot\frac{k}{n}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\forall\,i\in[k],\,\,\,\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\approx\left(1\pm\sqrt{\epsilon}\right)\cdot\frac{k}{n}\,.

It is then natural to distinguish vertices that are close to some cluster mean from vertices that are far from each of them. We say that the vertices of the first kind form
spectral clusters
, while the second kind are
cross vertices
, defined below.

Definition 2.2

(Spectral clusters and cross vertices – see
Definition

4.5
)
.

For
i\in[k]
, we define the
i
-th spectral cluster as

\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)=\{u\in V:\,\|f_{u}-\mu_{i}\|\lesssim\phi\|\mu_{i}\|\},

which are pairwise disjoint by design, since
Lemma

2.1
implies that the
\mu_{i}
’s are far from each other.
Moreover, let

\mathcal{X}=V\setminus\cup_{i\in[k]}\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)

denote the set of cross vertices. The notion of a spectral cluster induces a natural
s
pectral labeling
\tau:V\rightarrow[k]\cup\{*\}
that maps a cross vertex to
*
(undefined) and other vertices to the spectral clusters they belong to:

\tau(u)=\begin{cases}i\,,&\quad\text{if }u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\\
*\,,&\quad\text{if }u\in\mathcal{X}\end{cases}\,.

Remark 2.3
.

A vertex
u\in V
that belongs to
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
, i.e. satisfies
\tau(u)=i
,
does not
necessarily belong to cluster
C_{i}
. Instead,
\tau(u)=i
simply means that spectral information suggests that the cluster of
u
is
i
. In fact, up to
\approx\epsilon/\phi
fraction of vertices can be misclassified by this rule. Our algorithm will combine this prediction with the label
\sigma(u)
carefully to achieve
\approx\epsilon\delta
misclassification rate.

As per
Remark

2.3
, we would ideally like the vertices in the
i
-th spectral cluster to correspond to the vertices in
C_{i}
. However, this is not necessarily true, which motivates the notion of
impostors
: vertices that spectrally look like they belong to
C_{i}
but are actually from
C_{j}
.

Definition 2.4

(Impostors – see
Definition

4.9
)
.

For
i\neq j\in[k]
, we define the set of
(j\to i)
-impostors, denoted
\Gamma(i,j)
, to be the subset of
C_{j}
that lies in the
i
-th spectral cluster, i.e.
\Gamma(i,j)=\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap C_{j}
. Furthermore, let for convenience
\Gamma=\cup_{i\neq j\in[k]}\Gamma(i,j)
be the set of all impostor vertices.

With this notation in place, we can compactly state the recovery guarantee provided by the variance bound: all but
\epsilon n
vertices are close to their cluster mean.

Lemma 2.5

(Few cross and impostor vertices – see
Lemma

4.16
)
.

At most
\approx\epsilon n
vertices are cross or impostor vertices, i.e.

|\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma|\lesssim\epsilon n
.

In order to better appreciate the guarantee of this lemma, we introduce some notation to refer to the true cluster ids.

Notation
.

We use
\iota:V\rightarrow[k]
to denote the true cluster ids, such that
v\in C_{\iota(v)}
for every
v\in V
.

Then,
Lemma

2.5
can be restated as follows.

Remark 2.6
.

Lemma

2.5
guarantees that
\|f_{u}-\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}\lesssim\phi\|\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}^{2}
for all but
\approx\epsilon n
vertices. In other words, one has
\tau(u)=\iota(u)
for all but
\approx\epsilon n
vertices.

In light of this, one might try to combine the spectral information of
G
with the randomly perturbed labeling
\sigma
. A naive attempt to classify a vertex
u\in V
can be the following.

Naive spectral clustering

Input:

G
,
\sigma
, and a vertex
u\in V

Output:
a cluster id in
[k]

if

\tau(u)\neq*

then

\triangleright

u
is in a spectral cluster

return

\tau(u)

else
\triangleright

u
is a cross vertex, i.e.
u\in\mathcal{X}

return

\sigma(u)

end

if

The above algorithm would give misclassification rate
\epsilon\delta
, if we knew that any vertex that is far from its cluster mean is a cross vertex. While this is true for certain classes of random graphs
[
1
,
9
,
2
]
, it is not known for worst-case clusterable graphs: according to the variance bound and
Lemma

2.5
, it is plausible that
\epsilon n
vertices in
C_{i}
are not only far from
\mu_{i}
but also close to
\mu_{j}
. In other words, the naive spectral clustering algorithm is easily fooled by the (possibly
\epsilon n
many) impostor vertices (see
Definition

2.4
and
Lemma

2.5
). While we do not know of an explicit example graph that realizes this possibility, we do not know how to rule it out even when
k=2
. Therefore, all we can say about the algorithm outlined above is that it achieves a misclassification rate of
\epsilon
.

2.2
Our approach

Our goal is to leverage the spectral structure of clusterable graphs together with the perturbed labeling
\sigma
, as in the naive spectral clustering algorithm. That approach attempts to exploit the fact that one can easily identify vertices that lie far from any cluster mean – these are the cross vertices, which belong to no spectral cluster (see
Definition

4.5
) – and assigns labels to them using
\sigma
. The key obstacle to achieving a misclassification rate better than
\epsilon
is that we cannot easily identify the impostor vertices – those that appear close to a wrong cluster (see
Definition

4.9
) – whose existence we cannot rule out or bound more tightly than
\epsilon n
, as previously discussed. However, as it turns out, there is a crucial property of impostors that we can exploit to identify them: vaguely, most of them are able to leave the set of vertices that have the same label
\sigma
and are in the same spectral cluster. To make this more precise, we introduce convenient notation to refer to the set of vertices that have a given label
i
in
\sigma
. These sets, called
label clusters
, can be used by an algorithm as proxies to true clusters
C_{i}
’s.

Definition 2.7

(Label clusters – see
Definition

4.11
)
.

For
i\in[k]
, we define the
i
-th label cluster to be the set
\mathrm{LabelCluster}(i)=\{u\in V:\sigma(u)=i\}
.

Then, consider a vertex
u
in the
i
-th spectral cluster with label
\sigma(u)=j
. We want to test if
u
is a
(j\to i)
-impostor by checking if it can leave the set
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
. Specifically, we check if it can reach the cross vertices
\mathcal{X}
(which are disjoint from
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
by
Definition

2.2
) by walking in the subgraph induced by
(\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j))\cup\mathcal{X}
. We call this subgraph the
(i,j)
-cross graph, defined below and illustrated in
Figure

2
.

Definition 2.8

(Cross graphs – see
Definition

4.13
)
.

For
i\neq j\in[k]
, we define the cross graph
G_{i,j}=(V,E_{i,j})
to be the subgraph induced by the vertices in the
i
-th spectral cluster with label
j
, together with the cross vertices. In other words,
G_{i,j}
is the subgraph induced by the edges on
(\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j))\cup\mathcal{X}
.

\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(l)
vertices in
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
cross vertices
\mathcal{X}
\mu_{i}
\mu_{j}
\mu_{l}

Figure 2:
Illustration of the the cross graph
G_{i,j}
(see
Definition

4.13
). The axes represent three cluster means
\mu_{i},\mu_{j}
and
\mu_{l}
for distinct
i,j,l\in[k]
. The vertex set of
G_{i,j}
can be partitioned into
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
(vertices inside the shaded circle labeled
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
) and
\mathcal{X}
(vertices inside the dashed circle labeled “cross vertices
\mathcal{X}
”). The vertices marked by stars illustrate vertices that truly belong to
C_{j}
.
The black lines illustrate the edges with at least one endpoint in
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
, while edges with both endpoints in
\mathcal{X}
are omitted.
Note that the vertices in
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
that truly belong to
C_{j}
are typically connected to
\mathcal{X}
, while most of the vertices that do not belong to
C_{j}
are not.

With this notation, we can state our main technical insight: all but a small fraction of
(j\to i)
-impostors can leave the set
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
in the cross graph; in particular, they can reach the cross vertices
\mathcal{X}
. Note that this test is amenable to an algorithm, since it can compute
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
,
\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
,
\mathcal{X}
, and therefore test reachability.

Lemma 2.9

(Impostors can reach the cross vertices– see
5.8
,
5.9
)
.

For any
i\neq j\in[k]
, consider the
(j\to i)
-impostors
u\in\Gamma(i,j)
with label
\sigma(u)=j
, i.e. vertices from
C_{j}
with a correct label
\sigma
that are wrongly placed in the
i
-th spectral cluster. In expectation over
\sigma
, all but
\approx\epsilon\delta n
of these vertices are connected to the cross vertices
\mathcal{X}
in the cross graph
G_{i,j}
induced by
(\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j))\cup\mathcal{X}
.

To prove
Lemma

2.9
, we use the following fact: vertices in a spectral cluster that are not directed neighbors of
\mathcal{X}
have few neighbors in other spectral clusters. In other words, spectral clusters are sparsely connected to each other.

Lemma 2.10

(Few neighbors across spectral clusters– see
Lemma

4.15
)
.

Let
i\in[k]
and let
u
be a vertex in the
i
-th spectral cluster that is not a direct neighbor of
\mathcal{X}
, i.e.
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
. Then, the number of neighbors of
u
in spectral clusters other than
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
is at most
\phi/2\cdot d
, i.e.

\left|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\left(\bigcup_{j\neq i}\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)\right)\right|\leq\frac{\phi}{2}\cdot d\,.

Proof sketch.

Since the eigenvalues associated with the first
k
eigenvectors of
G
are bounded by
\approx\epsilon
, the embedding
f_{u}
is very close to the average of the embeddings of the neighbors of
u
, i.e.

\left\|d\cdot f_{u}-\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}f_{v}\right\|_{2}\lesssim d\epsilon\cdot\|f_{u}\|_{2}\approx d\epsilon\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\,,

where the last step follows from
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
. Now, note that the average
\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}f_{v}
is contributed to by
v
’s in
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
and
v
’s in other spectral clusters, since
u\notin\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
. But every
v
in
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
must have an embedding very close to
f_{u}
, which gives

\left\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u):\\
v\in\cup_{j\neq i}\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)\end{subarray}}(f_{u}-f_{v})\right\|_{2}\approx\left\|\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}(f_{u}-f_{v})\right\|_{2}=\left\|d\cdot f_{u}-\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}f_{v}\right\|_{2}\lesssim d\epsilon\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\,.

Recall again that
f_{u}\approx\mu_{i}
. Moreover, for any
v
that is considered in the sum on the left-hand side, we have
f_{v}\approx\mu_{j}
for some
j\neq i
(by the definition of spectral clusters, see
Definition

4.5
). Then, using the fact that all the cluster means
have
\ell_{2}
-norm roughly
\approx\sqrt{k/n}
and are almost orthogonal (by
Lemma

2.1
), we have

\Delta\cdot\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}\lesssim\left\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u):\\
v\in\cup_{j\neq i}\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)\end{subarray}}(f_{u}-f_{v})\right\|_{2}\lesssim d\epsilon\cdot\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}\,,

where
\Delta
is the number of terms in the sum on the left-hand side, i.e.
\Delta=|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\cup_{j\neq i}\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j))|
. Rearranging, we get the claim. The reason why we obtain a bound of
\approx\epsilon d
instead of
\approx\phi d
is that we did not keep careful track of the errors approximating the embeddings by their cluster mean, as well as the errors incurred by assuming the cluster means to be all of equal length.
∎

Equipped with this observation, we can show
Lemma

2.9
.

Proof sketch of
Lemma

2.9
.

The first crucial observation is that we can combine
Lemma

2.10
with the assumption that
C_{j}
induces an expander to argue that the
(j\to i)
-impostors
\Gamma(i,j)
have nice expansion properties.

Claim 2.11

(Impostors expand– see
Lemma

4.17
)
.

Let
i\neq j\in[k]
, and let
S
be a subset of
(j\to i)
-impostors that does not have direct neighbors in
\mathcal{X}
, i.e.
S\subseteq\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
. Then, one has
|E(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)|\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d|S|
.

Proof sketch.

By
Lemma

2.10
, every
u\in S
has
(1-\phi/2)d
of its neighbors in
\Gamma(i,j)
. Now, recall that the
(j\to i)
-impostors
\Gamma(i,j)
are vertices from
C_{j}
, so the cut
S
must
\phi
-expand in
G\{C_{j}\}
. Combining these two observations, we get the claim.
∎

Now, let
Q
be the set of
(j\to i)
-impostors
u\in\Gamma(i,j)
with label
\sigma(u)=j
that cannot reach
\mathcal{X}
in the cross graph
G_{i,j}
. Also, let
R
be the set of
(j\to i)
-impostors
v\in\Gamma(i,j)
with a wrong label
\sigma(v)\neq j
. One can note that
Q
does not overlap
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
, because if it did it would be able to reach
\mathcal{X}
in
G_{i,j}
. Therefore,
2.11
applies, and it gives

|E(Q,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus Q)|\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d|Q|\,.

Next, we upper-bound

|E(Q,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus Q)|
in terms of
R
to prove the lemma. By definition of
Q
, it cannot have edges to
(j\to i)
-impostors with label
\sigma(u)=j
that can reach
\mathcal{X}
. Hence,
|E(Q,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus Q)|
is only contributed to by edges from
Q
to
R
, which we defined to be the set of
(j\to i)
-impostors with a wrong label
\sigma(v)\neq j
. Thus, we have

d|R|\geq|E(Q,R)|\geq|E(Q,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus Q)|\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d|Q|\,,

where the leftmost inequality follows by
d
-regularity of
G
. Rearranging gives
|Q|\lesssim|R|
. Since
R
consists of mislabeled impostors, we can combine
Lemma

2.5
with linearity of expectation to bound its size by
\delta|\Gamma(i,j)|\lesssim\epsilon\delta n
, which proves the result.
∎

This insight provided by
Lemma

2.9
suggests a modification of the naive spectral approach, where we identify both cross vertices and most impostor vertices: the first kind is detected by checking
\tau(u)=*
, while the second kind can be detected by checking connectivity in the cross graphs. There is a small technical obstacle in this approach, which actually also affects naive spectral approach: we cannot exactly compute the spectral clusters or the cross vertices, since these definitions as we have stated them (see
Definition

2.2
) depend on the exact spectral embedding and the exact cluster means, which in turn depend on the clusters
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
. Therefore, our algorithms will rely on an oracle providing approximate access to these quantities.

Definition 2.12

(
\ell_{2}
-oracle, see
Definition

4.1
,
4.4
)
.

We denote by
apx-norm
an oracle that provides approximate access to the quantities needed to define spectral clusters
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
, cross vertices
\mathcal{X}
, and the spectral labeling
\tau
. In particular, this oracle provides approximate access to the
\ell_{2}
distances and
\ell_{2}
norms of spectral embeddings
f_{u}
’s and cluster means
\mu_{i}
’s.

Algorithm

1
outlines our approach: it uses the provided oracle
apx-norm
to compute
\tau(u)
, which determines whether
u
is in a spectral cluster or if it is a cross vertex (see
Definition

2.2
). Then, if the spectral information provided by
\tau(u)
matches the information provided by
\sigma(u)
, i.e. there is
i\in[k]
such that
\tau(u)=\sigma(u)=i
, then we output
i
as the cluster id of
u
(line (
3
)). If instead
\tau(u)=*
, i.e.
u
is a cross vertex, the spectral information is not useful and we output the cluster id given by
\sigma(u)
(line (
4
)). Finally, if the spectral information and the label disagree, i.e.
\tau(u)=i
and
\sigma(u)=j
for some
i\neq j\in[k]
, we want to test if
u
is an impostor or not as suggested by
Lemma

2.9
. Line (
7
) uses the following notation.

Definition 2.13

(Reachability test)
.

For a vertex
u\in V
and a subgraph
H
, we denote by
\textsc{reach}(u,H,\mathcal{X})
a procedure that checks if the vertex
u
can reach the set of cross vertices
\mathcal{X}
in
H
. In our context,
H
will be the
(i,j)
-cross graph
G_{i,j}
induced by the edges on
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
. The procedure
\textsc{reach}(u,H,\mathcal{X})
can be implemented as a simple BFS (as in our polynomial-time algorithm
Algorithm

2
) or as random-walk-based primitive (as in our sublinear-time algorithm
Algorithm

3
).

Algorithm 1

Meta-algorithm generalizing
Algorithm

2
and
Algorithm

Input:

G
,

, an oracle
apx-norm
, a vertex
u\in V

2:

Output:
a cluster id in

then

\triangleright

\triangleright

\left(\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\tau(u))\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(\sigma(u))\right)\cup\mathcal{X}

\triangleright
see

2.8

if

\textsc{reach}(u,H,\mathcal{X})
returns “yes”
then

\triangleright

u
can reach
\mathcal{X}
in the cross graph
H
, see
Definition

2.13

\sigma(u)

\triangleright
trust the label,
u
is likely an impostor

\triangleright

In
Section

4
, we explicitly define the central spectral objects (spectral cluster, cross vertices, etc.) in terms of these approximate norms, and in
Section

4.2
and
Appendix

A
we discuss how to implement such an oracle. In
Section

6.1
, we detail how to implement the reachability test (see line (
7
)) in sublinear time. In the remainder of this section, for sake of simplicity, we assume that we have access to exact spectral embeddings and cluster means, and we assume that the reachability test is always correct.

We now show how to bound the number of vertices that can possibly be misclassified in lines (
3
), (
4
), (
8
), (
10
).

Lemma 2.14

(Informal version of
Lemma

5.3
)
.

The number of vertices that can be misclassified in lines (
3
) and (
4
) is at most
\approx\delta\epsilon n
in expectation over
\sigma
.

Proof sketch.

We rely on a simple observation: every vertex
u
that is misclassified in line (
3
) must be an impostor whose label
\sigma(u)
is wrong (because
\tau(u)=\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
), and every vertex
u
that is misclassified in line (
4
) must be a cross vertex whose label
\sigma(u)
is wrong (because
\tau(u)=*
and
\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
). By
Lemma

2.5
and linearity of expectation, we get the claimed bound.
∎

Let us now consider the vertices that can be misclassified in lines (
8
), (
10
). The ones misclassified in lines (
8
) can be thought of as the
false positives
for the reachability test, while the ones misclassified in lines (
10
) can be thought of as
false negatives
.
Lemma

2.9
already suggests that there should be few false negatives, as shown below.

Lemma 2.15

(Few false negatives– see
Lemma

5.7
)
.

The number of vertices that can be misclassified in line (
10
) is at most
\approx\delta\epsilon n
in expectation over
\sigma
.

Proof sketch.

Let
u
be any vertex that is misclassified in line (
10
), and let
i,j\in[k]
such that
\tau(u)=i
(i.e.
u
is in the
i
-th spectral cluster) and
\iota(u)=j
(i.e.
u\in C_{j}
). One can observe that, because
u
has been misclassified, we must have
i\neq j
, which means that
u
is a
(j\to i)
-impostor vertex, i.e.
u\in\Gamma(i,j)
. Looking at
\sigma
, there are two types of vertices that can be misclassified in line (
10
): those with the correct label
\sigma(u)=j
and those with a wrong label
\sigma(u)\neq j
. The latter vertices are then impostors with the wrong label, so following the same proof as for
Lemma

2.14
we bound the number of such vertices by
\approx\delta\epsilon n
. For the vertices with the correct label
\sigma(u)=\iota(u)=j
, they must have failed the reachability test in line (
7
) in order to be misclassified in line (
10
). The number of vertices in
\Gamma(i,j)
with the correct label
\sigma(u)=j
that cannot reach
\mathcal{X}
in the cross graph
G_{i,j}
is at most
\approx\epsilon\delta n
by
Lemma

2.9
. Summing up over all
i\neq j\in[k]
gives the claimed bound (recall that
k=O(1)
).
∎

We are left with the task of showing that there are few false positives. Intuitively, they are contained in connected components induced by vertices with a wrong label, and these components can be shown to be small by a Galton-Watson type of argument.

Lemma 2.16

(Few false positives– see
Lemma

5.4
)
.

The number of vertices that can be misclassified in line (
8
) is at most
\approx d\delta\epsilon n
in expectation over
\sigma
.

Proof sketch.

Let
u
be a vertex that is misclassified in line (
8
), and let
i\neq j\in[k]
such that
u
belongs to the
i
-th spectral cluster and to the
j
-th label cluster, i.e.
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
. For
u
to be misclassified in line (
8
), it must be the case that the label
\sigma(u)
is wrong, i.e.
\iota(u)\neq j
. Moreover, it must be the case that
u
can reach
\mathcal{X}
in the
(i,j)
-cross graph
G_{i,j}
. Recall that
G_{i,j}
is the subgraph induced by the edges on
(\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j))\cup\mathcal{X}
. Note that these vertices can be of three types: the cross vertices
\mathcal{X}
, the impostors
\Gamma(i,j)
with the correct label, and the vertices in
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
with a wrong label. Note that our problematic vertex
u
must belong to the latter set. Therefore, the number of such problematic vertices
u
corresponds to the number of vertices that are reachable from a mislabeled vertex in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\Gamma(i,j))
or in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
. This can be thought of as starting a Galton-Watson process from every
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\Gamma(i,j))\cup\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
, where the offspring distribution is
d
with probability
\delta
and
0
with probability
1-\delta
. Hence, the number of such vertices can be bounded by
\approx\delta|\mathrm{N}_{G}(\Gamma(i,j))\cup\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})|
. By
d
-regularity of
G
and
Lemma

2.5
, we get the claim by summing over all pairs
i\neq j\in[k]
(again, recall that
k=O(1)
).
∎

Combining
Lemma

2.14
,
Lemma

2.15
, and
Lemma

2.16
, we conclude the following result.

Lemma 2.17

(
d\epsilon\delta
-rate in polynomial time– see
5.2
)
.

A naive implementation of
Algorithm

1
gives a polynomial-time algorithm with an expected misclassification rate of

\epsilon\delta n+\epsilon\delta n+d\cdot\epsilon\delta n\approx d\cdot\epsilon\delta n\,.

This essentially corresponds to
Algorithm

2
. The analysis of this algorithm, in
Section

5
, is essentially a formal version of the arguments described above. This result is summarized in
5.2
.

Finally, we remark that while the proof sketches of
Lemma

2.15
and
Lemma

2.16
assumed
k=O(1)
, our more careful analysis in
Section

5
gives the misclassification rate
O(\epsilon\delta d)
also for general
k\lesssim 1/\epsilon
.

Removing the factor of
d
.

In
Section

6
, we consider a more refined implementation of
Algorithm

1
, namely,
Algorithm

3
. There, we carry out an inherently more intricate analysis than the one of
5.2
, which allows to get rid of the factor
d
in the misclassification rate, at the cost of an extra
\log(1/\delta)
factor, thereby nearly matching the best possible rate of
\epsilon\delta
. Note that the factor-
d
loss in
Lemma

2.17
comes from the false positives (recall
Lemma

2.16
), i.e. vertices with a wrong label that are able to reach
\mathcal{X}
in their cross graph (see line (
7
) in
Algorithm

1
). Hence, we should make the reachability test harder to pass for vertices with a wrong label, while not making it much harder to pass for the false negatives, i.e. impostors with the correct label. Recall that the way we proved that only few such vertices fail to pass the reachability test in
Lemma

2.9
is by exploiting their expansion properties guaranteed by
2.11
. Therefore, imagine implementing the reachability test
\textsc{reach}(u,H,\mathcal{X})
in line (
7
) so as to check whether
u
has many paths to
\mathcal{X}
: the number of false negatives, i.e. the number of impostors with the correct label, that fail to pass the test remains roughly
\epsilon\delta n
, by virtue of the expansion property we already proved in
2.11
; the number of false positives should intuitively decrease, since a vertex with a wrong label that are able to reach
\mathcal{X}
through multiple paths for it to be misclassified.

Sublinear-time implementation.

Algorithm

1
presented above is actually amenable to being made sublinear time: given the
\ell_{2}
-oracle
apx-norm
, we can check whether a vertex has multiple paths to a target set by running a few random walks of bounded length. Moreover, it is known how to implement
\ell_{2}
-oracle
apx-norm
in sublinear time
[
13
]
. We present the sublinear version of
Algorithm

1

(
Algorithm

3
) and prove its guarantees in
Section

6
.

2.3
Refining communities

Given a graph
G
that admits a
(k,\epsilon,\Omega(1))
-clustering
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
, and given some additional information about the cluster ids of its vertices, we would like to reweight the edges of
G
to obtain a graph
G^{\prime}
with better clusterability in the following sense: for some
\gamma\ll\epsilon
, we demand that
G^{\prime}
admits a
(k,O(\gamma),\Omega(1))
-clustering
C^{\prime}_{1},\dots,C^{\prime}_{k}
that is
\gamma
-close to
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
. Of course, this task would be easy to achieve if we were given the clustering
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
: we can just down-weight every crossing edge in

E_{\mathrm{cross}}=\bigcup_{i\neq j\in[k]}E(C_{i},C_{j})

to have a weight of
0
. The reweighted graph
G^{\prime}
will then have a perfect community structure, as it would consists of
k
connected components, each of which induces an expander.

In reality, we do not have access to
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
. However, we can have approximate access to
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
: with the classifier from the previous section (e.g.
Lemma

2.17
or
1.4
), we can compute a labeling
\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]
that assigns the correct cluster id to all but an
\epsilon\delta
fraction of vertices. In particular,
\alpha
gives approximate access to the set of crossing edges
\cup_{i\neq j}E(C_{i},C_{j})
.

Definition 2.18

(Flagged edges – see
Definition

7.4
)
.

We define the set of edges flagged by
\alpha
, denoted
F
, as the set of edges
(u,v)\in E
whose endpoints are assigned to different clusters by
\alpha
, i.e.
F=\{(u,v)\in E:\,\alpha(u)\neq\alpha(v)\}
.

Lemma 2.19

(Flagged edges approximate crossing edges –
Lemma

7.5
)
.

If
\alpha
has a misclassification rate of
\gamma
, then the set of flagged and crossing edges differ in at most
\gamma dn
edges, i.e.
|F\triangle E_{\mathrm{cross}}|\leq\gamma dn
.

Proof sketch.

Every vertex misclassified by
\alpha
contributes at most
d
edges to
|F\triangle E_{\mathrm{cross}}|
.
∎

In light of this, one might hope that down-weighting every edge in
F
to
0
results in a graph
G^{\prime}
that consists of
k
disjoint expanders. However, this hope is readily dashed, as we now illustrate. Consider a vertex
u\in C_{1}
with, say,
3
neighbors
a,b,c
in
C_{1}
; then, if
\alpha
incorrectly flags
(u,a),(u,b),(u,c)
(which is admissible by
Lemma

2.19
), the vertex
u
will be isolated in the resulting graph
G^{\prime}
. The presence of an isolated vertex implies that
G^{\prime}
does not admit a
(k,\xi,\Omega(1))
-clustering for any
\xi>0
.

This observation prompts us with the idea of down-weighting the flagged edges so as to minimize the weight of the flagged edges
F
in
G^{\prime}
subject to the subgraphs
G^{\prime}\{C_{i}\}
remaining
\Omega(1)
-expanders. This idea cannot be implemented directly, as we do not have exact access to
G^{\prime}\{C_{i}\}
. Instead, we resort to the notion of
multi-way conductance
, defined below.

Definition 2.20

(Multi-way conductance
[
23
]
– see
Definition

7.11
)
.

The
(k+1)
-th way conductance of
G^{\prime}
is the value
\rho_{k+1}(G^{\prime})
such that no matter how we pick
k+1
disjoint cuts
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k+1}
in
G^{\prime}
, one of them will be
\rho_{k+1}(G^{\prime})
-expanding, i.e.

\rho_{k+1}(G^{\prime})=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k+1}\subseteq V:\\
\forall\,i\neq j,\,Q_{i}\cap Q_{j}=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\,\,\,\max_{i\in[k+1]}\Phi_{G^{\prime}}(Q_{i})\,.

We will use this quantity as a proxy for the conductance of the
G^{\prime}\{C_{i}\}
’s. In particular, we consider the following optimization problem that solves for a weight function
x\in[0,1]^{E}
so as to minimize the weight on the flagged edges subject to
(k+1)
-th way conductance of the reweighted graph
G_{x}=(V,E,x)
remaining large enough.

\displaystyle\text{minimize}

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in F}x_{e}

(2.2)

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle x_{e}=1\quad\forall\,e\in E\setminus F

\displaystyle\rho_{k+1}(G_{x})\geq\Omega(1)

\displaystyle x\in[0,1]^{E}

Let us ignore for a moment the fact that we cannot solve such an optimization problem as is. We can show that a solution
x
yields a graph
G_{x}
that admits a clustering with quality proportional to the objective value of
x
.

Lemma 2.21

(Clustering
G_{x}
–
Lemma

7.18
)
.

If
x
is a feasible solution to the program in (
2.2
) with objective value
\text{OBJ}=\nu\cdot dn
, then the resulting graph
G=(V,E,x)
admits a
(k,O(\nu),\Omega(1))
-clustering
C^{\prime}_{1},\dots,C^{\prime}_{k}
such that
\sum_{i}|C_{i}\triangle C^{\prime}_{i}|\lesssim\nu\cdot n
.

One can also observe that there is a solution to the program in (
2.2
) with objective value
\approx\gamma
.

Lemma 2.22

(Low-objective solution –
Lemma

7.9
)
.

There is a feasible solution
x^{*}
to the program in (
2.2
) with objective value
\approx\gamma dn
. Specifically, this solution gives a weight of
0
to every flagged edge that is also crossing, and leaves every other edge untouched, i.e.
x^{*}=\mathbbm{1}-\mathbbm{1}_{F\cap E_{\mathrm{cross}}}
.

Proof sketch.

Since we use
x^{*}=\mathbbm{1}-\mathbbm{1}_{F\cap E_{\mathrm{cross}}}
, the objective value is given by the flagged edges that are not crossing, i.e.
|F\setminus E_{\mathrm{cross}}|
. By
Lemma

2.19
, we now that this is at most
\gamma dn
. To check feasibility of
x^{*}
, we observe that we are not decreasing the weight of any edge inside the clusters
G\{C_{i}\}
’s, which therefore leaves their conductance unchanged. Hence, however we pick
k+1
disjoint sets
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k+1}
, one of them must cross one of
G_{x}\{C_{1}\},\dots,G_{x}\{C_{k}\}
. Therefore, one of
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k+1}
has conductance at least
\Omega(1)
in
G_{x}
, showing that
\rho_{k+1}(G_{x})\geq\Omega(1)
.
∎

Lemma

2.22
together with
Lemma

2.21
imply that solving the program in (
2.2
) and using the weights it outputs, we obtain a reweighted version of
G
that admits a
(k,O(\gamma),\Omega(1))
-clustering
C^{\prime}_{1},\dots,C^{\prime}_{k}
such that
\sum_{i}|C_{i}\triangle C^{\prime}_{i}|\lesssim\gamma\cdot n
. To turn the program in (
2.2
) into a polynomial-time algorithm, we use higher-order Cheeger inequalities
[
23
]
to rewrite the constraint
\rho_{k+1}(G_{x})\geq\Omega(1)
as an eigenvalue constraint. We exploit the clusterability of the input graph
G
to further rewrite the eigenvalue constraint as a semidefinite constraint, which gives
1.7
.

3
Preliminaries and notation

Graph notation.

Given a graph
G=(V,E)
and a set
S\subseteq V
, we let
E(S)
denote the set of edges with both endpoints in
S
, and we let
G[S]
denote the induced subgraph on
S
, that is
G[S]\coloneqq(S,E(S)).

We use the notation
G\{S\}
to denote the graph
G[S]
to which self-loops have been added so that every vertex in
G\{S\}
has the same degree as in
G
. The volume of
S
, denoted
\mathrm{vol}(S)
, is defined as the sum of degrees of vertices in
S
. If
G
has self-loops, we write it as a triple
G=(V,E,\ell)
, where
(V,E)
is a simple graph (so the self-loops are not represented as edges) and
\ell
maps each
u\in V
to the number of self-loops on
u
. The degree of a vertex in
G=(V,E,\ell)
is the number of its incident edges plus the number of its self-loops, and the volume of
S\subseteq V
uses this notion of degree. The adjacency matrix of
G=(V,E,\ell)
is the adjacency matrix of
(V,E)
plus
\text{diag}(\ell)
. The set of neighbors of a vertex
u\in V
is the set
\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)=\{v\in V:(u,v)\in E\}
, and the set of neighbors of
S\subseteq V
is the set
\mathrm{N}_{G}(S)=\cup_{u\in S}\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)
.

Linear algebra notation.

For a vertex
v\in V
, we write
\mathbbm{1}_{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
for the indicator vector of
v
, that is the vector that is
1
at index
v
and
0
elsewhere. Similarly, for a set
S\subseteq V
, we write
\mathbbm{1}_{S}
for the indicator of
S
. For a matrix
M
, we write
\|M\|_{\mathrm{op}}
for its operator (spectral) norm,
\|M\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\max_{\|x\|_{2}=1}\|Mx\|_{2}.

Graph access model.

In this paper, we work with the
bounded-degree graph model
. This means that, for a graph
G=(V,E)
with degrees bounded by
d
, we can specify a vertex
v\in V
as well as a number
i:1\leq i\leq d
, and access the
i
-th neighbor of the vertex
v
in constant time.

3.1
Facts from spectral graph theory

Throughout this section, let us fix an integer
d\geq 3
and
d
-regular graph
G=(V,E)
, and also fix an integer
k\geq 2
and conductance parameters
\epsilon,\phi\in(0,1)
.
Let
A
be the adjacency matrix of
G
, and let
\mathcal{L}=I-\frac{1}{d}A
denote its normalized Laplacian. Also let
\mathcal{L}=U\Sigma U^{T}
denote its eigendecomposition.

Definition 3.1

(Spectral embedding)
.

Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be the eigenvectors of
\mathcal{L}
sorted in non-decreasing order of eigenvalue. We define the spectral embedding of
V
, denoted
(f_{u})_{u\in V}
, as

f_{u}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle x_{i},\mathbbm{1}_{u}\rangle e_{i}=U_{[k]}^{\top}\mathbbm{1}_{u}

for each
u\in V
, where
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
U_{[k]}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
denotes the matrix with columns
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
.

Lemma 3.2

(Lemma 3 from
[
13
]
)
.

Let
\lambda_{k}
and
\lambda_{k+1}
be the
k
-th and
(k+1)
-th smallest eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}
, respectively.
If
G
is
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clusterable as per
Definition

1.2
, then
\lambda_{k}\leq 2\epsilon
and
\lambda_{k+1}\geq{\phi^{2}}/{2}
.

Remark 3.3
.

If
G
is
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clusterable with
\epsilon/\phi^{2}
small enough, then
it follows from Lemma
3.2
that the space spanned by the bottom
k
eigenvectors of
\mathcal{L}
is uniquely defined, i.e. the choice of
U_{[k]}
is unique up to multiplication by an orthonormal matrix
R\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
on the right. We note
that while the choice of
f_{u}
for
u\in V
is not unique, the dot product
between the spectral embedding of
u\in V
and
v\in V
is well defined, since for every orthonormal

R\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
one has

\langle Rf_{u},Rf_{v}\rangle=(Rf_{u})^{\top}(Rf_{v})=\left(f_{u}\right)^{\top}(R^{\top}R)\left(f_{v}\right)=\left(f_{u}\right)^{\top}\left(f_{v}\right)\,.

Since we are ultimately interested in recovering the clustering by using (also) the spectral embedding, it is useful to know what the “typical” embedding is for a cluster
C_{i}
. Hence, we next define the cluster means.

Definition 3.4

(Cluster means)
.

Let
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
be a partitioning of
V
and let
(f_{u})_{u\in V}
be its spectral embedding. We define the cluster means (or cluster centers), denoted
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}
, as

\mu_{i}=\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}\sum_{u\in C_{i}}f_{u}\quad\text{ for each $i\in[k]$.}

Lemma 3.5

(Spectral embedding is close to the average of neighbors)
.

If
G
is
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clusterable, then for every
v\in V
one has

\left\|f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{w\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{w}\right\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon\|f_{v}\|_{2}\,.

Proof.

Let
0\leq\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
denote the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{L}
, and let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be the corresponding eigenvectors.
By
Definition

3.1
we have

U_{[k]}^{\top}\mathcal{L}\mathbbm{1}_{v}=\frac{1}{d}U_{[k]}^{\top}\sum_{w\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}(\mathbbm{1}_{v}-\mathbbm{1}_{w})=f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{w\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{w}.

(3.1)

On the other hand, expanding
\mathbbm{1}_{v}
in the eigenbasis, we obtain

U_{[k]}^{\top}\mathcal{L}\mathbbm{1}_{v}=U_{[k]}^{\top}\mathcal{L}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{v},x_{i}\rangle x_{i}\right)=U_{[k]}^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{v},x_{i}\rangle x_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{v},x_{i}\rangle e_{i},

(3.2)

where
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
form an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
Combining (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), we obtain

\left\|f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{w\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{w}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{v},x_{i}\rangle e_{i}\right\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{v},x_{i}\rangle e_{i}\right\|_{2}=2\epsilon\|f_{v}\|_{2},

where the first inequality uses the fact that
0\leq\lambda_{i}\leq 2\epsilon
for
i=1,\ldots,k
by
Lemma

3.2
.
∎

Lemma 3.6

(Lemma 7 from
[
13
]
)
.

If
G
admits a
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
, then the following hold:

(1)

for all
i\in[k]
, one has

\left|\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}\right|\leq\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}\,;

(2)

for all
i\neq j\in[k]
, one has

|\langle\mu_{i},\mu_{j}\rangle|\leq\frac{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|C_{i}||C_{j}|}}\,.

Remark 3.7
.

Assuming that
{|C_{i}|}/{|C_{j}|}\leq\eta
for all
i,j\in[k]
, one has

\frac{1}{\eta}\cdot\frac{n}{k}\leq|C_{i}|\leq\eta\cdot\frac{n}{k}
for all
i\in[k]
. Combining this with
(1)
and further assuming
\epsilon\leq\phi^{2}/64
, for all
i\in[k]
one has

\frac{1}{2\eta}\cdot\frac{k}{n}\leq\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\leq 2\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}\,.

Lemma 3.8

(Lemma 6 from
[
13
]
; “variance bounds”)
.

If
G
admits a
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
, then for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
\|\alpha\|_{2}=1
we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in C_{i}}\left\langle f_{v}-\mu_{i},\alpha\right\rangle^{2}\leq\frac{4\epsilon}{\phi^{2}}\,.

Lemma 3.9
.

If
G
admits a
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in C_{i}}\|f_{v}-\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{4\epsilon k}{\phi^{2}}\,.

Proof.

Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Applying Lemma
3.8
to
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}
and summing, we obtain

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in C_{i}}\|f_{v}-\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in C_{i}}\left\langle f_{v}-\mu_{i},\alpha_{j}\right\rangle^{2}\leq\frac{4\epsilon k}{\phi^{2}}.

∎

Lemma 3.10

(Lemma 9 from
[
13
]
)
.

If
G
admits a
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
, then for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
\|\alpha\|_{2}=1
we have

\left|\alpha^{\top}\left(I_{k}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right)\alpha\right|\leq\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|\alpha\|^{2}_{2}\,.

4
Model setting and basic concepts

Throughout this paper, we fix an
n
-vertex
d
-regular graph
G=(V,E)
that admits a
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
as per our input model
(IM)
. A complete list of the objects and parameters that we use and assumptions we make on them is presented in
Table

1
and
Table

2
. In the remainder of this section we introduce these objects and prove some of their basic properties.

•

G=(V,E)
— regular graph

•

d\geq 3
— degree of
G

•

n=|V|
— number of vertices in
G

•

\eta=O(1)
— upper bound on
\max_{i,j\in[k]}\frac{|C_{i}|}{|C_{j}|}

•

\epsilon,\phi\in(0,1)
— conductance parameters

•

k\geq 2
— number of communities

•

\{C_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
—
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering of
G
(Def.
1.2
)

•

\mathcal{L}
— normalized Laplacian of
G

•

(f_{u})_{u\in V}
— spectral embedding of
V
(Def.
3.1
)

•

(\mu_{i})_{i\in[k]}
— cluster means (Def.
3.4
)

•

(\widetilde{\mu}_{i})_{i\in[k]}
— approximate cluster means (Def.
4.4
)

•

\delta\in(0,1)
— perturbation parameter

•

{\xi}\in(0,1)
– approximate inner products quality

•

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
– approximate inner products (Def.
4.1
)

•

\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{apx}}
— approximate distance (Rmk.
4.3
)

•

L=\lceil\frac{150}{\phi^{2}}\log(1/\delta)\rceil
— random walk length (Sec.
6
)

•

\epsilon/\phi^{6}\leq 1/(10^{5}{\eta^{4}})
,
\phi^{2}\eta<1/10^{3}

•

k\log k\leq{\phi^{6}}/({10^{9}\eta^{4}\epsilon})

•

\delta d\leq 1/100

•

{\xi}/{n}\leq{\phi^{2}}/{(20^{4}\eta)}\cdot\min_{i}\{\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\}

Table 1:
Parameters and objects used in this section (
Section

4
) and
Sections

5
and
6
.

•

\iota:V\rightarrow[k]
— target labeling:
u\in C_{\iota(u)}

•

\sigma:V\rightarrow[k]
— perturbed labeling as per our input model
(IM)

•

\tau:V\rightarrow[k]\cup\{*\}
— spectral labeling:
u\in\mathcal{X}
if
\tau(u)=*
,
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\tau(u))
otherwise (Def.
4.5
, Lem.
4.7
)

Table 2:
Different labelings of the vertices available to us.

Our algorithm will work with the spectral embeddings of the vertices of
G
. More specifically, the algorithms which we build in this paper need to compute inner products between the spectral embeddings of the vertices of
G
. It suffices for our purposes to compute the inner products approximately. In
Definition

4.1
we define the requirement which a function should satisfy in order to be a useful approximation.

Definition 4.1

(Approximate spectral inner product function)
.

Let
\xi
be as per
Table

1
. We say that a function
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}:V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
is an
approximate inner product function
if for any
u,v\in V
we have

\left|\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}-\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle\right|\leq\frac{\xi}{n}.

We sometimes concisely refer to the inner product and approximate inner product of spectral embeddings as
spectral inner product
and
approximate spectral inner product
respectively.

Remark 4.2

(Notation
\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
)
.

Technically, the function
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
takes as input two vertices
u
and
v
and not their spectral embeddings. We will slightly abuse notation and write
\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
instead of
\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
to highlight that
\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
is an approximation of a spectral object
\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle
.

Remark 4.3

(Approximate spectral distance function)
.

We can use the the approximate inner product function as per
Definition

4.1
to approximately calculate the
\ell_{2}
-norms of spectral embeddings
f_{u}
as well as the distances
f_{u}-f_{v}
as follows:

\|f_{u}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}\coloneqq\langle f_{u},f_{u}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}\,,

\|f_{u}-f_{v}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}\coloneqq\|f_{u}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}+\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}-2\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}\,.

By
Definition

4.1
, one has

\left|\|f_{u}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|f_{u}\|^{2}_{2}\right|=\left|\langle f_{u},f_{u}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}-\langle f_{u},f_{u}\rangle\right|\leq\frac{\xi}{n}\,;

\left|\|f_{u}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|f_{u}\|_{2}\right|\leq\sqrt{\left|\|f_{u}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|f_{u}\|^{2}_{2}\right|}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}\,;

\left|\|f_{u}-f_{v}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|f_{u}-f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}\right|\leq\left|\|f_{u}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|f_{u}\|^{2}_{2}\right|+\left|\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}\right|+2\left|\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}-\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle\right|\leq\frac{4\xi}{n}\,;

\left|\|f_{u}-f_{v}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|f_{u}-f_{v}\|_{2}\right|\leq\sqrt{\left|\|f_{u}-f_{v}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|f_{u}-f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}\right|}\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}\,.

We will not fix a particular approximate inner product function in the definitions and results – we state them in terms of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
. We specify the choice of a function whenever we state an algorithm which computes approximate spectral inner products as a subroutine. We describe the set of approximate spectral inner product functions which we use in
Section

4.2
.

Apart from spectral inner products, our algorithm will also require access to the cluster means, defined in
Definition

3.4
. Note that, as our algorithm never explicitly computes spectral embeddings, finding the cluster means as exact vectors is both not feasible nor useful. Just as with spectral inner products, we can opt for approximations to the cluster means. In
Definition

4.4
we formalize which vectors are both sufficiently close to the cluster means and are accessible to our algorithms.

Definition 4.4

(Approximate cluster means)
.

Let
\phi
and
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}
as per
Table

1
,
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{apx}}
as per
Remark

4.3
. We say that the vectors
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\dots\widetilde{\mu}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
are
approximate cluster means
if

•

\|\mu_{i}-\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{1600\eta}\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}
for all
i\in[k]
;

•

there exists a set of vertices
\{u_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}\subseteq V
such that
\widetilde{\mu}_{i}=f_{u_{i}}
for all
i\in[k]
.

Spectral embeddings together with the approximate cluster means give rise to natural vertex classes: the vertices whose embedding is close to an approximate cluster mean (called spectral clusters), and the vertices whose embedding is far from every approximate cluster mean (called cross vertices).

Definition 4.5

(Spectral clusters and cross vertices)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. For
i\in[k]
, we define the
i
-th spectral cluster, denoted
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
, as

\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\coloneqq\left\{v\in V\colon\|f_{v}-\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}<\frac{\phi^{2}}{400\eta}\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}^{2}\right\}\,.

We also define the set of cross vertices, denoted
\mathcal{X}
, as

\mathcal{X}\coloneqq V\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i\in[k]}\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\right)\,.

If follows from Definitions
4.4
and
4.5
that vertices in a spectral cluster
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
are close to the true cluster mean
\mu_{i}
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. For all
i\in[k]
and all
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
, one has

\|f_{u}-\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{100\eta}\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\,.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\|f_{u}-\mu_{i}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|f_{u}-\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\|_{2}+\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{i}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|f_{u}-\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}+\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

by
Remark

4.3
and
Definition

4.4

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}+\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

by
Definition

4.5

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\|_{2}+\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}+\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

by
Remark

4.3

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}(\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{i}\|_{2})+\left(\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}+\frac{\phi}{100\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}

since
\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}\leq\frac{\phi}{400\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}
,
\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{\eta}}\leq 1

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}\left(1+\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\left(\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}+\frac{\phi}{100\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}

by Definition
4.4

\displaystyle\leq\frac{69}{800}\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2},

using the fact that
\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{\eta}}\leq 1
.

\displaystyle<\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}.

∎

It is clear from
Definition

4.5
that the cross vertices are disjoint from any spectral cluster, but one can in fact show that any two spectral clusters are also disjoint. This means that the spectral clusters and the cross vertices form a partitioning of the vertices. In particular, the spectral labeling
\tau
as per
Table

2
is well defined.

Lemma 4.7

(Spectral clusters are disjoint)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. For all
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
, one has

\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)=\emptyset.

Proof.

Suppose that there exists
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)
for some
i,j\in[k]
such that
i\neq j
. Then, by triangle inequality, we get

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}-\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}-\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\|_{2}+2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

by
Remark

4.3

\displaystyle\leq\|f_{u}-\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\|_{2}+\|f_{u}-\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\|_{2}+2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}(\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\|_{2}+\|\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\|_{2})+8\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

by Def.
4.5
and
Remark

4.3

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}\left(\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\widetilde{\mu}_{j}-\mu_{j}\|_{2}+\|\mu_{j}\|_{2}\right)+8\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\phi}{10}(\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\mu_{j}\|_{2})+8\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

\displaystyle\text{by Definition \ref{def:approxmeans}}\,.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}-\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}

\displaystyle\geq\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}-\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\|_{2}-2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

by
Remark

4.3

\displaystyle\geq\|\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}\|_{2}-\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{i}\|_{2}-\|\widetilde{\mu}_{j}-\mu_{j}\|_{2}-2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mu_{j}\|^{2}_{2}-2\langle\mu_{i},\mu_{j}\rangle}-\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}(\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\mu_{j}\|_{2})-2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

by Definition
4.4

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}+\|\mu_{j}\|^{2}_{2}-\frac{32\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\|\mu_{j}\|_{2}}-\frac{\phi}{10}(\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\mu_{j}\|_{2})-2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

by Lemma
3.6

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}(\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\mu_{j}\|_{2})-\frac{\phi}{10}(\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\mu_{j}\|_{2})-2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

using
\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{2}}\eta^{2}\leq 10^{-5}

\displaystyle>\phi\cdot(\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\mu_{j}\|_{2})-2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

using
\phi\leq 0.1
,

where
\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{2}}\eta^{2}\leq 10^{-5}
and
\phi\leq 0.1
are assumptions stated in
Table

1
. It remains to note that

\phi\cdot(\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\mu_{j}\|_{2})-2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}>\frac{\phi}{10}(\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\mu_{j}\|_{2})+8\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}\,,

because
{\phi}\leq 1/\sqrt{\eta}
and
\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}\leq\frac{\phi}{100\sqrt{\eta}}\min\{\|\mu_{i}\|_{2},\|\mu_{j}\|_{2}\}
, which leads to a contradiction.
∎

While we would like the spectral clusters to reflect the target communities
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
, not all vertices in a spectral cluster are in the corresponding community. It is then useful to sometimes restrict our attention to the vertices in a spectral cluster that are indeed in the corresponding community.

Definition 4.8

(Spectral cores)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. For
i\in[k]
, we define the
i
-th spectral core, denoted
\mathrm{SpecCore}(i)
, as

\mathrm{SpecCore}(i)\coloneqq\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap C_{i}\,.

We will also want to restrict our attention to vertices that spectrally look like they are from the
i
-th cluster but in fact belong to
C_{j}
. We call these vertices
impostors
, because they can easily fool a spectral algorithm. This is not the case for the cross vertices, since they do not pretend to be spectrally close to the wrong cluster, but rather their embedding is uninformative.

Definition 4.9

(Spectral impostors)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. For
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
, we define the
(j\to i)
-spectral impostors, denoted
\Gamma(i,j)
, as

\Gamma(i,j)\coloneqq\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap C_{j}\,.

We define the set of all spectral impostors, denoted
\Gamma
, as

\Gamma\coloneqq\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]:\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\Gamma(i,j)\,.

We will often refer to spectral impostors simply as
impostors
.

Remark 4.10

(Spectral impostors are disjoint)
.

For all
i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}\in[k]
with
(i_{1},j_{1})\neq(i_{2},j_{2})
, one has

\Gamma(i_{1},j_{1})\cap\Gamma(i_{2},j_{2})=\emptyset

since all of the spectral clusters are disjoint by
Lemma

4.7
and since all of the clusters
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
are disjoint.

The perturbed labeling, which the algorithm is given as input, also gives rise to natural vertex classes: we can partition the vertices according to their label
\sigma(u)
, and call these classes
label clusters
.

Definition 4.11

(Label clusters)
.

Let
\sigma
, as per
Table

2
. For
i\in[k]
, we define the
i
-th label cluster, denoted
\mathrm{LabelCluster}(i)
, as

\mathrm{LabelCluster}(i)\coloneqq\{u\in V:\sigma(u)=i\}\,.

Definition 4.12

(Mislabeled vertices)
.

Let
\sigma,\iota
as per
Table

2
. We define the set of all vertices mislabeled by
\sigma
, denoted
\mathrm{\Lambda}
, as

\mathrm{\Lambda}\coloneqq\left\{v\in V:\sigma(v)\neq\iota(v)\right\}

Notation
.

Let
\sigma,\tau
as per
Table

2
. For a vertex
u\in V
, we abuse notation and write
\mathrm{LabelCluster}(u)
and
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(u)
to refer to
\mathrm{LabelCluster}(\sigma(u))
and
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\tau(u))
respectively. For an index
i\in[k]
, we also write
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i)
to refer to
\cup_{j\in[k]\setminus\{i\}}\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)
.

As briefly mentioned before, our algorithm will combine spectral information with label information. It will in particular try to get a sense for which vertices are impostors by checking if vertices part of both the
i
-th spectral cluster and the
j
-th label cluster can reach the cross vertices. This gives rise to the following notion.

Definition 4.13

(Cross graphs)
.

Let
G
as per
Table

1
and
\sigma
as per
Table

2
. For
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
, we let
V_{i,j}\coloneqq(\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j))\cup\mathcal{X}
and define the
(i,j)
-cross graph to be the graph
G_{i,j}=(V,E_{i,j},\ell)
consisting of all the edges induced by
V_{i,j}
together with a number of self-loops to preserve regularity, i.e.

E_{i,j}\coloneqq\{(u,v)\in E:\,u,v\in V_{i,j}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\ell(u)\coloneqq d-|\{e\in E_{i,j}:\,u\in e\}|\text{ for all }u\in V\,.

Remark 4.14
.

Cross graphs are defined over the whole vertex set
V
because we will want to conveniently take unions of them. For our purposes, it does not matter if there are isolated vertices, since we will be interested in the reachability of vertices in
(\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j))\cup\mathcal{X}
.

We now proceed to prove properties of spectral clusters that will be crucial for our analysis.

4.1
Properties of spectral clusters

A key property of the spectral clusters is that any vertex that belongs to
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
and is not connected to
\mathcal{X}
can have at most a
\frac{\phi}{2}
fraction of its edges to other spectral clusters.

Lemma 4.15

(Few neighbors across spectral clusters)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. For every
i\in[k]
and every
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
one has

\left|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i)\right|<d\cdot\frac{\phi}{2}\,.

Proof.

The high-level idea is as follows: If
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
, then by
Lemma

4.6
,
f_{u}
is close to the cluster mean
\mu_{i}
. On the other hand, by Lemma
3.5
, we know that
f_{u}
is close to the average of its neighbours’ embeddings. In particular, if
u
has many neighbours in other spectral clusters, then
f_{u}
has a large component coming from other cluster means. But since the cluster means are almost orthogonal (by Lemma
3.6
), this would mean that
f_{u}
has a large component orthogonal to
\mu_{i}
, contradicting the fact that
f_{u}
is close to
\mu_{i}
. We now prove this more formally.

Suppose for contradiction that
|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i)|\geq d\cdot{\phi}/{2}
for some
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)
. By Lemma
3.5
, we have

\left\|f_{u}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in N_{G}(u)}f_{v}\right\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon\|f_{u}\|_{2}.

(4.1)

By the assumption that
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
(as per Definition
4.5
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|f_{u}-\mu_{i}\|_{2}

by
Lemma

4.6

\displaystyle=\left\|f_{u}-\left(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}f_{v}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}f_{v}\right)-\mu_{i}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\mu_{i}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}f_{v}\right\|_{2}-\left\|f_{u}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}f_{v}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\mu_{i}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}f_{v}\right\|_{2}-2\epsilon\|f_{u}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\text{by \eqref{eq:nbhrs}}.

Recall the definition of the spectral labeling
\tau
from
Table

2
:
\tau(v)=*
for a vertex
v\in\mathcal{X}
and otherwise
\tau(v)
returns the spectral cluster which
v
belongs to. Since
u\notin\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
, we have that
\tau(v)\neq*
for all
v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)
. In particular,
v\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\tau(v))
for all
v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)
. Hence, by
Lemma

4.6
, for all
v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)
, it holds that

\|f_{v}-\mu_{\tau(v)}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{10}\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{\tau(v)}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\phi}{5}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2},

(4.2)

where the last inequality follows from
Lemma

3.6

(1)
together with the assumption that
\eta\geq\frac{|C_{j}|}{|C_{l}|}
for all
j,l\in[k]
. Hence, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{10}\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\mu_{i}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}f_{v}\right\|_{2}-2\epsilon\|f_{u}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mu_{i}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\mu_{\tau(v)}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\left(f_{v}-\mu_{\tau(v)}\right)\right\|_{2}-2\epsilon\|f_{u}\|_{2}
