\displaystyle\geq\left\|\mu_{i}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\mu_{\tau(v)}\right\|_{2}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\|f_{v}-\mu_{\tau(v)}\|_{2}-2\epsilon\|f_{u}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\mu_{i}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\mu_{\tau(v)}\right\|_{2}-\frac{\phi}{5}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}-4\epsilon\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{by \eqref{eq:4.16} and \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:spec_exact_mu}}.

Now let
l\coloneqq|\{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u):\tau(v)\neq i\}|
be the number of neighbors that belong to a different spectral cluster. By assumption, it holds that
l\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d
. We have

\mu_{i}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\mu_{\tau(v)}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{\tau(v)})=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u):\tau(v)\neq i}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{\tau(v)})=\frac{l}{d}\mu_{i}-\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u):\tau(v)\neq i}\mu_{\tau(v)}\,,

which gives

\displaystyle\left\|\mu_{i}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\mu_{\tau(v)}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{l}{d}\mu_{i}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u):\tau(v)\neq i}\mu_{\tau(v)}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{l}{d}\mu_{i}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{d^{2}}\left\|\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u):\tau(v)\neq i}\mu_{\tau(v)}\right\|_{2}^{2}-2\frac{l}{d}\left|\left\langle\mu_{i},\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u):\tau(v)\neq i}\mu_{\tau(v)}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{l^{2}}{d^{2}}\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}-2\frac{l^{2}}{d^{2}}\frac{16\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi^{2}}\sqrt{\eta}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\qquad\text{by Lemma \ref{lemma:clustermeans} and using $\frac{l}{d}\geq\frac{\phi}{2}$}

\displaystyle=\frac{l^{2}}{d^{2}}\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\left(1-\frac{32\sqrt{\epsilon}\sqrt{\eta}}{\phi^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{l}{d}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\left(1-\frac{32\sqrt{\epsilon}\sqrt{\eta}}{\phi^{2}}\right)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{\phi}{3}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\right)^{2}\qquad\text{by assumption that $l\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d$, and $\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{6}}\leq\frac{10^{-5}}{\eta^{4}}$ as per \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:setting}. }

Hence, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{10}\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\mu_{i}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\mu_{\tau(v)}\right\|_{2}-\frac{\phi}{5}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}-4\epsilon\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi}{3}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}-\frac{\phi}{5}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}-4\epsilon\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2}{15}\phi-4\epsilon\right)\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}

\displaystyle>\frac{\phi}{10}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{by assumption $\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{2}}\leq 10^{-5}$},

which is a contradiction.
∎

The impostors together with the cross vertices are all the vertices for which the spectral embeddings assign a wrong cluster, in the sense that it does not give the correct classification. By known results, one can show that they account for at most an
\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\phi)
fraction of vertices.

Lemma 4.16

(
\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X}
is small)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. Then,

|\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X}|\leq 2\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n\,.

Proof.

The main idea is to combine
Lemma

3.9
together with the observation that for all
u\in\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X}
, the distance
\|f_{u}-\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}
is large. We first show that for all
u\in\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X},
it holds that

\|f_{u}-\widetilde{\mu}_{\iota(u)}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}^{2}\geq\frac{\phi^{2}}{400\eta}\|\widetilde{\mu}_{\iota(u)}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}.

(4.3)

Indeed, if
u\in\mathcal{X}
is a cross vertex, then (
4.3
) holds by definition of
\mathcal{X}
(Definition
4.5
).
If instead
u\in\Gamma
is an impostor, then
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap C_{j}
for some
j\neq i
(by Definition
4.9
). In particular, since the spectral clusters are disjoint (by Lemma
4.7
), we have
u\notin\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)
, and so

\|f_{u}-\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}^{2}\geq{\phi^{2}}/({400\eta})\cdot\|\widetilde{\mu}_{j}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}
(by Definition
4.5
), which gives (
4.3
) since
\iota(u)=j
. Thus, we get

\displaystyle\|f_{u}-\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|f_{u}-\widetilde{\mu}_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}-\|\mu_{\iota(u)}-\widetilde{\mu}_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|f_{u}-\widetilde{\mu}_{\iota(u)}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}-2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}-\|\mu_{\iota(u)}-\widetilde{\mu}_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}

by
Remark

4.3

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}\|\widetilde{\mu}_{\iota(u)}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}-\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}-2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

by (
4.3
) and Def.
4.4

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}\|\widetilde{\mu}_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}-\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}-2\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}-\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{n}}

by
Remark

4.3

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}\left(\|\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}-\|\mu_{\iota(u)}-\widetilde{\mu}_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}\right)-\left(\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}+\frac{\phi}{100\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\|\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}

by
Table

1

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi}{20\sqrt{\eta}}\left(1-\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\|\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}-\left(\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}+\frac{\phi}{100\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\|\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}

by Definition
4.4

\displaystyle\geq\frac{119}{8000}\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2},

using
\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{\eta}}\leq 0.1
.

so

\|f_{u}-\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{14161}{64\cdot 10^{6}}\frac{\phi^{2}}{\eta}\|\mu_{\iota(u)}\|^{2}_{2}.

(4.4)

Summing (
4.4
) over all
u\in\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X}
, we get

\sum_{u\in\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X}}\|f_{u}-\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{14161}{64\cdot 10^{6}}\frac{\phi^{2}}{\eta}\sum_{u\in\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X}}\|\mu_{\iota(u)}\|^{2}_{2}\geq|\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X}|\cdot\frac{\phi^{2}}{5000\eta^{2}}\cdot\frac{k}{n}.

(4.5)

Here, the last inequality uses that
\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\geq(1-4/10^{5})\frac{k}{n}\frac{1}{\eta}
for all
i
, by
Remark

3.7
.
On the other hand, by
Lemma

3.9
, we have

\sum_{u\in\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X}}\|f_{u}-\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\sum_{u\in V}\|f_{u}-\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{4\epsilon k}{\phi^{2}}.

(4.6)

Combining Equations (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) gives the result.
∎

An important property of impostors is that they essentially induce an expander. This will help the algorithm detect the impostors, hence averting being fooled by their spectral embedding.

Lemma 4.17

(Impostors expand)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
.
For every
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
and every
S\subseteq\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
, it holds that

|E(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)|\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d|S|\,.

Proof.

Note that
|S|\leq|\Gamma|\leq 2\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n\leq\frac{|C_{j}|}{2}
, where the second inequality follows by
Lemma

4.16
, and the last inequality follows by the setting of
\epsilon,\phi
as per
Table

1
. Since
S\subseteq C_{j}
, and since
G\{C_{j}\}
is a
\phi
-expander, we have

|E(S,C_{j}\setminus S)|\geq\phi\cdot d\cdot|S|.

(4.7)

We will now show that a large fraction of the edges in
E(S,C_{j}\setminus S)
go to the
(j\to i)
-impostors
\Gamma(i,j)
.
We have

\displaystyle C_{j}

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i)\cup\Gamma(i,j)\,.

Thus,

\displaystyle|E(S,C_{j}\setminus S)|

\displaystyle\leq\left|E(S,\mathcal{X})\right|+\left|E(S,\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i))\right|+|E(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)|

(4.8)

\displaystyle=\left|E(S,\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i))\right|+|E(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)|\qquad\text{by the assumption $S\cap\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})=\emptyset$}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{u\in S}\left|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i))\right|+|E(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)|

\displaystyle\leq|S|\cdot d\cdot\frac{\phi}{2}+|E(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)|\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:cores_sparsely_connected}.}

Here the last inequality uses the assumption that
S\subseteq\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})\subseteq\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
, (since
\Gamma(i,j)\subseteq\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
by Definitions
4.5
and
4.9
), so every vertex
u\in S
satisfies the assumption in
Lemma

4.15
. Combining Equations (
4.7
) and (
4.8
), and rearranging gives the result.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

Lemma

4.17
and
Lemma

4.15
only hold outside of

\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
. Since
|\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})|\approx\epsilon dn
, this is one of the reasons why our algorithm in Section
5
(Algorithm
2
) will incur a
d
-dependence in the misclassification rate. Later, in Section
6
, we improve this by defining a subset
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\subseteq\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
of size
|\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|\approx\epsilon n
(see Definition
6.6
), and proving
that
Lemma

4.17
and
Lemma

4.15
can also be extended to
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
(See
Lemma

6.13
and
Lemma

6.8
, respectively).

4.2
Approximate spectral inner product oracles and approximate cluster means

In this section, we present two algorithms for approximating spectral inner products: a simpler polynomial time algorithm and a more sophisticated sublinear time and space algorithm. As it is essential for the polynomial and sublinear time classifiers to have access to approximate cluster means, we also present a subroutine for finding approximate cluster means in
Lemma

4.24
, which may be used by both. We present the analysis of
Lemma

4.24
in
Appendix

A
.

We use the polynomial time algorithm in
Remark

4.20
as a subroutine in the polynomial time classifier, introduced in
Section

5
.

Remark 4.19

(Polynomial time algorithm for approximating
U_{[k]}U^{T}_{[k]}
)
.

There exists an
O(n^{2}\cdot k^{2}\cdot\frac{\log n}{\phi^{2}})
time algorithm which recovers a matrix
\widehat{Q}
such that

\|\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{T}-U_{[k]}U^{T}_{[k]}\|_{2}\leq 1/n^{100}.

We prove the above remark in
Appendix

A
.

Remark 4.20

(Euclidean inner product oracle)
.

From
Definition

3.1
, we have
f_{u}=U^{T}_{[k]}\mathbbm{1}_{u}
. From
Remark

4.19
, there exists a polynomial time algorithm which recovers a matrix
\widehat{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
such that
\|U_{[k]}U^{T}_{[k]}-\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{T}\|_{2}\leq 1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
. From there, we have

\left|\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle-\mathbbm{1}_{u}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{v}\right|=\left|\mathbbm{1}^{T}_{u}U_{[k]}U^{T}_{[k]}\mathbbm{1}_{v}-\mathbbm{1}_{u}\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{T}\mathbbm{1}_{v}\right|\leq\|U_{[k]}U^{T}_{[k]}-\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{T}\|_{2}\leq 1/\mathrm{poly}(n),

so we can in polynomial time approximate the inner products up to precision
1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
.

In
Section

6
, we use a sublinear time and space algorithm, introduced in
[
13
]
. We refer to this algorithm as Spectral Dot Oracle and we provide its performance guarantees below.

Theorem 4.21

(Spectral Dot Product Oracle from
[
13
]
)
.

Let
\epsilon,\phi\in(0,1)
with
\epsilon\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{10^{5}}
. Let
G=(V,E)
be a
d
-regular graph that admits a
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering
C_{1},\ldots,C_{k}
. Let
1>\xi>1/n^{5}
. There exists a preprocessing algorithm which computes in time
O\left(2^{O\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{\epsilon}k^{4}\log(k)\right)}\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(n))\cdot\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\xi)\right)
a sublinear space data structure

\mathcal{D}
of size
O\left(\mathrm{poly}(k)\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(n))\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\xi)\right)
such that with probability at least
1-n^{97}
the following
property is satisfied:

There exists an algorithm, referred to as
Spectral Dot Product Oracle
which has access to
\mathcal{D}
and for every pair of vertices
u,v\in V
computes an output value
\textsc{DP}(u,v)

such that

\left|\textsc{DP}(u,v)-\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle\right|\leq\frac{\xi}{n}.

The running time of Spectral Dot Product Oracle for every query
u,v\in V
is

O\left(\mathrm{poly}(k)\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(n))\cdot\frac{1}{\phi^{2}}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\xi)\right).

Remark 4.22
.

We note that
4.21
is stated slightly differently in
[
13
]
. There, both the preprocessing algorithm and Spectral Dot Product Oracle are probabilistic algorithms; the probability with which the preproccessing algorithm is successful is
1-n^{100}
and the probability with which the Spectral Dot Product Oracle is successful, conditioned on the success of the preprocessing algorithm, is also
1-n^{100}
for any
u,v\in V
. We note, however, that the preproccessing algorithm and Spectral Dot Product Oracle use different random bits, and together they require
O\left(\mathrm{poly}(k)\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(n))\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\phi)\right)
many random bits in total to process all pairs of vertices in
V
. Therefore, without loss of generality, we may assume that all of these random bits are sampled in the preproccesing stage. The preprocessing stage is then successful if it both returns a valid data structure
\mathcal{D}
and if the Spectral Dot Product Oracle returns valid approximations to the spectral inner products for all pairs of vertices in
V
which, by the union bound arguments, has probability at least

1-n^{100}-n^{2}\cdot n^{100}>1-n^{97}.

Remark 4.23
.

We fix
\xi=\frac{\phi^{2}}{(20)^{4}\eta}\min_{i}\{\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\}n\geq\Omega(\phi^{2}k)
so that
DP
is a valid approximate inner product function, as per
Definition

4.1
. The space complexity of the data structure
\mathcal{D}
is then bounded by

O\left(\mathrm{poly}(k)\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(n))\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\phi)\right),

and one call to the Spectral Dot Product Oracle takes time

O\left(\mathrm{poly}(k)\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(n))\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\phi)\right).

Finally, we present
Lemma

4.24
, which shows that we can compute the approximate clusters means.

Lemma 4.24
.

There exists an algorithm that returns
k
vertices
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}
(
Algorithm

7
) and an algorithm that returns a permutation
\pi:[k]\rightarrow[k]
(
Algorithm

8
) such that with probability 0.97 over the internal randomness of
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
the following holds:

\Pr_{\sigma}\left[\forall i\in[k]:\|f_{u_{i}}-\mu_{\pi(i)}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{1600\eta}\|\mu_{\pi(i)}\|^{2}_{2}\right]\geq 1-10^{-3}.

Our algorithm for computing the cluster means (
Algorithm

7
) is similar to Algorithm 1 of
[
34
]
. We present the algorithm and its analysis in Appendix
A
. We also present the algorithm for finding the right permutation (
Algorithm

8
) and its analysis, and prove
Lemma

4.24
, in Appendix
A
. As we see later, the algorithm for computing the cluster means depends on the choice of the approximate spectral inner product oracle and, if
Q
is the time required to make one call to the chosen oracle, then the runtime of the algorithm is
O(k^{2}\cdot\log^{2}(k)\cdot Q)
.

5
Warm-up: polynomial-time classifier with suboptimal rate

In this section, we outline and analyze
Algorithm

2
, a polynomial-time algorithm with misclassification rate
\approx d\epsilon\delta
. The algorithm takes as input the graph, the approximate cluster means, and the perturbed label. It outputs a cluster id for each vertex. We will show that the number of vertices such that the output id is different from
\iota(u)
is at most a
O({d\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{5}})
-fraction.

In the previous sections, we defined several objects, such as the cross graphs
G_{i,j}
, the spectral clusters
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
, and the spectral labeling
\tau
, which we would like to use in our algorithm. The definitions of those objects depend on the choice of an approximate inner product function. In line (
3
) we fix the choice of an approximate inner product function as the Euclidean inner product, introduced in
Section

4.2
.

We now describe the main idea of
Algorithm

2
. In the previous sections, we showed that the cluster means are roughly orthogonal (see
Lemma

3.6
) and that the spectral embeddings of all except for
\approx\epsilon n
vertices are located very close to their cluster means (see
Lemma

4.16
). If we knew that for each of those
\approx\epsilon n
vertices the spectral embedding is separated from each of the cluster means (recall that we refer to this set as
\mathcal{X}
,
Definition

4.5
), then (given access to the cluster means), the following simple algorithm would suffice: (1) for a vertex
u
, verify if
f_{u}
is close to some cluster mean; (2) if yes, return the number of this cluster mean; (3) if no, return
\sigma(u)
.
Indeed, in that case only vertices which are far from any cluster mean could potentially be misclassified, which, by Markov inequality, would yield that the total number of vertices misclassified by the above algorithm is
\approx\epsilon\delta n
.
However, we are not able to establish that a vertex is either in
\mathcal{X}
or it is spectrally close to its own cluster mean. There could potentially be
\approx\epsilon n
many vertices that actually belong to some cluster
C_{j}
but whose spectral embeddings are located close to the cluster mean
\mu_{i}
for
i\neq j
(recall that we refer to such vertices as
(j\to i)
-impostors
Definition

4.9
). If we settled for the above algorithm, we could misclassify up to
\approx\epsilon n
many vertices.

Instead, in
Lemma

4.17
we show that for any pair
i\neq j\in[k]
the vertices in
\Gamma(i,j)
are well-connected to
\mathcal{X}
and induce an expander-like subgraph. We argue that, due to the expander-like properties of
\Gamma(i,j)
, even if we remove all of the vertices misclassified by
\sigma
from
\Gamma(i,j)
, the remaining set of vertices is still well-connected to
\mathcal{X}
. This allows us to modify the above tentative algorithm to detect not only vertices from
\mathcal{X}
, but also vertices from
\Gamma
. This idea results in
Algorithm

2
, which operates as follows: for a vertex
u
, if
f_{u}
is close to some cluster mean and its predicted cluster
\sigma(u)
agrees with the spectral information, then return
\sigma(u)
(line (
4
)); if
f_{u}
is far from all cluster means, then return
\sigma(u)
(line (
5
)); if
f_{u}
is close to some cluster mean but its predicted cluster
\sigma(u)
is different from the one suggested by the spectral information (that is, if a vertex looks like it is in
\Gamma
), then check if
u
can reach
\mathcal{X}
(see
Definition

5.1
) through only vertices in the same predicted cluster and whose embeddings are close to the same cluster mean (line (
8
)). In other words, the last step checks reachability in the
(\tau(u),\sigma(u))
-cross graph (see
Definition

4.13
).

Definition 5.1

(Reachability)
.

For a graph
H=(V(H),E(H))
we say that a subset
S\subseteq V(H)
is reachable from a vertex
u\in V(H)
if the connected component of
u
contains at least one vertex from
S
. We write
u\sim_{H}S
if
S
is reachable from
u
in the graph
H
and
u\not{\sim}_{H}S
otherwise.

Algorithm 2

Polynomial-time classifier

1:

Input:

G,\phi,\eta,(\widetilde{\mu}_{i})_{i\in[k]}
as per
Table

1
,
\sigma
as per
Table

2
, and a vertex
u\in V

2:

Output:
a cluster id in
[k]

3:

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\text{apx}}\leftarrow\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle

\triangleright
Euclidean inner products, see
Remark

4.20

4:

if

\tau(u)=\sigma(u)

then

return

\sigma(u)

\triangleright
label agrees with spectral information

5:

else

if

\tau(u)=*

then

return

\sigma(u)

\triangleright
spectral information is ambiguous

6:

else
\triangleright
label disagrees with spectral information

7:

H\leftarrow G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}

\triangleright
see
Definition

4.13

8:

if

u\sim_{H}\mathcal{X}

then

\triangleright
see
Definition

5.1

9:

return

\sigma(u)

\triangleright
trust the label,
u
is likely a spectral impostor

10:

else

11:

return

\tau(u)

\triangleright
trust spectral information, the label is likely wrong

12:

end

if

13:

end

if

For
Algorithm

2
to run, we need to feed it
(\widetilde{\mu}_{i})_{i\in[k]}
. In
Appendix

A
, we describe and analyze
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
, and show that with good probability they produce valid approximate cluster means
(\widetilde{\mu}_{i})_{i\in[k]}
. Together, the three algorithms give the following result.

Theorem 5.2

(Polynomial time classifier)
.

There is a polynomial time algorithm that given as input
G,\phi,k,\eta
as per
Table

1
and
\sigma
as per
Table

2
outputs a labeling
\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]
with misclassification rate
O({d\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{5}})
with probability
0.9
over the internal randomness of the algorithm and the draw of
\sigma
.

To prove the theorem, we analyze the number of vertices misclassified by
Algorithm

2
. We do so by bounding the number of vertices misclassified in each return point of
Algorithm

2
, namely lines (
4
), (
5
), (
9
), (
11
). We begin by bounding the expected number of vertices that can be misclassified in line (
4
) or (
5
).

Lemma 5.3

(Misclassification in lines (
4
) and (
5
))
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
, and let
\iota,\sigma,\tau
as per
Table

2
. The expected fraction of vertices
u\in V
such that
\tau(u)
is either
\sigma(u)
or
*
and
\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
, is at most
O({\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{4}})
, i.e.

\E_{\sigma}\left[\left|\{u\in V:\,\tau(u)\in\{\sigma(u),*\}\text{and }\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)\}\right|\right]\leq 2\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon\delta}{\phi^{4}}n\,.

Proof.

For any
\sigma
, the set of vertices
u\in V
such that
\tau(u)\in\{\sigma(u),*\}\text{ and }\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
is necessarily contained in
\{v\in\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X}:\,\sigma(v)\neq\iota(v)\}
. By linearity of expectation and
Lemma

4.16
, we get the claim.
∎

Next, we bound the expected number of vertices that can be misclassified in line (
9
), which can be thought of as the “false positives” of the connectivity test in line (
8
). Proving this bound relies on the randomness of
\sigma
to relate the percolation of the connectivity test in line (
8
) to a Galton-Watson type of process.

Lemma 5.4

(Misclassification in line (
9
))
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
, and let
\iota,\sigma,\tau
as per
Table

2
. The expected fraction of vertices
u\in V
such that
\sigma(u)
is wrong and
u
is reachable from
\mathcal{X}
in the
(\tau(u),\sigma(u))
-cross graph is at most
O({d\epsilon\delta}/{\phi^{4}})
, i.e.

\E_{\sigma}\left[\left|\{u\in V:\,\sigma(u)\notin\{\iota(u),\tau(u)\},\tau(u)\neq*\text{ and }u\sim_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\}\right|\right]\leq 6\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{d\epsilon\delta}{\phi^{4}}n\,.

Proof.

The proof idea can be summarized as follows: (1) consider a breadth-first search starting from the set of cross and impostor vertices
\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma
that only traverses an edge if the other endpoint is mislabeled; (2) observe that any mislabeled vertex that can reach
\mathcal{X}
in its cross graph is visited by this breadth-first search at distance
1
or higher; (3) think of the breadth-first search as a Galton-Watson process whose offspring distribution has mean
\delta d<1
, and conclude that we only visit
O(\delta d)|\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma|
vertices on top of the starting ones. To make this formal,
let
S_{1}
be the set of neighbors of cross and impostor vertices, i.e.

S_{1}=\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma)\,.

For a fixed labeling
\alpha
(a possible realization of the random labeling
\sigma
), we define the following objects.
For all
i\in[n]
we let
S_{i+1}(\alpha)
be the set of neighbors in
G
of the mislabeled vertices in
S_{i}(\alpha)
that are not in any previously defined sets, i.e.

S_{i+1}(\alpha)=\mathrm{N}_{G}\left(\{u\in S_{i}(\alpha):\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}\right)\setminus\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}S_{j}(\alpha)\right)\,.

Note that the definition of the set
S_{1}
does not depend on the labeling
\alpha
but, for the sake of uniformity of notation, we will still write
S_{1}(\alpha)
. For convenience, we let
\widehat{S}_{i}(\alpha)
be the set of vertices in
S_{i}(\alpha)
with a wrong label, i.e.

\widehat{S}_{i}(\alpha)=\{u\in S_{i}(\alpha):\,\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}\,,

and let
{S}_{\leq i}(\alpha)
be the set of vertices in any of
S_{1}(\alpha),\dots,S_{i}(\alpha)
, i.e.

S_{\leq i}(\alpha)=\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}S_{j}(\alpha)\,,

so we can rewrite

S_{i+1}(\alpha)=\mathrm{N}_{G}(\widehat{S}_{i}(\alpha))\setminus S_{\leq i}(\alpha)\,.

Similarly, we also let
\widehat{S}_{\leq i}(\alpha)
be the set of vertices in any of
\widehat{S}_{1}(\alpha),\dots,\widehat{S}_{i}(\alpha)
, i.e.

\widehat{S}_{\leq i}(\alpha)=\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}\widehat{S}_{j}(\alpha)\,,

Next, we relate these level sets to the quantity we want to ultimately bound.

Claim 5.5
.

For any fixed
\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]
, one has

\{u\in V:\,\alpha(u)\notin\{\iota(u),\tau(u)\},\tau(u)\neq*\text{ and }u\sim_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\}\subseteq\widehat{S}_{\leq n}(\alpha)\cup\{u\in\mathcal{X}:\,\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}\,.

Proof.

We let
G^{\prime}=(V,E^{\prime})
be the subgraph of
G
obtained by the union of all the
(i,j)
-cross graphs, i.e. we define

E^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i,j\in[k]:\,i\neq j}E_{ij}\,.

Clearly, for any vertex
u\in V
such that
\alpha(u)\notin\{\iota(u),\tau(u)\},\tau(u)\neq*\text{ and }u\sim_{G_{\tau(u),\alpha(u)}}\mathcal{X}
one has that
\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\text{ and }u\sim_{G^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}
. Therefore, we prove

\{u\in V:\,\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\text{ and }u\sim_{G^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}\}\subseteq\widehat{S}_{\leq n}(\alpha)\cup\{u\in\mathcal{X}:\,\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}\,.

(5.1)

For any
u\in V
such that
\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)
and
u\sim_{G^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}
, there exists a simple path
w_{0},\dots,w_{t}
for
0\leq t\leq n-1
where
w_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
,
w_{t}=u
, and
(w_{r-1},w_{r})\in E^{\prime}
for all
r\in[t]
. Then, for every
t\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
, we define the set
Z_{t}
to be the set of vertices
u\in V
with
\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)
such that there exists a simple path
w_{0},\dots,w_{t}
where
w_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
,
w_{t}=u
, and
(w_{r-1},w_{r})\in E^{\prime}
for all
r\in[t]
. Also, we define
\widehat{S}_{\leq 0}(\alpha)\coloneqq\emptyset
. We prove (
5.1
) by showing by induction that for all
t\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
one has

Z_{t}\subseteq\widehat{S}_{\leq t}(\alpha)\cup\{u\in\mathcal{X}:\,\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}\,.

For the base case, let
u\in V
be any vertex with
\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)
which is also connected to
\mathcal{X}
in
G^{\prime}
by a path of length
0
. Trivially, we have
u\in\{v\in\mathcal{X}:\,\alpha(v)\neq\iota(v)\}
. For the inductive step, let
0\leq t\leq n-2
and assume that
Z_{t}\subseteq\widehat{S}_{\leq t}(\alpha)\cup\{u\in\mathcal{X}:\,\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}
. Let
w\in Z_{t+1}\setminus Z_{t}
be a vertex with
\alpha(w)\neq\iota(w)
that is connected to
\mathcal{X}
in
G^{\prime}
by a path of length
t+1
. Then, there is a vertex
u\in V
connected to
\mathcal{X}
in
G^{\prime}
by a path of length at most
t
such that
w
is a neighbor of
u
in
G^{\prime}
. In particular,
w
is a neighbor of
u
in
G
. We then consider two cases.

•

If
\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)
, then
u\in Z_{t}
and hence
u\in\widehat{S}_{\leq t}(\alpha)\cup\{v\in\mathcal{X}:\,\alpha(v)\neq\iota(v)\}
by the inductive hypothesis. Thus,
w\in S_{\leq t+1}(\alpha)
. Since
\alpha(w)\neq i(w)
by assumption, we have that
w\in\widehat{S}_{\leq t+1}(\alpha)
.

•

Suppose
\alpha(u)=\iota(u)=j
for some
j\in[k]
. Note that since
u
is not isolated in
G^{\prime}
,
u\notin\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)
. Hence, either
u\in\mathcal{X}
or there exists
i\in[k]
such that
i\neq j
and
u\in\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
. Hence,
u\in\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X}
, which implies that
w\in S_{1}(\alpha)
. Recalling that
\alpha(w)\neq i(w)
, we conclude
w\in\widehat{S}_{1}(\alpha)
.

∎

By virtue of the above claim, it suffices to bound
|\widehat{S}_{\leq n}(\alpha)\cup\{v\in\mathcal{X}:\,\alpha(v)\neq\iota(v)\}|
. By linearity of expectation, the expected number of vertices in
\{u\in\mathcal{X}:\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}
is easily upper-bounded by
\delta|\mathcal{X}|
. Then, we only need to bound
|\widehat{S}_{\leq n}(\alpha)|
. To do so, we observe that for each
i\in[n]
, the expected size of
\widehat{S}_{i}(\alpha)
drops by a factor of
\delta d
at every level.

Claim 5.6
.

For all
i=2,\dots,n-1
, one has
\E_{\sigma}[|\widehat{S}_{i+1}(\sigma)|]\leq\delta d\cdot\E_{\sigma}[|\widehat{S}_{i}(\sigma)|]
.

We defer the proof of
5.6
to
Section

C.1
, but the idea is simple:
\widehat{S}_{i+1}
essentially consists of the mislabeled neighbors of
\widehat{S}_{i}
, and since the graph is
d
-regular and each neighbor is mislabeled with probability
\delta
, we get a factor
\delta d
drop. Then,
by virtue of
5.6
and by definition of
S_{1}
, we have

\E_{\sigma}\left[|\widehat{S}_{i}(\sigma)|\right]\leq(\delta d)^{i}\cdot|\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma|

for every
i\in[n]
. Then, by linearity of expectation, we get

\E_{\sigma}\left[|\widehat{S}_{\leq n}(\sigma)|\right]\leq|\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\delta d)^{i}\leq 2\delta d|\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma|\,.

By
Lemma

4.16
, we conclude the proof.
∎

Finally, we bound the number of vertices that can be misclassified in line (
11
). These vertices can be thought of as the “false negatives” of the connectivity test in line (
8
). To show this last bound, we rely on the randomness of
\sigma
in order to apply Markov’s inequality to the number of mislabeled impostors.

Lemma 5.7

(Misclassification in line (
11
))
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
, and let
\iota,\sigma,\tau
as per
Table

2
. The expected fraction of vertices over the draw of
\sigma
such that
\tau(u)\in[k]
is wrong but
\sigma(u)
is correct and
u
is not reachable from
\mathcal{X}
in the
(\tau(u),\sigma(u))
-cross graph is at most
O(\epsilon\delta/\phi^{5})
, i.e.

\E_{\sigma}\left[\left|\{u\in V:\,\tau(u)\notin\{*,\sigma(u)\}\text{ and }\sigma(u)=\iota(u)\text{ and }u\not{\sim}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\}\right|\right]\leq 4\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon\delta}{\phi^{5}}n\,.

In other words, at most
4\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot{\epsilon\delta}/{\phi^{5}}\cdot n
correctly labeled impostor vertices
u
are not connected to the set
\mathcal{X}
in the cross graph
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
, in expectation over
\sigma
.

Proof.

The main idea of this proof is as follows. Recall that in
Lemma

4.17
we showed that for all
i\neq j
the set
\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
has certain expander properties: every subset
S
of
\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
has many edges going to
\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S
. From here, we get that all vertices in
\Gamma(i,j)
are connected to
\mathcal{X}
in the graph induced by
\Gamma(i,j)\cup\mathcal{X}
. Moreover, as we show in
5.8
, removing an arbitrary set of vertices
R
from
\Gamma(i,j)
prevents only a few vertices in
\Gamma(i,j)\setminus R
from reaching
\mathcal{X}
in the graph induced by
(\Gamma(i,j)\setminus R)\cup\mathcal{X}
. If we choose
R\coloneqq\Gamma(i,j)\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}
(recall from
Definition

4.12
that
\Lambda
denotes the set of misclassified vertices), then the above implies the following: all the correctly labeled vertices in
\Gamma(i,j)
, except for a small subset, are connected to the set
\mathcal{X}
only through correctly labeled vertices in
\Gamma(i,j)
. This is even stronger than the statement of
Lemma

5.7
, as for every correctly labeled
u\in\Gamma(i,j)
we have that the cross graph
G_{i,j}
contains both correctly labeled vertices from
\Gamma(i,j)
and some mislabeled vertices from
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
.

We now formalize the above argument.
First, we introduce notation for the induced graphs which we discussed above. Let
E^{*}_{i,j}=\{(u,v)\in E:\,u,v\in\Gamma(i,j)\cup\mathcal{X}\}
and let
G^{*}_{i,j}=(\Gamma(i,j)\cup\mathcal{X},E^{*}_{i,j})
be the subgraph of
G
induced by
\Gamma(i,j)\cup\mathcal{X}
. Additionally, for a set of vertices
R\subseteq V
, we denote by
E^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R
the subset of edges of
E^{*}_{i,j}
except all of the edges incident on
R
(i.e.
E^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R=E^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\{(u,v)\in E:u\in R\text{ or }v\in R\}
), and define
G^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R=((\Gamma(i,j)\cup\mathcal{X})\setminus R,E^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R)
– the subgraph of
G
induced by
(\Gamma(i,j)\cup\mathcal{X})\setminus R
. With this notation, we first argue that removing a few vertices from
G^{*}_{i,j}
only causes a few more to become disconnected from
\mathcal{X}
.

Claim 5.8
.

Let
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
and let
R\subseteq\Gamma(i,j)
. Then, number of vertices in
\Gamma(i,j)
that are not reachable from
\mathcal{X}
in
G^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R
is at most
{2|R|}/{\phi}
.

Proof.

Let
S\subseteq\Gamma(i,j)\setminus R
denote the set of vertices that are isolated from
\mathcal{X}
in
G^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R
, i.e.

S=\left\{u\in\Gamma(i,j)\setminus R:\,u\not{\sim}_{G^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R}\mathcal{X}\right\}\,.

First, we argue that
S\cap\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})=\emptyset
. Indeed, every vertex in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})\setminus R
has a neighbor in
\mathcal{X}
. But from the definition of
S
, no vertex in
S
can have a neighbor in
\mathcal{X}
. Therefore,
S\cap\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})=\emptyset
. Hence, we have
S\subseteq\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
, which allows us to apply
Lemma

4.17
and get

|E(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)|\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d|S|\,.

Note that
\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S
consists of two types of vertices: the vertices reachable from
\mathcal{X}
in
G^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R
, and the vertices in
R
. Observe that
S
cannot have any edges to the vertices reachable from
\mathcal{X}
in
G^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R
, or else there would be at least one vertex in
S
which is reachable from
\mathcal{X}
which contradicts the definition of
S
. So
E(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)\subseteq E(S,R).
Therefore, we have

\frac{\phi}{2}|S|d\leq|E(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)|\leq|E(S,R)|\leq|R|d\,,

which rearranges to
|S|\leq{2|R|}/{\phi}.

∎

We define the set
R_{i,j}=\Gamma(i,j)\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}
of mislabeled
(j\to i)
-impostors for each
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
. Just as discussed in the proof sketch, we would like to apply
5.8
with
R\coloneqq R_{i,j}
to each of the graphs
G^{*}_{i,j}
. We do this in
5.9
and derive from that the statement of
Lemma

5.7
.

Claim 5.9
.

Suppose
u\in V
is such that
\tau(u)\notin\{*,\sigma(u)\}
,
\sigma(u)=\iota(u)
, and
u\not{\sim}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}
. For
i=\tau(u)
and
j=\iota(u)
we have that
u\not{\sim}_{G^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R_{i,j}}\mathcal{X}
and
u\in\Gamma(i,j)
.

Proof.

By the statement of the claim,
u
is a correctly labeled impostor, and so, by the choice of
i
and
j
,
u\in\Gamma(i,j)
. Since
G^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R_{i,j}
is the subgraph of
G
induced by
(\Gamma(i,j)\cup\mathcal{X})\setminus R_{i,j}
we have that
E_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}\supseteq E^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R_{i,j}
, as
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
contains
G^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R_{i,j}
as a subgraph. Hence, the claim follows.
∎

By virtue of
5.9
and
5.8
, for any fixed outcome of
\sigma
we have

\displaystyle\left|\{u\in V:\,\tau(u)\notin\{*,\sigma(u)\},\,\sigma(u)=\iota(u)\,,u\not{\sim}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]:\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\left|\left\{u\in\Gamma(i,j):u\not{\sim}_{G^{*}_{i,j}\setminus R_{i,j}}\mathcal{X}\right\}\right|

by
5.9

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]:\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\frac{2}{\phi}|R_{i,j}|

by
5.8

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\phi}|\Gamma\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|\,.

Finally, taking the expectation over the random labeling
\sigma
, we get

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma}\left[\left|\{u\in V:\,\tau(u)\notin\{*,\sigma(u)\},\,\sigma(u)=\iota(u)\,,u\not{\sim}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\phi}\E_{\sigma}\left[|\Gamma\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\delta}{\phi}\left|\Gamma\right|\qquad

by
Table

2

\displaystyle\leq 4\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon\delta}{\phi^{5}}n

by
Lemma

4.16
.

∎

Combining the above three lemmas, we can conclude a proof of the main result of the section.

Proof of
5.2
.

The polynomial time algorithm which we refer to in the statement of
5.2
consists of two blocks.

1.

First, compute our approximate inner product function via
Remark

4.20
with
\xi=1/n^{10}
so as to meet the requirement of
Table

1
.

2.

Then, we run
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
(see Appendix
A
) to obtain
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mu}_{k}
6
6

6

In reality,
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
do not return
k
vectors but rather
k
vertices
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
that indicate the embedding that should be used for the corresponding approximate cluster mean. This is because we do not have explicit access to vectors, but only inner product access. Hence,
Algorithm

2
will query
\langle f_{u_{i}},\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
whenever it wants to access
\langle\widetilde{\mu}_{i},\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
. We write
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}
for readability.
;

3.

Finally, we launch
Algorithm

2
with
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mu}_{k}
as input to classify each of the vertices
v\in V
. Let
\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]
be the mapping of a vertex
u\in V
to the cluster id output by
Algorithm

2
on input
u
.

Probability of success and mislassification rate.

Since both
Algorithm

2
and
Algorithm

8
use the randomness of
\sigma
, we should be careful while analyzing the probability of success of the above algorithm.
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
use additional random bits (for sampling) different from the random bits of
\sigma
, and therefore this randomness is independent from the randomness of
\sigma
.
Lemma

4.24
shows that with probability
0.97
over the randomness of
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
one has that
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mu}_{k}
meet the condition of
Definition

4.4
with probability
0.999
over
\sigma
.

Let us then fix the internal randomness
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
such that
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mu}_{k}
meet the condition of
Definition

4.4
with probability
0.999
over
\sigma
. One can note that
\sigma
only influences the ordering of the
k
vectors
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mu}_{k}
: this is because the set of
k
vectors is computed by
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
computes the permutation
\pi
that gives their ordering, but
Algorithm

7
does not use
\sigma
at all. Hence, when the randomness of
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
is fixed, the labeling
\alpha
constructed with
Algorithm

2
only depends on
\sigma
and on the permutation
\pi
produced by
Algorithm

8
. Let
\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi(\sigma))
denote the event that the labeling
\alpha
is wrong on more than
10^{9}\eta^{2}d\epsilon\delta/\phi^{5}\cdot n
vertices when
Algorithm

2
is given as input the labels and
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mu}_{k}
which are indexed using the permutation
\pi
(which is an output of
Algorithm

8
and therefore it depends on
\sigma
). Observe that
Algorithm

2
itself is deterministic, and therefore
Fail
is a deterministic function of a labeling and a permutation. Let
\pi^{*}
denote the correct permutation for the set of vectors produced by
Algorithm

7
.

\displaystyle\Pr_{\sigma}[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi(\sigma))]

(5.2)

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\sigma}\left[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi(\sigma))\middle|\pi(\sigma)=\pi^{*}\right]\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}\left[\pi(\sigma)=\pi^{*}\right]+\Pr_{\sigma}\left[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi(\sigma))\middle|\pi(\sigma)\neq\pi^{*}\right]\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\pi(\sigma)\neq\pi^{*}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr_{\sigma}\left[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi(\sigma))\middle|\pi(\sigma)=\pi^{*}\right]+\Pr_{\sigma}[\pi(\sigma)\neq\pi^{*}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\Pr_{\sigma}[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi(\sigma))\land\pi(\sigma)=\pi^{*}]+\Pr_{\sigma}[\pi(\sigma)\neq\pi^{*}]

\displaystyle=2\Pr_{\sigma}[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi^{*})\land\pi(\sigma)=\pi^{*}]+\Pr_{\sigma}[\pi(\sigma)\neq\pi^{*}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\Pr_{\sigma}[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi^{*})]+\Pr_{\sigma}[\pi(\sigma)\neq\pi^{*}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\Pr_{\sigma}[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi^{*})]+10^{-3}\quad\quad\text{by the fixed internal randomness \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{alg:app_centers} and \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{alg:permutation}.}

Observe that
\Pr_{\sigma}[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi^{*})]
is precisely the probability of failure of
Algorithm

2
. To bound it, we now define four groups of vertices
W_{1},W_{2},W_{3},W_{4}
that together cover the set of vertices
u\in V
such that
\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)
.

•

Let
W_{1}
be the set of vertices
u\in V
such that
\tau(u)\in\{*,\sigma(u)\}
and
\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)
. For every
u\in W_{1}
, we know that
\alpha(u)=\sigma(u)
by definition of
Algorithm

2
(see the return statements in lines (
4
) and (
5
)). Then, each
u\in W_{1}
satisfies
\tau(u)\in\{*,\sigma(u)\}
and
\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
, so by virtue of
Lemma

5.3
we know that the expected size of
W_{1}
is at most
2\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\phi^{-4}\cdot\epsilon\delta\cdot n
.

•

Let
W_{2}
be the set of vertices
u\in V
such that
\tau(u)\notin\{*,\sigma(u)\}
,
u\sim_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}
and
\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)
. For every
u\in W_{2}
, we know that
\alpha(u)=\sigma(u)
by definition of
Algorithm

2
(see the return statement in line (
9
)). Then, each
u\in W_{2}
satisfies
\sigma(u)\notin\{\iota(u),\tau(u)\},\tau(u)\neq*
and
u\sim_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}
, so by
Lemma

5.4
we know that the expected size of
W_{2}
is at most
6\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\phi^{-4}\cdot d\epsilon\delta\cdot n
.

•

Let
W_{3}
be the set of vertices
u\in V
such that
\tau(u)\notin\{*,\sigma(u)\}
,
u\not{\sim}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}
,
\sigma(u)=\iota(u)
, and
\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)
. Then, each
u\in W_{3}
satisfies the three conditions
\tau(u)\notin\{*,\sigma(u)\}
,
\sigma(u)=\iota(u)
, and
u\not{\sim}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}
, so by
Lemma

5.7
we know that the expected size of
W_{3}
is at most
4\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\phi^{-5}\cdot\epsilon\delta\cdot n
.

•

Let
W_{4}
be the set of vertices
u\in V
such that
\tau(u)\notin\{*,\sigma(u)\}
,
u\not{\sim}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}
,
\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
, and
\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)
. For every
u\in W_{4}
, we know that
\alpha(u)=\tau(u)
by definition of
Algorithm

2
(see the return statement in line (
11
)). Then, each vertex
u\in W_{4}
satisfies
\tau(u)\neq\iota(u)
and
\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
, which implies that
W_{4}\subseteq\{u\in\Gamma:\,\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)\}
. By linearity of expectation and
Lemma

4.16
, the expected size of
W_{4}
is at most
2\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\phi^{-4}\cdot\epsilon\delta\cdot n
.

Thus, by a simple application of Markov’s inequality we have an algorithm that misclassifies at most a
O({d\epsilon\delta}/{\phi^{5}})
fraction of vertices with probability

\Pr_{\sigma}[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi^{*})]\leq 10^{-3}\,.

By (
5.2
), we arrive at

\Pr_{\sigma}[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi(\sigma))]\leq 2\cdot 10^{-3}+10^{-3}\leq 0.99\,.

Since by
Lemma

4.24
the probability that the internal randomness of
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
meets our requirement is
0.97
, we get an overall success probability of
0.96
as required.

Running time.

The running time of the algorithm is dominated by by the time needed to approximate
\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle
for all pairs
u,v\in V
and the time needed to check whether the set
\mathcal{X}
is reachable from a vertex
u
for all
u\in V
. We approximate the spectral inner products with the polynomial-time algorithm in
Remark

4.20
, and we check reachability with the BFS algorithm.
∎

6
Sublinear-time classifier with near-optimal rate

In this section we outline and analyze a sublinear time algorithm that given the graph, the approximate cluster means, and the perturbed label, outputs a cluster id for each vertex. We will show that the number of vertices such that the output id is different from
\iota(u)
is at most a
{\epsilon\delta/\phi^{6}\cdot\log(1/\delta)}
fraction.

Algorithm 3

Sublinear-time classifier

1:

Input:

G,\phi,\eta,(\widetilde{\mu}_{i})_{i\in[k]}
as per
Table

1
,
\sigma
as per
Table

2
, and a vertex
u\in V

2:

Output:
a cluster id in
[k]

3:

if

\tau(u)=\sigma(u)

then

4:

return

\sigma(u)

\triangleright
Label agrees with spectral information

5:

else

if

\tau(u)=*

then

6:

return

\sigma(u)

\triangleright
Spectral information is ambiguous

7:

else
\triangleright
Label disagrees with spectral information

8:

if

RobustConn
(
G,\phi,\eta,(\widetilde{\mu}_{i})_{i\in[k]},\sigma,u
) returns “yes”
then

\triangleright
See
Algorithm

4

9:

return

\sigma(u)

\triangleright
Trust the label,
u
is likely a spectral impostor

10:

else

11:

return

\tau(u)

\triangleright
Trust spectral information, the label is likely wrong

12:

end

if

13:

end

if

Note that
Algorithm

3
is very similar to
Algorithm

2
. For a vertex
u
, both
Algorithm

3
and
Algorithm

2
first check if the label and the spectral information about
u
indicate the same cluster and whether
u
is a cross vertex (as per
Definition

4.5
). After this,
Algorithm

2
simply checks whether there exists a walk in the graph
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
connecting
u
to some vertex in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
. In the analysis of
Algorithm

2
,
Lemma

5.4
and
Lemma

5.7
informally assert the following: for all except for
\approx d\epsilon\delta/\mathrm{poly}(\phi)\cdot n
many vertices
u
that are not cross vertices and for which the spectral information
\tau(u)
disagrees with the label
\sigma(u)
, there exists a walk in the graph
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
connecting
u
and some vertex in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
if and only if
\sigma(u)=\iota(u)
. As it turns out, a stronger version of this statement is true:

Lemma

(Informal – see
Lemma

6.27
and
Lemma

6.35
)
.

There exists a set
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\subseteq\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
of a much smaller size than
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
such that the following is true: for all except
\approx\epsilon\delta\log(1/\delta)n
many vertices
u
that are not cross vertices and for which the spectral information
\tau(u)
disagrees with the label
\sigma(u)
, one has that

•

if
\sigma(u)=\iota(u)
, then
almost all
of bounded-length walks in
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
starting at
u
pass through
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
;

•

if
\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
then
almost no
bounded-length walk in
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
starting at
u
passes through
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
.

We formalize and prove this statement in
Section

6.3
.
Intuitively, we think of
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
as a set of “problematic” vertices: the vertices in the set
\mathcal{X}
do not bear any spectral information about which cluster they belong to, and they “confuse” their immediate neighbors too. In
Section

6.2
we show that all of the actually “problematic” vertices of
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
are contained is a subset of much smaller size, referred to as
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
in the above. This observation allows us to improve the upper bound on the misclassification rate from
\approx d\epsilon\delta
to
\approx\epsilon\delta\log(1/\delta)
. Therefore, we replace the reachability test in
Algorithm

2
with a
robust connectedness vs separatedness
test, which amounts to sampling a few walks starting at
u
in the graph
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
and using them to approximate the total number of walks passing through
S
. We formally define this procedure, referred to as
RobustConn
, and prove its performance guarantees in
Section

6.1
. This modification allows us to replace the expensive graph connectivity test with sublinear time sampling.

We formally state the performance guarantees of
Algorithm

3
in
6.1
.

Theorem 6.1

(Sublinear-time and -space classifier)
.

There is an algorithm running in time
d\cdot 2^{O({\phi^{2}}/{\epsilon})k^{4}\log k}

n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/(\xi\phi))\mathrm{poly}(\log(n/\delta))
that, given as input
G,d,n,\eta,\epsilon,\phi,k,\delta
as per
Table

1
and
\sigma
as per
Table

2
, produces a data structure of size
d\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\mathrm{poly}(k/(\phi\xi))\mathrm{poly}(\log(n/\delta))
with query time
d\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(k/(\phi\xi))\mathrm{poly}(\log(n/\delta))
. With probability
0.95
over the internal randomness of the algorithm and the draw of
\sigma
, there exists a subset of vertices
B\subseteq V
of size
|B|\leq O({\epsilon\delta}\cdot{\phi^{-6}}\log(1/\delta))\cdot n
such that for every vertex
u\in V\setminus B
, querying the data structure on
u
returns a cluster id
i\in[k]
such that
u\in C_{i}
.

Remark 6.2

(Additional bounds on parameters)
.

Observe that the above algorithm has truly sublinear time and space under the assumption

\frac{\phi^{2}}{\epsilon}\cdot k^{4}\log k\leq c\log n

for a small enough constant
c>0
, which implies both an upper bound on
k
and a lower bound on
\epsilon
. In particular, the above is satisfied when
k\leq\log^{c_{1}}n
for a sufficiently small constant
c_{1}
, and
\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{2}}\geq 1/\log^{c_{2}}n
for a sufficiently large constant
c_{2}
. We do not put these assumptions in the
Table

1
as they are not necessary for anything apart from ensuring the data structure from
[
13
]
has sublinear time and space. Furthermore, note that if the above assumptions are not satisfied, we may opt for the Euclidean inner product oracle introduced in
Remark

4.20
and used in
Section

5
in design of a simpler algorithm. While this makes the running time of
Algorithm

3
polynomial,
Algorithm

3
still has merit as its misclassification rate guarantee is independent of
d
.

The rest of the section is structured as follows: in
Section

6.1
, we formally define the set
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, present the
RobustConn
algorithm, and prove its performance guarantees; in
Section

6.2
, we establish the key properties of the set
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
; finally, in
Section

6.3
, we prove
6.1
.

6.1
RobustConn

Before we describe
RobustConn
, we introduce several notions we will use in the discussion.

Definition 6.3

(Lazy walk)
.

Let
H
be a graph, and let
l\geq 1
be an integer. A sequence of
l
vertices
\bf{w}

\in V(H)^{l}
is a
l
-step lazy walk in
H
if for all
i\in[l-1]
one has either
(w_{i},w_{i+1})\in E(H)
or
w_{i}=w_{i+1}
.

Definition 6.4

(Lazy random walks)
.

Let
d\geq 3
be an integer, let
H
be a
d
-regular graph, let
A
be its adjacency matrix, let
M=\frac{1}{2}I+\frac{1}{2d}A
, and let
l\geq 1
be an integer. We define the
l
-step lazy random walk distribution in
H
, denoted
p^{l}[H]
, to be the following distribution over
l
-step lazy walks
\bf{w}
in
H
:

•

w_{1}
is distributed uniformly over
V(H)
;

•

for each
i\in[l-1]
,
w_{i+1}
conditioned on
w_{i}
is distributed as
M\mathbbm{1}_{w_{i}}
.

Similarly, for a set
S\subseteq V(H)
, we define the
l
-step lazy random walk distribution in
H
starting from
S
, denoted
p_{S}^{l}[H]
, to be the following distribution over
l
-step lazy walks
\bf{w}
in
H
:
w_{1}
is distributed uniformly over
S
and
w_{i+1}
conditioned on
w_{i}
is distributed as
M\mathbbm{1}_{w_{i}}
for each
i\in[l-1]
.

Notation
.

For
H,l,S
as in the definition above, if
S=\{u\}
for some
u\in V(H)
, we allow ourselves to abuse notation and write
p^{l}_{u}[H]
instead of
p_{\{u\}}^{l}[H]
.

Definition 6.5

(Walks reaching a set)
.

Let
H
be a graph, let
l\geq 1
be an integer, and let
\bf{w}
be a
l
-step lazy walk in
H
, and let
T\subseteq V(H)
. We say that
\bf{w}
reaches
T
if there exists
i\in[l]
such that
w_{i}\in T
. If
\bf{w}
reaches
T
we write
\bf{w}

\sim T
, otherwise we write
\bf{w}

\not{\sim}\ T
.

As we mentioned at the beginning of
Section

6
,
RobustConn
(
Algorithm

4
) is based on testing reachability to a certain special set
\textsc{S}\subseteq\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
. We define
S
as
\mathcal{X}
itself and the
regularized neighborhood
of
\mathcal{X}
, denoted
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, which we formally introduce in
Definition

6.6
below.

Definition 6.6

(Regularized neighborhood of
\mathcal{X}
)
.

Let
\phi
,
(f_{u})_{u\in V}
and
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mu}_{k}
be as per
Table

1
.
We define the regularized neighborhood of
\mathcal{X}
, which we denote
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, to be the vertices in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
that belong to some spectral cluster
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
but have at least
\frac{\phi}{2}d
neighbors in other spectral clusters, i.e.

\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\coloneqq\left\{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})\setminus\mathcal{X}:|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\lnot\tau(u))|\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d\right\}.

Definition 6.7

(Crossing walk)
.

Let
L
as per
Table

1
. Fix
i
and
j
,
i\neq j
. We say that a walk
\bf{w}
in
G_{i,j}
(as per
Definition

4.13
) starting from a vertex
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
of length
L
is a crossing walk if
\bf{w}

\sim\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, i.e. if
\bf{w}
reaches
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
(as per
Definition

6.5
).

Algorithm 4

RobustConn

1:

Input:

G,\phi,\eta,\{\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\}_{[k]},L
as per
Table

1
,
\sigma,\tau
as per
Table

2
, a vertex
u

2:

Output:
“yes” or “no”

3:

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\text{apx}}\leftarrow\textsc{DP}

\triangleright
Dot product oracle from Thm.
4.21

4:

r\leftarrow 0

\triangleright
Counter of crossing walks

5:

for

i=1
to
450\log n

do

6:

\mathbf{w}_{i}\leftarrow
lazy random walk of length
L
in
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
started at
u

7:

for
every vertex
w
in
\mathbf{w}_{i}

do

8:

if

\|f_{w}-\widetilde{\mu}_{\tau(u)}\|^{2}_{\text{apx}}>\frac{\phi^{2}}{400\eta}\|\widetilde{\mu}_{\tau(u)}\|^{2}_{\text{apx}}

then

9:

r\leftarrow r+1

\triangleright

w\in\mathcal{X}
, so
\mathbf{w}_{i}
is a crossing walk

10:

break

11:

end

if

12:

q\leftarrow 0

\triangleright
Counter of neighbors in
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg\tau(u))

13:

for
every neighbor
v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(w)

do

14:

if
for some
\widetilde{\mu}_{l}\neq\widetilde{\mu}_{\tau(u)}
, it holds that
\|f_{v}-\widetilde{\mu}_{l}\|^{2}_{\text{apx}}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{400\eta}\|\widetilde{\mu}_{l}\|^{2}_{\text{apx}}

then

15:

q\leftarrow q+1

\triangleright

v\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg\tau(u))

16:

end

if

17:

end

for

18:

if

q\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d

then

19:

r\leftarrow r+1

\triangleright

w\in\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, so
\mathbf{w}_{i}
is a crossing walk

20:

break

21:

end

if

22:

end

for

23:

end

for

24:

if

r\geq 0.5\cdot 450\log n

then

25:

return
“yes”
\triangleright

u
is robustly connected
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}

26:

else

27:

return
“no”
\triangleright

u
is
not
robustly connected
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}

28:

end

if

In
Lemma

4.15
, we showed that vertices in spectral clusters which are
not
connected to the cross vertices
\mathcal{X}
, have few edges to other spectral clusters. This property is important because it allows us prove that spectral impostors have good expansion properties outside of
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
(see
Lemma

4.17
).

This motivates the definition of
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
as the “problematic” part of
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
: It identifies the vertices in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
that have too many edges to other spectral clusters. With this definition, we can extend
Lemma

4.15
to also apply to vertices in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, as the following lemma shows.

Lemma 6.8

(Few neighbors across spectral clusters, extended to
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
.
For every
i\in[k]
and every
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, it holds that

\left|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\lnot i)\right|\leq d\cdot\frac{\phi}{2}.

Proof.

This proof is an extension of
Lemma

4.15
. Recall that
Lemma

4.15
proves the above statement for
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
. Therefore, it remains to consider
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}.

By definition of
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
(Definition
4.5
),
u
is not in the cross vertices
\mathcal{X}
, so
u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})\setminus(\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\cup\mathcal{X}).
So from the definition of
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
(Definition
6.6
), we have

\left|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\lnot i)\right|\leq d\cdot\frac{\phi}{2}.

∎

In
Section

6.2
we strengthen the “impostors expand” lemma (
Lemma

4.17
) to also hold for vertices in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
(see
Lemma

6.13
), and we also
show an upper bound of
\approx\epsilon n
on the size of
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
(see
Lemma

6.14
), which is stronger than the upper bound of
\approx d\epsilon n
on the size of
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
.

Recall from
Definition

4.13
the notion of a cross graph
G_{i,j}
and
V_{i,j}=(\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j))\cup\mathcal{X}
.

Remark 6.9

(Sampling from
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
, line (
6
))
.

Given a vertex
u\in G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
, access to the
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
, and a sampler from the graph
G
, we can generate a random walk
\text{$\bf{w}$}=(u,w_{1},\ldots,w_{L})
of length
L
in the graph
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
with the following simple procedure:

1.

For every
l\leq L
, given
w_{l}
, sample a vertex
v\sim\mathrm{unif}(\mathrm{N}_{G}(w_{l}))
;

2.

If
v\notin V_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
set
w_{l+1}\coloneqq w_{l}
; otherwise,
w_{l+1}\coloneqq v
.

Condition
v\notin V_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
is satisfied either if
\sigma(v)\neq\sigma(u)
or if
v\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg\tau(u))
. The first condition may be verified immediately. To verify the second condition, we need to check if there exists
i\in[k]
,
i\neq\tau(u)
such that
\|f_{v}-\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{400\eta}\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}
. This can be done by using the Spectral Dot Product Oracle from
4.21
at most
k
times.

A random walk in the graph
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
starting from a vertex
u
can thus be generated in time

O(L\cdot k\cdot Q)=O(\mathrm{poly}(k)\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(\log(n))\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\phi)\cdot\log(1/\delta)),

where
Q
denotes the query time of the Spectral Dot Product Oracle.

We are finally ready to formally define robust connectedness/separatedness and prove why the procedure
RobustConn
successfully determines vertices which satisfy either one of these properties in the corresponding cross graph.

Definition 6.10

(Robustly connected/separated)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
.

We say that a vertex
u\in V_{i,j}
is
robustly connected to

\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
in the graph
G_{i,j}
if

\Pr[\text{a lazy random walk of length $L$ in $G_{i,j}$ started at $u$ is crossing}]\geq 0.9.

We write
u\rightsquigarrow_{G_{i,j}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
if
u
is robustly connected to
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
and
u\not{\rightsquigarrow}_{G_{i,j}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
otherwise.

We say that a vertex
u\in V_{i,j}
is
robustly separated from

\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
in the graph
G_{i,j}
if

\Pr[\text{a lazy random walk of length $L$ in $G_{i,j}$ started at $u$ is crossing}]<0.1.

We write
u\perp_{G_{i,j}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
if
u
is robustly separated from
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
and
u\not{\perp}_{G_{i,j}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
otherwise.

Lemma 6.11

(
RobustConn
detects robust conectedness and separatedness)
.

Conditioned on the success of all of the calls to the Spectral Dot Product Oracle,
DP
, we have that

1.

If a vertex
u
is robustly connected to
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, then
RobustConn
outputs “yes” with probability at least
1-2/n^{5}
.

2.

If a vertex
u
is robustly separated from
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, then
RobustConn
outputs “no” with probability at least
1-2/n^{5}
.

Proof.

First, we show that if all of the calls to
DP
are correct, then the parameter
r
, counted in lines (
9
), (
19
) is exactly equal to the number of crossing walks out of the
450\log n
sampled in line (
6
).
Fix
i\in[450\log n]
. Suppose that
\mathbf{w}_{i}
is a crossing walk. Then, by
Definition

6.7
, there exists a vertex
w\in\mathbf{w}_{i}
such that
w\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
. Let
w
be the first such vertex in
\mathbf{w}_{i}
. If
w\in\mathcal{X}
, then by
Definition

4.5
, line (
8
) gets triggered and we increment the counter
r
and finish processing
\mathbf{w}_{i}
. If instead
w\in\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\setminus\mathcal{X}
, then
\tau(w)\neq*
, so
w\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\tau(w))
. Then in lines (
12
) - (
17
),
q
counts the number of elements in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(w)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg\tau(u))
. Since
w\in\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, it holds that
|\mathrm{N}_{G}(w)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg\tau(u))|\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d
(by
Definition

6.6
), so we increment
r
in line (
19
) and finish processing
\mathbf{w}_{i}
. Thus, if
\mathbf{w}_{i}
is a crossing walk then the counter
r
gets incremented by exactly one.

Suppose instead that
\mathbf{w}_{i}
is
not
a crossing walk. Then, by
Definition

6.7
, for every
w\in\mathbf{w}_{i}
, it holds that
w\notin\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
. Fix
w\in\mathbf{w}_{i}
. Since
w\notin\mathcal{X}
, line (
8
) does not get triggered (by
Definition

4.5
). Furthermore, since
w\notin\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, by
Definition

6.6
we either have
|\mathrm{N}_{G}(w)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg\tau(w))|<\frac{\phi}{2}d
or
w\notin\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
. In the former case, we have
q<\frac{\phi}{2}d
, so line (
18
) does not get triggered. In the latter case, i.e.
w\notin\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
, by
Lemma

4.15
we have
|\mathrm{N}_{G}(w)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg\tau(w))|<\frac{\phi}{2}d
, so again line (
18
) does not get triggered. Since this holds for all
w\in\mathbf{w}_{i}
, the counter
r
does not get incremented.

Having shown that the parameter
r
correctly counts the number of crossing walks, we now prove each of the two statements in the Lemma statement.

Statement 1.

By
Definition

6.10
, if
u\rightsquigarrow_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
then a random lazy walk starting at
u
in the graph
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
is a crossing walk with probability
\geq 0.9
. Let
X
be the number of crossing walks among the
450\log n
generated walks. We can think of
X
as the number of successes in a Bernoulli process with
450\log n
trials and success probability
\geq 0.9
. Therefore, by Chernoff bounds,

\Pr[X\leq 0.9\cdot 450\log n-90\log n]\leq 2\exp(5\log n)\leq\frac{2}{n^{5}}.

In other words, with probability
1-\frac{2}{n^{5}}
at least
0.7\cdot 450\log n
of the walks are crossing walks.
RobustConn
will recognize each of those walks and the line (
24
) will be triggered.

Statement 2.

By
Definition

6.10
, if
u\perp_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
then a random lazy walk starting at
u
in the graph
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
is a crossing walk with probability
\leq 0.1
. Let
X
be the number of crossing walks among the
450\log n
generated walks. We can think of
X
as the number of successes in a Bernoulli process with
450\log n
trials and success probability
\leq 0.1
. Therefore, by Chernoff bounds,

\Pr[X\geq 45\log n+45\log n]\leq 2\exp(-5\log n)\leq\frac{2}{n^{5}}.

In other words, with probability
1-\frac{2}{n^{5}}
at most
90\log n
of the walks are crossing walks.
RobustConn
will recognize at most
90\log n
of the walks as crossing walks and the line (
26
) will be triggered.

∎

Lemma 6.12

(Runtime and space complexity of
RobustConn
)
.

The space complexity of
RobustConn
is the same as space complexity of the Spectral Dot Product Oracle, as per
4.21
, which equals

O(\mathrm{poly}(k)\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{polylog}(n)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\phi)),

and the runtime is

O(d\cdot L\cdot k\cdot Q\cdot\log n)=O(d\cdot\mathrm{poly}(k)\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{polylog}(n)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\phi)\cdot\log(1/\delta)),

where
Q
denotes the query time of the Spectral Dot Product Oracle.

Proof.

Besides the data structure
\mathcal{D}
required by the Spectral Dot Product Oracle,
RobustConn
stores at most one random walk of length
L
and two counters
r
and
q
at any point in time. Therefore, the space complexity of
RobustConn
is the same as the space complexity of the Spectral Dot Product Oracle.

Now, we show an upper bound on the runtime of
RobustConn
. By
Remark

6.9
, one call to line (
7
) takes time
O(L\cdot k\cdot Q)
. For every vertex
w\in\text{$\bf{w}$}_{i}
, the procedure in lines (
8
)-(
10
) which checks if
w\in\mathcal{X}
takes time
O(Q)
, and the procedure in lines (
12
)-(
20
) which checks if
w\in\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
takes time
O(d\cdot k\cdot Q)
. Therefore, the total runtime of
RobustConn
is
O(d\cdot k\cdot L\cdot Q\cdot\log n)
, as desired.
∎

6.2
The regularized neighborhood

In this section, we prove the two crucial properties of the set
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
: first, its size is upper-bounded by
\approx\epsilon n
; second, the “impostors expand” Lemma (see
Lemma

4.17
), which states that subsets of
\Gamma\setminus\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})
expand, can be strengthened to guarantee that subsets of
\Gamma\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
expand. These two facts together allow us to reduce the number of misclassified vertices from
\approx\delta|\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})|\approx\delta d\epsilon n
to
\approx\delta|\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|\approx\delta\epsilon n
.

We begin by showing the latter of the two properties of
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
.

Lemma 6.13

(Impostors expand, extended to
\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X})\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
.
For every
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
and every
S\subseteq\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, one has

|E_{G[C_{j}]}(S,(\Gamma(i,j)\cup\mathcal{X})\setminus S)|\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d|S|\,.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to the proof of Lemma
4.17
.
For simplicity of notation, we write
E_{j}
to denote
E(C_{j})
throughout this proof.
Note that
|S|\leq|\Gamma|\leq 2\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n\leq\frac{|C_{j}|}{2}
, where the second inequality follows by
Lemma

4.16
, and the third inequality follows by the setting of parameters in
Table

1
. Since
S\subseteq C_{j}
, and since
C_{j}
is a
\phi
-expander, we have

\left|E_{j}(S,C_{j}\setminus S)\right|=\left|E(S,C_{j}\setminus S)\right|\geq\phi\cdot d\cdot|S|.

(6.1)

We will now show that a large fraction of the edges in
E_{j}(S,C_{j}\setminus S)
go to
\Gamma(i,j)\cup\mathcal{X}
.
We have

\displaystyle C_{j}

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i)\cup\Gamma(i,j).

Thus,

\displaystyle|E_{j}(S,C_{j}\setminus S)|

\displaystyle\leq\left|E_{j}(S,\mathcal{X})\right|+\left|E_{j}(S,\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i))\right|+\left|E_{j}(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)\right|

(6.2)

\displaystyle\leq\left|E_{j}(S,\mathcal{X})\right|+\sum_{u\in S}\left|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i)\right|+\left|E_{j}(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|E_{j}(S,\mathcal{X})\right|+|S|\cdot d\cdot\frac{\phi}{2}+\left|E_{j}(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)\right|\qquad\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:NM_neighbors}.}

If we combine the above bound with (
6.1
) and rearrange, we conclude

\displaystyle|E_{j}(S,(\Gamma(i,j)\cup\mathcal{X})\setminus S)|

\displaystyle=|E_{j}(S,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus S)|+|E_{j}(S,\mathcal{X})|

since
S\subseteq\Gamma
and
\Gamma\cap\mathcal{X}=\emptyset

\displaystyle\geq|E_{j}(S,C_{j}\setminus S)|-|S|\cdot d\cdot\frac{\phi}{2}

by Equation (
6.2
)

\displaystyle\geq|S|\cdot d\cdot\frac{\phi}{2}

by Equation (
6.1
).

∎

In the rest of the section, we prove the other property of
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, namely
|\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|\lesssim\epsilon n
.

Lemma 6.14
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. Then,

|\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|\leq 10^{8}\eta^{5}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n\,.

To prove the lemma, we first introduce an auxiliary family of sets
\{H_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
, which are the sets of vertices that have an abnormally high projection onto a cluster mean
\mu_{i}
.

Definition 6.15

(High-projection vertices)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
.
For
i\in[k]
, we define the
i
-th set of high-projection vertices, denoted
H_{i}
, as

H_{i}\coloneqq\left\{v\in V:\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle\geq\left(1+\frac{\phi}{8}\right)\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\right\}\,.

We show that the high projection sets
H_{i}
are subsets of the cross-vertices
\mathcal{X}
. Intuitively, this is true because vertices in
H_{i}
are essentially parallel to
\mu_{i}
, so they cannot be in any
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)
for
j\neq i
since
\mu_{i}
and
\mu_{j}
are roughly orthogonal, and they cannot be in
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
since they are too long in the direction of
\mu_{i}
.

Lemma 6.16
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
.
Then,
H_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
for all
i\in[k].

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that there exists some
v\in H_{i}\setminus\mathcal{X}
. Since
v\notin\mathcal{X}
is not a cross vertex, we must have
v\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)
for some
j\in[k]
(by Definition
4.5
). Therefore, by
Lemma

4.6
, we have

\|f_{v}-\mu_{j}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{j}\|_{2},

so by the triangle inequality we have

|\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle|\leq|\langle f_{v}-\mu_{j},\mu_{i}\rangle|+|\langle\mu_{j},\mu_{i}\rangle|\leq\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{j}\|_{2}\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}+|\langle\mu_{i},\mu_{j}\rangle|.

(6.3)

If
i=j
, then by (
6.3
), we get

|\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle|\leq\left(1+\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}<\left(1+\frac{\phi}{8}\right)\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\,,

since
\eta\geq 1
by definition (see
Table

1
), which contradicts
v\in H_{i}
. If instead
i\neq j
, we plug guarantee
(2)
of
Lemma

3.6
into (
6.3
) and get

|\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle|\leq\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\|\mu_{j}\|_{2}+\frac{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{|C_{i}||C_{j}|}}

(6.4)

Now, by guarantee
(1)
of
Lemma

3.6
, we also have

\left(1-\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\right)\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}\leq\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\left(1+\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\right)\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}\quad\text{and}\quad\left(1-\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\right)\frac{1}{|C_{j}|}\leq\|\mu_{j}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\left(1+\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\right)\frac{1}{|C_{j}|}\,.

(6.5)

We can plug these into (
6.4
) to get

|\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle|\leq\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\|\mu_{j}\|_{2}+\frac{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}}\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\|\mu_{j}\|_{2}\,,

Finally, we combine the definition of
\eta
(see
Table

1
) with (
6.5
) and upper-bound

\|\mu_{j}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\left(1+\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\right)\frac{1}{|C_{j}|}}\leq\sqrt{\left(1+\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\right)\frac{\eta}{|C_{i}|}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1+\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}}{1-\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}}\cdot\eta\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}}\leq\frac{1+\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}}{1-\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}}\sqrt{\eta}\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\,,

which ultimately gives

|\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle|\leq\left(\frac{\phi}{10}+\frac{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\eta}}{1-\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}}\right)\frac{1+\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}}{1-\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}}\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}<\frac{\phi}{8}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\,,

(6.6)

where the penultimate step uses our assumption that
{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sqrt{\eta}\leq{10^{-5}}\phi
(see
Table

1
). We have thus reached a contradiction with
v\in H_{i}
, since
\phi/8\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}<(1+\phi/8)\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}
.
∎

We now introduce some terminology that will help us motivate the definition of another auxiliary family of sets and facilitate our discussions in the remainder of the section.

Terminology
.

In
Lemma

3.5
, we show that a spectral embedding of a vertex roughly equals the average of the spectral embeddings of its neighbors. For a vertex
u
and one of its neighbors
v
, we say that
v

pulls

f_{u}
with
force

f_{u}-f_{v}
. Thus,
Lemma

3.5
informally says that the average force with which the neighbors of
v
pull
f_{v}
is roughly
0
.

In
Figure

3
we depict a vertex
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
and it’s neighbors whose projections on
\mu_{i}
is either much bigger than
\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}
(these are connected to
u
with solid vetors) or much smaller (these are connected to you with dashed vectors). From the above, the sum of projections of the solid vectors on
\mu_{i}
should we roughly equal to the sum of projections of dashed vectors. Furthermore,
Figure

3
also provides an illustration of the set
H_{i}
.

\mu_{i}
u
\sqrt{1+\frac{\phi}{8}}\cdot\mu_{i}
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
H_{i}

Figure 3:
An illustration of a vertex
u
in
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
. The solid arrows point to the neighbors of
u
with high projection on
\mu_{i}
, and dashed arrows point to the neighbors with small projection on
\mu_{i}
. The solid and dashed arrows balance each other.

Thus, we define the family of
strongly-pulled vertices

\{P_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
as follows:
P_{i}
consists of the vertices in the
i
-th spectral cluster that experience a strong pulling force in the direction of
\mu_{i}
from their neighbors in
H_{i}
, which have a large projection on
\mu_{i}
(by
Definition

6.15
). Another way to think about
P_{i}
is the following: it consists of vertices in the
i
-th spectral cluster that have “many” neighbors in
H_{i}
, where “many” is weighted by the force
f_{v}-f_{u}
. The sets
\{P_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
are formally introduced in
Definition

6.17
below.

Definition 6.17

(Strongly-pulled vertices)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
.
For
i\in[k]
, we define the
i
-th set of strongly-pulled vertices, denoted
P_{i}
, as

P_{i}\coloneqq\left\{u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i):\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap H_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\geq\frac{\phi}{10}d\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\right\}\,.

Remark 6.18
.

For any
i\in[k]
, according to the definition above, we put
u\in V
in
P_{i}
if it satisfies two conditions:
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
and
\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap H_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\geq d\phi/10\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}
. We remark that the first condition uses approximate cluster means and approximate inner products (see
Definition

4.5
), while the second condition uses exact cluster means and the standard dot product between spectral embeddings.

Equipped with the notion of strongly-pulled vertices, we have the following two lemmas, from which we can easily conclude a proof of
Lemma

6.14
.

Lemma 6.19
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. Then

\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[k]}P_{i}\,.

Lemma 6.20
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. Then

\left|\bigcup_{i\in[k]}P_{i}\right|\leq 10^{8}\eta^{5}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n\,.

Proof of
Lemma

6.14
.

Follows immediately from
Lemma

6.19
and
Lemma

6.20
.
∎

To conclude, we prove
Lemma

6.19
in
Section

6.2.1
and

Lemma

6.20
in
Section

6.2.2
.

6.2.1
The regularized neighborhood consists of strongly-pulled vertices

In this section, we prove
Lemma

6.19
, which states that the regularized neighborhood
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
is a subset of the union of
P_{1},\dots,P_{k}
. Before diving into the details, we discuss the intuition for the proof.

Consider a vertex
u\in\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
. By definition,
u
is in the
i
-th spectral cluster, for some
i\in[k]
, and it has
\phi d/2
neighbors in other spectral clusters. Informally, if
u\in\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
and
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
then
u
has many neighbors in other spectral clusters, so looking at
Figure

3
, there must be many dashed arrows coming out of
u
. Since the dashed arrows have to be balanced by solid arrows, there must be many solid arrows as well. And this, in turn, implies that
u
has to have many neighbors in
H_{i}
which means that
u\in P_{i}
by the definition of
P_{i}
.

We now provide a more formal sketch of the proof. Let
u\in\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
for some
i\in[k]
.
Suppose that
v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i)
. By definition of
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
(
Definition

6.6
),
u
has at least
\phi d/2
such neighbors. Note that the amount of force with which any such neighbor
v
pulls
f_{u}
in the direction of
\mu_{i}
is negative, more specifically

\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\approx\left\langle\mu_{\tau(v)}-\mu_{i},\mu_{i}\right\rangle\approx-\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\,.

On the other hand, we know that
f_{u}
actually ends up being very close to
\mu_{i}
(since
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
by definition of
P_{i}
), but by the balance equations (see
Lemma

3.5
), we know that

\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\approx 0\,.

This implies that the other neighbors of
u
must compensate by exerting a strong pulling force on
f_{u}
back in the direction of
\mu_{i}
, i.e.

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\,:\\
v\notin\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i)\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\gtrsim d\frac{\phi}{2}\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\,.

One can now observe that the sum above is contributed to by the neighbors of
u
in
\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap H_{i}
(which are those with a large projection onto
\mu_{i}
, see
Definition

6.15
, and note that they are not in
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i)
by
Lemma

6.16
) and by the neighbors of
u
in
(\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\setminus\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i))\setminus H_{i}
. However, the latter type of neighbors, can only contribute weakly in this direction, since for each
v\in(\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\setminus\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i))\setminus H_{i}
, it holds that

\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle<(1+\phi/8)\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}
. Therefore,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)\,:\\
v\notin\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i),\\
v\notin H_{i}\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\approx\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)\,:\\
v\notin\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i),\\
v\notin H_{i}\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle-\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim d\frac{\phi}{4}\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\,,

where the last inequality uses that each terms has
\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle-\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\phi}{8}\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}
.

Therefore,
f_{u}
must be strongly “pulled” by neighbors
v\in H_{i}
with a high projection onto
\mu_{i}
, which in turn implies that
u
is in
P_{i}
:

\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)\cap H_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)\,:\\
v\notin\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i)\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)\,:\\
v\notin\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i),\\
v\notin H_{i}\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\gtrsim d\frac{\phi}{2}\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\,.

We now proceed to make the above proof plan formal. We start by showing a version of
Lemma

3.5
specialized to vertices
u
in a spectral cluster
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
, in order to bound the average force with which
u
’s neighbor pull
f_{u}
in the direction of
\mu_{i}
.

Lemma 6.21
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
.
For all
i\in[k]
and
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
, one has

\left|\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\right|\leq 4\epsilon d\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\,.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.5
, we have
