\left\|\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}(f_{u}-f_{v})\right\|_{2}=d\left\|f_{u}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}f_{v}\right\|_{2}\leq 2d\epsilon\|f_{u}\|_{2}\,.

Then, an application of Cauchy-Schwarz gives

\left|\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\right|\leq\left\|\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}(f_{u}-f_{v})\right\|_{2}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon d\|f_{u}\|_{2}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\,.

(6.7)

Furthermore, since
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
, we have by
Lemma

4.6
that
\|f_{u}-\mu_{i}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}
. So by triangle inequality we get

\|f_{u}\|_{2}\leq\|f_{u}-\mu_{i}\|_{2}+\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\leq\left(1+\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\|\mu_{i}\|\leq 2\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\,,

where the last inequality uses that
\phi\leq 1\leq\eta
as per
Table

1
. Combining this with (
6.7
) gives the statement.
∎

To continue, it is useful to partition the vertices based on their projection onto a cluster mean
\mu_{i}
: the high projection set
H_{i}
(which we already defined in
Definition

6.15
), the medium projection set
M_{i}
, and the low projection set
L_{i}
, which we now define.

Definition 6.22

(Medium- and low-projection vertices)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
.
For
i\in[k]
, we define the
i
-th set of medium-projection vertices, denoted
M_{i}
, as

M_{i}\coloneqq\left\{v\in V:\frac{\phi}{8}\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle<\left(1+\frac{\phi}{8}\right)\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\right\}\,,

and define the
i
-th set of low-projection vertices, denoted
L_{i}
, as

L_{i}\coloneqq\left\{v\in V:\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle<\frac{\phi}{8}\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\right\}\,.

With this notation, we argue that the average force with which medium-projection neighbors of
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
pull
f_{u}
in the direction of
\mu_{i}
is positive but mild, while the average force exerted by its low-projection neighbors is negative in the direction of
\mu_{i}
.

Lemma 6.23
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
.
For all
i\in[k]
and
u\in\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
, one has

\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap M_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\leq\frac{1}{4}\phi d\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap L_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\leq-\frac{3}{8}\phi d\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\,.

Proof.

Since
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
, by
Lemma

4.6
, we have
\|f_{u}-\mu_{i}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}
. Therefore,

\langle f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle=\langle\mu_{i},\mu_{i}\rangle-\langle\mu_{i}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\geq\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}-\|f_{u}-\mu_{i}\|_{2}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}\geq\left(1-\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\,.

(6.8)

For all
v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap M_{i}
, by
Definition

6.22
we have

\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle\leq(1+{\phi}/{8})\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}
, which combined with gives (
6.8
) gives

\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle-\langle f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\leq\left(1+\frac{\phi}{8}\right)\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}-\left(1-\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}<\frac{\phi}{4}\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\,,

since
\eta\geq 1
by definition (see
Table

1
). Summing over all
v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap M_{i}
, we get

\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap M_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\leq\frac{\phi}{4}d\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\,.

Which proves the first bound in the statement.
Similarly, for all
v\in L_{i}\cap\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)
, it holds that

\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle-\langle f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\leq\frac{\phi}{8}\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}-\left(1-\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\leq-\frac{3}{4}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\,,

where the first inequality uses
Definition

6.22
and (
6.8
).

Therefore, we have

\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap L_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\leq-\frac{3}{4}|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap L_{i}|\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\,.

To lower bound
|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap L_{i}|
, we will show that
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\lnot i)\subseteq L_{i}
. Then we can lower bound
|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap L_{i}|
by
|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\lnot i)|
, which in turn we know to be at least
d\phi/2
since
u\in\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
(see
Definition

6.6
), giving us the second bound in the statement:

\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap L_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\leq-\frac{3}{4}|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap L_{i}|\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq-\frac{3}{4}|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\neg i)|\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{3}{8}d\phi\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\,.

It remains to show that
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\lnot i)\subseteq L_{i}
. To this end, let
v\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\lnot i)
and let
j\in[k]
such that
v\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(j)
. One can repeat the calculation from the proof of
Lemma

6.16
in the case
j\neq i
and get the bound
\langle f_{v},\mu_{i}\rangle<\frac{\phi}{8}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}
, as shown in (
6.6
). Then,
v\in L_{i}
as per
Definition

6.22
, proving that
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\lnot i)\subseteq L_{i}
.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

6.19
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.19
.

Let
u\in\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
. By Definition
6.6
, we have
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\cap\mathcal{X}=\emptyset
. Therefore,
u\notin\mathcal{X}
, so from Definition
4.5
, we have
u\in\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
for some
i\in[k]
. Since
L_{i}
,
M_{i}
and
H_{i}
partition
V
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap H_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle-\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap M_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle-\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap L_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\,.

The first term is lower-bounded using
Lemma

6.21
, and the second and third term are lower-bounded using
Lemma

6.23
, which together give

\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap H_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\geq-4\epsilon d\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{\phi}{4}d\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}+\frac{3}{8}\phi d\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\phi}{10}d\cdot\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\,,

where the last inequality used
\epsilon\leq 10^{-5}\phi
as per Table
1
. Hence, we conclude
x\in P_{i}
, as per
Definition

6.17
.
∎

6.2.2
Bounding the number of strongly-pulled vertices

In this section, we prove
Lemma

6.19
, which bounds the size of the storngly-pulled vertices
P_{1}\cup\dots\cup P_{k}
by
\epsilon n
. Again, before diving into the details, we discuss the intuition for the proof.

Fix
i\in[k]
, and let us consider the total force exerted in the direction of
\mu_{i}
by vertices in
H_{i}
on their neighbors across the cut
H_{i}
, i.e. the sum
\sum_{(u,v)\in E:v\in H_{i},u\notin H_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle
. We recall that
Lemma

3.5
roughly states that
\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\approx 0
for any vertex
v
, so summing this up over
v\in H_{i}
gives

\sum_{(u,v)\in E:v\in H_{i},u\notin H_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle=\sum_{v\in H_{i}}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\approx 0\,.

On the other hand, the force across
H_{i}
cannot be too small, since any strongly-pulled vertex
u
in
P_{i}
non-trivially contributes to the sum (see
Definition

6.17
). Combining these two observation, we conclude that there can only be few vertices in
P_{i}
.

To implement this strategy, we start with an intermediate upper-bound on the size of
\cup_{i\in[k]}P_{i}
in terms of the force across the cuts
H_{1},\dots,H_{k}
in direction
\mu_{i}
.

Lemma 6.24
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. Then

\left|\bigcup_{i\in[k]}P_{i}\right|\leq\frac{n}{k}\cdot\frac{20\eta}{d\phi}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,u)\in E:\\
v\in H_{i},u\in V\setminus H_{i}\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\,.

Proof.

We prove the statement by fixing
i\in[k]
and bounding the size of
P_{i}
. First, we note that
P_{i}\subseteq V\setminus H_{i}
, since
P_{i}\subseteq\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
by
Definition

6.17
while
H_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
by
Lemma

6.16
and
\mathcal{X}\cap\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)=\emptyset
by
Definition

4.5
. Also observe that for every
v\in H_{i}
and
u\in V\setminus H_{i}
one has

\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\geq 0

by
Definition

6.15
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,u)\in E:\\
v\in H_{i},u\in V\setminus H_{i}\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,u)\in E:\\
v\in H_{i},u\in P_{i}\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle

since
P_{i}\subseteq V\setminus H_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in P_{i}}\sum_{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap H_{i}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq|P_{i}|\cdot d\cdot\frac{\phi}{10}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}

by Definition
6.17

\displaystyle\geq|P_{i}|\cdot d\cdot\frac{\phi}{20\eta}\cdot\frac{k}{n}

by Remark
3.7
.

Rearranging gives the claim.
∎

Next, we give an upper-bound on the sum of the forces across the cuts
H_{1},\ldots,H_{k}
in terms of
|\mathcal{X}|
and
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{u}\|^{2}_{2}
.
Lemma

6.25
formalizes our intuition of the forces across these cuts being roughly zero.

Lemma 6.25
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. Then

\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,u)\in E:\\
v\in H_{i},u\in V\setminus H_{i}\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\leq\epsilon\cdot\frac{4\eta k}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{|\mathcal{X}|\cdot\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\,.

Proof.

We begin with an outline of the argument. First, we rewrite the left-hand side of the above as
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in H_{i}}\langle f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle
. Next,
using the fact that the cluster means almost form a basis (see
Lemma

3.10
), we apply Cauchy-Schwarz to relate the quantity
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in H_{i}}\langle f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle
to
\sum_{v\in\cup_{i}H_{i}}\|f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u}\|^{2}_{2}
. Finally, we resort to the fact that
\|f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u}\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon\|f_{v}\|_{2}
for all
v\in V
(see
Lemma

3.5
), together with
\cup_{i}H_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
(see
Lemma

6.16
), in order to upper-bound
\sum_{v\in\cup_{i}H_{i}}\|f_{u}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u}\|^{2}_{2}
by
4\epsilon^{2}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}
.

With the outline in place, we now give the detailed proof.
Let us first fix
i\in[k]
and consider the inner sum in the left-hand side.
Observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,u)\in E:\\
v\in H_{i},u\in V\setminus H_{i}\end{subarray}}(f_{v}-f_{u})=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{v\in H_{i}}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}\left(f_{v}-f_{u}\right)=\sum_{v\in H_{i}}\left(f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u}\right)\,.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,u)\in E:\\
v\in H_{i},u\in V\setminus H_{i}\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle=\sum_{v\in H_{i}}\left\langle f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u},\mu_{i}\right\rangle\,.

Taking absolute values and using that
H_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
(by Lemma
6.16
), we obtain

\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,u)\in E:\\
v\in H_{i},u\in V\setminus H_{i}\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\leq\sum_{v\in H_{i}}\left|\left\langle f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u},\mu_{i}\right\rangle\right|\leq\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\left|\left\langle f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u},\mu_{i}\right\rangle\right|\,.

(6.9)

Summing (
6.9
) over all
i\in[k]
, we get

\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,u)\in E:\\
v\in H_{i},u\in V\setminus H_{i}\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\left|\left\langle f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u},\mu_{i}\right\rangle\right|\,.

(6.10)

By Cauchy-Schwarz, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\left|\left\langle f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u},\mu_{i}\right\rangle\right|\leq\sqrt{k|\mathcal{X}|}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\left\langle f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u},\mu_{i}\right\rangle^{2}}

(6.11)

It remains to upper-bound the second factor in the right-hand side of (
6.11
)
For simplicity of notation, let
\alpha_{v}\coloneqq f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u}
for
v\in\mathcal{X}
.
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\left\langle f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u},\mu_{i}\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\left\langle\alpha_{v},\mu_{i}\right\rangle^{2}

(6.12)

\displaystyle\leq\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|\left\langle\alpha_{v},\mu_{i}\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\text{using $\eta=\frac{\max_{j}|C_{j}|}{\min_{j}|C_{j}|}\geq\frac{n}{k}\cdot\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}$}

\displaystyle=\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\alpha_{v}^{T}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right)\alpha_{v}

\displaystyle\leq\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\left(1+\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\right)\|\alpha_{v}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{by Lemma \ref{lemma:gklmsL9}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\eta k}{n}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\|\alpha_{v}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{using $\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{2}}\leq 10^{-5}$.}

By Lemma
3.5
, for all
v\in\mathcal{X}
, it holds that

\|\alpha_{v}\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq 4\epsilon^{2}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}\,,

(6.13)

so combining (
6.12
) and (
6.13
) we get

\sqrt{k|\mathcal{X}|}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\left\langle f_{v}-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{u\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(v)}f_{u},\mu_{i}\right\rangle^{2}}\leq\sqrt{k|\mathcal{X}|}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{2\eta k}{n}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}4\epsilon^{2}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\leq\epsilon\cdot\frac{4\eta k}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{|\mathcal{X}|\cdot\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}}\,.

Combining the above equation with (
6.10
) and (
6.11
) gives the lemma statement.
∎

The last thing that remains is to upper-bound
\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}
.

Lemma 6.26
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
. Then

\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}\leq 10^{5}\eta^{3}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}k
.

Proof.

We can split the sum as

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{X}:\\
\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}\leq 4\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}\end{subarray}}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{X}:\\
\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}>4\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}\end{subarray}}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}

(6.14)

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{X}|\cdot 4\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{X}:\\
\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}>4\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}\end{subarray}}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 8\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{3}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}k+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{X}:\\
\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}>4\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}\end{subarray}}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}\qquad\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:impostor_n_cross_size}.}

It remains to bound the second term.
Suppose
v\in\mathcal{X}
is such that
\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}>4\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}
.
By Remark
3.7
, we have
\|\mu_{\iota(v)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2\eta k}{n}
, so then
\|\mu_{\iota(v)}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|f_{v}\|_{2}
. This gives

\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}=4\left(\|f_{v}\|_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|f_{v}\|_{2}\right)^{2}\leq 4(\|f_{v}\|_{2}-\|\mu_{\iota(v)}\|_{2})^{2}\leq 4\|f_{v}-\mu_{\iota(v)}\|^{2}_{2}\,.

Summing over all
v\in\mathcal{X}
with
\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}>4\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}
, and applying
Lemma

3.9
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{X}:\\
\|f_{u}\|^{2}_{2}>4\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}\end{subarray}}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 4\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{X}:\\
\|f_{v}\|^{2}_{2}>4\eta\cdot\frac{k}{n}\end{subarray}}\|f_{v}-\mu_{\iota(v)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 4\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in C_{i}}\|f_{v}-\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}\leq 16\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{2}}k\,.

Combining the above inequality with (
6.14
) completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

6.20
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.20
.

Combining the results from this subsection, we obtain:

\displaystyle\left|\bigcup_{i\in[k]}P_{i}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n}{k}\cdot\frac{20\eta}{d\phi}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,u)\in E:\\
v\in H_{i},u\in V\setminus H_{i}\end{subarray}}\langle f_{v}-f_{u},\mu_{i}\rangle

by
Lemma

6.24

\displaystyle\leq 80\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi}\sqrt{n}\sqrt{|\mathcal{X}|\cdot\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}}

by
Lemma

6.25

\displaystyle\leq 10^{5}\eta^{3}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{3/2}}{\phi^{3}}n\sqrt{\sum_{v\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{v}\|_{2}^{2}}

by
Lemma

4.16

\displaystyle\leq 10^{5}\eta^{3}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{3/2}}{\phi^{3}}n\sqrt{10^{5}\eta^{3}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}k}

by
Lemma

6.26

\displaystyle\leq 10^{8}\eta^{5}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n

since
\epsilon k\leq\phi^{2}
by Table
1
.

∎

6.3
Analysis

In this section, we analyze the misclassification rate and the probability of success of
Algorithm

3
. Just like with the simpler polynomial-time algorithm described in
Section

5
, we upper bound the expected number of misclassified vertices over the randomness of
\sigma
in each of the lines of
Algorithm

3
(Lemmas
6.27
and
6.35
), and conclude with a simple applications of Markov’s inequality.

Recall from
Definition

6.10
the notion of robustly connected and robustly separated vertices. In
Section

6.1
, we show that the primitive
RobustConn
(
Algorithm

4
) correctly distinguishes robustly connected vertices (and outputs “yes”) and robustly separated vertices (and outputs “no”) with high probability. Conditioned on the correctness of
RobustConn
every vertex
u
that is misclassified in line (
9
) has to: have a wrong label
\sigma(u)
, not be robustly separated from
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, and satisfy
\tau(u)\notin\{*,\sigma(u)\}
. We think of these vertices as
false positives
since the procedure
RobustConn
, which was supposed to identify these vertices as mislabeled through the connectivity test, failed to do so. We bound the number of such vertices in
Lemma

6.27
.

Lemma 6.27

(Misclassification in line (
9
))
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
, and let
\iota,\sigma,\tau
as per
Table

2
. The expected fraction of vertices
u\in V
over the draw of
\sigma
such that
\sigma(u)
is wrong and
u
is not robustly separated (as per
Definition

6.10
) from
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
in the
(\tau(u),\sigma(u))
-cross graph, is at most
O(\epsilon\delta\log(1/\delta)/\phi^{6})
, i.e.

\E_{\sigma}\left[\left|\{u\in V:\,\tau(u)\notin\{\sigma(u),*\}\text{ and }\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)\text{ and }u\not{\perp}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\}\right|\right]\leq 2\cdot 10^{10}\eta^{5}\frac{\delta\epsilon}{\phi^{6}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot n\,.

Proof.

Denote the set that we aim to bound as
FP
(for “False Positives”), i.e.,

\textsc{FP}\coloneqq\{u\in V:\,\tau(u)\notin\{\sigma(u),*\}\text{ and }\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)\text{ and }u\not{\perp}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\}\,.

The proof of this lemma relies on one crucial observation: any crossing walk which starts in a mislabeled vertex outside of
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
must contain a “special” edge, which we will define later. Indeed, any such walk must pass through some vertex in
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
so, at the very least, it must contain an edge incident on
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
. This allows us to bound the number of crossing walks starting in a mislabeled vertex outside of
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
. At the same time, by the definition of
FP
, any vertex in
FP
must be a starting point of sufficiently many such walks, which allows us to upper bound the size of
FP
.

Now we move on to the formal proof. Recall that we use
\Lambda
to denote the set of mislabeled vertices (as per
Definition

4.12
), and note that

|\textsc{FP}\cap\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|\leq|\mathrm{\Lambda}\cap\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|,

since, by the definition of
FP
, any vertex in
FP
must be mislabeled. Therefore, the expected contribution of the vertices in
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
to
FP
is

\E_{\sigma}[|\textsc{FP}\cap\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|]\leq\delta\cdot|\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|\leq 10^{8}\eta^{5}\cdot\frac{\delta\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n\,,

(6.15)

where the last inequality uses
Lemma

6.14
.

In what follows, we bound
|\textsc{FP}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|
.

Fix
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
. We denote the subset of vertices
u\in\textsc{FP}
with
(\tau(u),\sigma(u))=(i,j)
as
\textsc{FP}_{i,j}
. Recall from
Definition

4.13
that
V_{i,j}=(\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j))\cup\mathcal{X}
and
E_{i,j}=E(V_{i,j})
. Define
V^{*}_{i,j}=\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
. We have that
\textsc{FP}_{i,j}\subseteq V^{*}_{i,j}
, since for any
u\in\mathcal{X}
we have that
\tau(u)=*
. Since all of the
V^{*}_{i,j}
are disjoint (by
Lemma

4.7
), we have that all
\textsc{FP}_{i,j}
are also disjoint. Moreover, we have that
\textsc{FP}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}=\cup_{i,j:i\neq j}(\textsc{FP}_{i,j}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*})
. We will now upper bound the size of each
\textsc{FP}_{i,j}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
.

For a fixed
\sigma
, let
p_{i,j}
denote the probability that a vertex
u\sim V
sampled uniformly at random belongs to
V_{i,j}^{*}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, is mislabeled, and that a lazy random walk of length
L
in the graph
G_{i,j}
started from
u
is a crossing walk (as per
Definition

6.7
), i.e.

p_{i,j}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{u\in V_{i,j}^{*}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}}\Pr[\text{a lazy random walk in $G_{i,j}$ of length $L$ stated from $u$ is a crossing walk}].

(6.16)

Note that
\textsc{FP}_{i,j}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\subseteq V_{i,j}^{*}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
by definition of
\textsc{FP}_{i,j}
. Furthermore, for every vertex
u\in\textsc{FP}_{i,j}
, we have that
u
is
not
robustly separated from
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, and so the probability that a sampled random walk in
G_{i,j}
of length
L
started from
u
is a crossing walk, must be at least
0.1
(by
Definition

6.10
). Hence,

\displaystyle p_{i,j}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{u\in\textsc{FP}_{i,j}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}}\Pr[\text{a lazy random walk in $G_{i,j}$ of length $L$ stated from $u$ is a crossing walk}]

(6.17)

\displaystyle\geq 0.1\frac{|\textsc{FP}_{i,j}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|}{n}.

The above inequality formally shows that any vertex in
FP
is a starting point of sufficiently many crossing walks starting in a mislabeled vertex outside of
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
. We now formally prove that any such crossing walk has to contain one of the few “special” edges. This will help us obtain an upper bound on
p_{i,j}
, which we will use to upper bound
|\textsc{FP}_{i,j}|
.

Recall the spectral impostors
\Gamma(i,j)\coloneqq\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap C_{j}
(
Definition

4.9
), and let

X_{i,j}\coloneqq\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\cup\Gamma(i,j).

We now show that every crossing walk in
G_{i,j}
which starts from a mislabeled vertex
u\notin\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, must have an edge which connects a vertex from
X_{i,j}
to some vertex outside of
X_{i,j}
. This is our notion of “special” edges:

Claim 6.28
.

Every walk in the graph
G_{i,j}
which starts from a mislabeled vertex
u\notin\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
and passes through
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, must use at least one edge
e=(v,w)
such that
v\in V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j}
and
w\in X_{i,j}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{w}
be a walk
G_{i,j}
which starts from a mislabeled vertex
u\notin\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
and passes through
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
. First, we will show that the starting point must be outside of
X_{i,j}
. Indeed, let
u\in V_{i,j}
be the starting point of
\mathbf{w}
. Then

•

By assumption,
u\notin\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
;

•

By definition of
\Gamma(i,j)
and
V_{i,j}^{*}
, every vertex in
\Gamma(i,j)\cap V^{*}_{i,j}
is correctly labeled. By assumption,
u
is mislabeled, so
u\notin\Gamma(i,j)
.

From the above two bullet points, we see that the starting vertex
u
is indeed not in
X_{i,j}
.
By assumption,
\mathbf{w}
passes through
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\subseteq X_{i,j}
, so is must use at lest one edge
e=(v,w)
with
v\in V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j}
and
w\in X_{i,j}
.

∎

Using the above claim, we can upper bound
p_{i,j}
in terms of
|E_{i,j}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})|
.

Claim 6.29
.

p_{i,j}\leq\frac{|E_{{i,j}}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})|L}{n\cdot 2d}.

Proof.

Recall that the lazy random walk on the graph
G_{i,j}
has transition
matrix
\frac{1}{2d}A_{G_{i,j}}+\frac{1}{2}I.
Equivalently, we can view this walk as a simple random walk on the graph
\widetilde{G}_{i,j}

obtained by adding an additional
d
self-loops to each vertex in
G_{i,j}
. In
\widetilde{G}_{i,j}
every vertex has degree
2d
, and at each step we pick
one of the
2d
incident edges uniformly at random. If the chosen edge is
a self-loop, the walk stays at the current vertex.

With this in mind, we can rewrite the definition (
6.16
) of
p_{i,j}
as

p_{i,j}=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{|\{\mathbf{w}:\mathbf{w}\text{ is a crossing walk in $\widetilde{G}_{i,j}$ of length $L$ starting from $V_{i,j}^{*}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}$}\}|}{(2d)^{L}}.

By
6.28
, every crossing walk
\mathbf{w}
in
G_{i,j}
starting from
V_{i,j}^{*}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, must use at least one edge from
E_{{i,j}}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})
. This gives

p_{i,j}\leq\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{|\{\mathbf{w}:\mathbf{w}\text{ is a walk in $\widetilde{G}_{i,j}$ of length $L$ and uses }E_{i,j}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})\}|}{(2d)^{L}}.

Finally, for fixed
\sigma
, the number of walks of length
L
in
\widetilde{}G_{i,j}
which use an edge in
E_{i,j}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})
is upper bounded by
|E_{{i,j}}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})|\cdot(2d)^{L-1}\cdot L
. This is because any such walk must have an edge from
E_{i,j}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})
at place
l=1,\ldots,L
. Once the edge from
E_{i,j}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})
and the position
l
are fixed, there are at most
(2d)^{L-1}
choices for the remaining steps.
∎

It remains to upper bound the quantities
|E_{{i,j}}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})|
.

Claim 6.30
.

\E_{\sigma}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]:\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}|E_{{i,j}}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})|\right]\leq 2\cdot 10^{8}\eta^{5}\cdot\frac{\delta\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}d\cdot n.

Proof.

By definition of
X_{i,j}
, for every
i\neq j
, we have

E_{i,j}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})=E_{i,j}(\Gamma(i,j),V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})\cup E_{i,j}(\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})
. We start by bounding the contribution from
E_{i,j}(\Gamma(i,j),V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})
.

Fix
i\neq j
. Observe that by definition of
X_{i,j}
, every vertex in
V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j}
has to be mislabeled. This is because

V_{i,j}\setminus(\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma(i,j))
is exactly the set of vertices that belong to
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\left(\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)\setminus C_{j}\right)
. Therefore, for every edge in
E(\Gamma(i,j),V\setminus X_{i,j})
, the probability (over the randomness of
\sigma
) that it belongs to the edge set
E_{i,j}
of
G_{i,j}
is at most
\delta
, since its end point in
V\setminus X_{i,j}
has to be mislabeled. So

\E_{\sigma}\left[|E_{i,j}(\Gamma(i,j),V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})|\right]\leq\delta\cdot|E(\Gamma(i,j),V\setminus X_{i,j})|\leq\delta\cdot d\cdot|\Gamma(i,j)|,

(6.18)

where the second inequality follows since
G
is a
d
-regular graph.

Next, we bound the contribution from
E_{i,j}(\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})
. Similarly, for every edge in
E(\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*},V\setminus X_{i,j})
the probability that it belongs to
E_{i,j}
is at most
\delta
, since its end point in
V\setminus X_{i,j}
has to be mislabeled. Therefore,

\E_{\sigma}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]:\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}|E_{{i,j}}(\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})|\right]\leq\E_{\sigma}\left[\sum_{u\in\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}}\left|\mathrm{N}_{G}(u)\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}\right|\right]\leq\delta\cdot d\cdot|\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|\,.

Combining the above with Equation (
6.18
), we get

\E_{\sigma}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]:\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}|E_{{i,j}}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})|\right]\leq\delta\cdot d\cdot|\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|+\sum_{i,j:i\neq j}\delta\cdot d\cdot|\Gamma(i,j)|\leq\delta d|\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|+\delta d|\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma|\,.

where the last inequality uses that
\{\Gamma(i,j)\}_{i\neq j}
partitions
\Gamma
(by
Remark

4.10
), and that
\Gamma
is disjoint from
\mathcal{X}
(by
Definition

4.5
and
Definition

4.9
).
Finally, since
|\Gamma\cup\mathcal{X}|\leq 2\cdot 10^{4}\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n
by
Lemma

4.16
and
|\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|\leq 10^{8}\eta^{5}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n
by
Lemma

6.14
, we get

\E_{\sigma}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]:\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}|E_{{i,j}}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})|\right]\leq 2\cdot 10^{8}\eta^{5}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n\,.

∎

Putting everything together, we now obtain an upper bound on
|\textsc{FP}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|
. By Equation (
6.17
) and
6.29
, for every fixed
\sigma
, we have

|\textsc{FP}_{i,j}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|\leq 10n\cdot p_{i,j}\cdot\leq\frac{5L}{d}|E_{i,j}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})|.

Summing over all
i\neq j
, taking the expectation over
\sigma
, and applying
6.30
, we get

\E_{\sigma}[|\textsc{FP}\setminus\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}|]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]:\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\E_{\sigma}\left[|\textsc{FP}_{i,j}|\right]\leq\frac{5L}{d}\E_{\sigma}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]:\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}|E_{{i,j}}(X_{i,j},V_{i,j}\setminus X_{i,j})|\right]\leq 10^{9}\eta^{5}\cdot\frac{\delta\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}L\cdot n.

Recalling that
L=\lceil 150/\phi^{2}\cdot{\log(1/\delta)}\rceil
(see
Table

1
), and combining with Equation (
6.15
) we get the desired result.

∎

Lemma 6.31
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
, let
\sigma
as per
Table

2
, and let
V^{*}_{i,j}=\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
. For all
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
, one has

\left|\left\{u\in\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}:u\not{\rightsquigarrow}_{G_{i,j}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\right\}\right|\leq 10\delta|\Gamma(i,j)|+5L|\Gamma(i,j)\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|+\frac{5L}{d}|E(\Gamma(i,j),V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\Gamma(i,j))|\,.

Remark 6.32
.

We note that, while many of our results related to the labeling
\sigma
hold in expectation or with high probability,
Lemma

6.31
holds deterministically for any labeling
\sigma:V\to[k]
.

Proof.

Let
T
(
T
for “true”) be the set of vertices in
V^{*}_{i,j}
with the correct label, i.e.

T=V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}=V^{*}_{i,j}\cap\Gamma(i,j)=\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}.

Consider a lazy walk
\bf{w}
starting at
u\in T
that does not reach
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
within
L
steps in
G_{i,j}
. Then,
\bf{w}
must satisfy at least one of this conditions:
\bf{w}
stays inside
T
for all
L
steps, or
\bf{w}
reaches
V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus T=V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\Gamma(i,j)
. Formally, we have

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p_{T}^{L}[G_{i,j}]}\left[\text{$\bf{w}$}\not{\sim}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\right]\leq\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p_{T}^{L}[G_{i,j}]}\left[\forall\,r\in[L],\,w_{r}\in T\right]+\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p_{T}^{L}[G_{i,j}]}\left[\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus T\text{ and }\text{$\bf{w}$}\not{\sim}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\right]\,,

(6.19)

where
\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{L}_{T}[G_{i,j}]
describes
\bf{w}
as an
L
-step lazy random walk in
G_{i,j}
starting in
T
, as per
Definition

6.4
.

To bound the first term in (
6.19
), we first define a bigger graph
H=(V,F,\ell^{\prime})
where
F
consists of the edges
E_{i,j}\cup E(T,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus T)
(recall from
Definition

4.13
that
E_{i,j}
is the set of edges of the cross graph
G_{i,j}
), and
\ell^{\prime}:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
assigns a number of self-loops
\ell^{\prime}(u)
to every
u\in V
such that
\deg_{H}(u)=d
(recall that self-loops are not in the edge set). Note that the cross graph
G_{i,j}
is a subgraph of
H
. With this notation, we have the following claim, which relates the probability of staying in a set
S
when one walks in a graph
H_{2}
, to the probability of staying
S
when one walks in a subgraph
H_{1}\subseteq H_{2}
. The proof of
6.33
is deferred to
Section

C.3
.

Claim 6.33
.

Let
d\geq 3
be an integer, let
G=(V,E)
,
H_{1}=(V,E_{1},\ell_{1})
,
H_{2},=(V,E_{2},\ell_{2})
be
d
-regular graphs such that
E_{1}\subseteq E_{2}\subseteq E
, and there exists
S\subseteq V
such that
E_{2}\setminus E_{1}\subseteq E(S,V\setminus S)
. Then, for every
t\geq 1
, it holds that

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p_{S}^{t}[H_{1}]}\left[\forall\,r\in[t],\,w_{r}\in S\right]\leq\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[H_{2}]}\left[\forall\,r\in[t],\,\,w_{r}\in S\right]+t\cdot\frac{|E_{2}\setminus E_{1}|}{2d|S|}\,.

We want to apply
6.33
with
H_{1}=G_{i,j}
,
H_{2}=H
,
t=L
and
S=T
. That way, to bound the first term in (
6.19
) it will suffice to bound the probability of reaching
T
in the graph
H
. We now verify that our setting meets the conditions of
6.33
. We have
E_{1}=E_{i,j}
and
E_{2}=F=E_{i,j}\cup E(T,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus T)
, so
E_{1}\subseteq E_{2}
and
E_{2}\setminus E_{1}=E(T,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus T)\subseteq E(T,V\setminus T)
, as required. Hence, we have

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{L}_{T}[G_{i,j}]}\left[\forall\,r\in[L],\,w_{r}\in T\right]\leq\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{L}_{T}[H]}\left[\forall\,r\in[L],\,w_{r}\in T\right]+L\cdot\frac{|E(T,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus T)|}{2d|T|}\,.

(6.20)

To bound the first term, it remains to bound
\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{L}_{T}[H]}\left[\forall\,r\in[L],\,w_{r}\in T\right]
. We use the following fact.

Claim 6.34
.

Let
d\geq 3
be an integer, let
\psi\in(0,1)
, let
H=(V,E^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
be a
d
-regular graph, and let
\emptyset\neq S\subseteq V
such that
|E^{\prime}(Q,V\setminus Q)|\geq\psi d|Q|
for all
Q\subseteq S
. Then, for every
t\geq 1
one has

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[H]}\left[\forall\,r\in[t],\,w_{r}\in S\right]\leq 2\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{\psi^{2}}{36}t\right)\,.

We defer the proof of
6.34
to
Section

C.2
, but the main idea is as follows: a graph
H
as in the lemma statement behaves like an expander locally to
S
, so a lazy random walk has a constant probability of leaving
S
at every step, resulting in an exponential decay.

We apply
6.34
to the graph
H=(V,F,\ell^{\prime})
and the set
T
.
Since
T\subseteq\Gamma(i,j)
and since
E(T,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus T)\subseteq F
by definition of
F
, we get that for all
Q\subseteq T
, it holds that
F(Q,V\setminus Q)\subseteq E(Q,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus Q)
, and so by
Lemma

4.17
, it holds that

|F(Q,V\setminus Q)|\geq|E(Q,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus Q)|\geq\frac{\phi}{2}d|Q|
. So we can apply
6.34
with
\psi=\phi/2
. Combining this with (
6.20
) and using the setting
L=\lceil\frac{150}{\phi^{2}}\log(1/\delta)\rceil
(as per
Table

1
), we have

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{L}_{T}[G_{i,j}]}\left[\forall\,r\in[L],\,w_{r}\in T\right]\leq\delta+L\cdot\frac{|E(T,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus T)|}{2d|T|}\,.

(6.21)

Then, we bound the second term in (
6.19
) and get

\displaystyle\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{L}_{T}[G_{i,j}]}\left[\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus T\text{ and }\text{$\bf{w}$}\not{\sim}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{L}_{T}[G_{i,j}]}\left[\exists r\in[L-1]:\,(w_{i},w_{i+1})\in E(T,V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus T)\right]

(6.22)

\displaystyle\leq L\cdot\frac{|E(T,V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus T)|}{2d|T|}\,,

(6.23)

and plug (
6.21
) and (
6.23
) into (
6.19
) to obtain

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{L}_{T}[G_{i,j}]}\left[\text{$\bf{w}$}\not{\sim}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\right]\leq\delta+L\cdot\frac{|E(T,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus T)|}{2d|T|}+L\cdot\frac{|E(T,V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus T)|}{2d|T|}\,.

(6.24)

By Markov’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{u\sim\mathrm{unif}(T)}\left[u\not{\rightsquigarrow}_{G_{i,j}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{u\sim\mathrm{unif}(T)}\left[\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{L}_{u}[G_{i,j}]}\left[\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\right]<0.9\right]

\displaystyle\leq 10\cdot\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{L}_{T}[G_{i,j}]}\left[\text{$\bf{w}$}\not{\sim}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\right]\,.

Recalling
T=\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}=V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}
, we plug (
6.24
) into the above bound to obtain

\displaystyle\left|\left\{u\in\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}:u\not{\rightsquigarrow}_{G_{i,j}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\right\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 10\delta|T|+\frac{10L}{2d}|E(T,\Gamma(i,j)\setminus T)|+\frac{10L}{2d}|E(T,V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus T)|\,.

Note that
T\subseteq\Gamma(i,j)
, that
V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus T=V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\Gamma(i,j)
and that
\Gamma(i,j)\setminus T\subseteq\mathrm{\Lambda}
. Hence, we conclude

\displaystyle\left|\left\{u\in\Gamma(i,j)\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}:u\not{\rightsquigarrow}_{G_{i,j}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\right\}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 10\delta|\Gamma(i,j)|+5L|\Gamma(i,j)\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|+\frac{5L}{d}|E(\Gamma(i,j),V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\Gamma(i,j))|\,.

∎

Every vertex misclassified in line (
11
) has to: be an impostor, be not robustly connected to
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, and satisfy
\tau(u)\notin\{*,\sigma(u)\}
. We think of these vertices as
false negatives
since the procedure
RobustConn
, which was supposed to identify these vertices as correctly labeled impostors through connectivity test, failed to do so. We bound the number of such vertices in
Lemma

6.35
.

Lemma 6.35

(Misclassification in line (
11
))
.

Consider the setting of
Table

1
, and let
\iota,\sigma,\tau
as per
Table

2
. The expected fraction of vertices
u\in V
over the draw of
\sigma
such that
\tau(u)\in[k]
is wrong but
\sigma(u)
is correct and
u
is not robustly connected to
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
in the
(\tau(u),\sigma(u))
-cross graph (as per
Definition

6.10
), is at most
O(\epsilon\delta\log(1/\delta)/\phi^{6})
, i.e.

\E_{\sigma}\left[\left|\{u\in V:\,\tau(u)\notin\{*,\sigma(u)\}\text{ and }\tau(u)\neq\iota(u)\text{ and }u\not{\rightsquigarrow}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}\}\right|\right]\leq 5\cdot 10^{7}\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon\delta\log(1/\delta)}{\phi^{6}}\cdot n\,.

Proof.

Denote the set that we aim to bound as
FN
(for “False Negatives”). Fix
i,j\in[k]
with

i\neq j
. We denote the subset of vertices
u\in\textsc{FN}
with
(\tau(u),\sigma(u))=(i,j)
as
\textsc{FN}_{i,j}
. Note that
\textsc{FN}_{i,j}\subseteq\Gamma(i,j)
, since for any
u\in\mathcal{X}
we have that
\tau(u)=*
. From
Remark

4.10
, all
\Gamma(i,j)
are disjoint, and therefore we have that all
\textsc{FN}_{i,j}
are also disjoint. Moreover, by the definition of
FN
, we have that
\textsc{FN}=\cup_{i,j:i\neq j}\textsc{FN}_{i,j}
.

Lemma

6.31
bounds the number of impostors which have the correct
\sigma
label and are not robustly connected to
\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
in the graph
G_{i,j}
. In other words,
Lemma

6.31
bounds precisely the size of
\textsc{FN}_{i,j}\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}
:

|\textsc{FN}_{i,j}\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}|\leq\frac{5L}{d}\cdot|E(\Gamma(i,j),V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\Gamma(i,j))|+5L\cdot|\Gamma(i,j)\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|+10\delta\cdot|\Gamma(i,j)|,

where
V^{*}_{i,j}=\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)\cap\mathrm{LabelCluster}(j)
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle|\textsc{FN}\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]:\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{5L}{d}\cdot|E(\Gamma(i,j),V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\Gamma(i,j))|+5L\cdot|\Gamma(i,j)\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|+10\delta\cdot|\Gamma(i,j)|\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]:\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}\frac{5L}{d}\cdot|E(\Gamma(i,j),V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\Gamma(i,j))|+5L\cdot|\Gamma\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|+10\delta\cdot|\Gamma|\,,

where the last equality follows from the fact that all
\Gamma(i,j)
are disjoint. Recall that
Lemma

4.16
ensures
|\Gamma|\leq 2\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n
, so

\E_{\sigma}\left[5L\cdot|\Gamma\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|+10\delta\cdot|\Gamma|\right]\leq 2\cdot 10^{5}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon\delta}{\phi^{4}}L\cdot n\,.

Now note that

\sum_{i,j:i\neq j}\frac{5L}{d}|E(\Gamma(i,j),V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\Gamma(i,j))|\leq\frac{5L}{d}|E(\Gamma,\mathrm{\Lambda}\setminus\Gamma)|\,,

because any
u\in V^{*}_{i,j}\setminus\Gamma(i,j)
, by the definition of
V^{*}_{i,j}
, is a mislabeled vertex, and because the sets
V^{*}_{i,j}
are disjoint. In the graph
G
, there are at most
d\cdot|\Gamma|
many edges with at least one endpoint in
\Gamma
. For any edge, the probability to have at least one mislabeled endpoint is bounded by
2\delta
. Therefore,

\E_{\sigma}\left[|E(\Gamma,\mathrm{\Lambda}\setminus\Gamma)|\right]\leq 2\delta\cdot d\cdot|\Gamma|\leq 4\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{d\epsilon\delta}{\phi^{4}}n\,,

so, using the setting
L=\lceil 150\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\phi^{2}}\rceil
from
Table

1
, we get

\E_{\sigma}\left[|\textsc{FN}\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}|\right]\leq 3\cdot 10^{5}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon\delta L}{\phi^{4}}n\leq 4.5\cdot 10^{7}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon\delta\log(1/\delta)}{\phi^{6}}\cdot n\,.

To finish the proof, it remains to bound the expected size of
\textsc{FN}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}
. Since
\textsc{FN}\subseteq\Gamma
, by
Lemma

4.16
we have

\E_{\sigma}[|\textsc{FN}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|]\leq 2\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon\delta}{\phi^{4}}n\,.

From here, we conclude

\E_{\sigma}[|\textsc{FN}|]\leq\E_{\sigma}[|\textsc{FN}\setminus\mathrm{\Lambda}|]+\E_{\sigma}[|\textsc{FN}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|]\leq 5\cdot 10^{7}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon\delta\log(1/\delta)}{\phi^{6}}\cdot n

as desired.
∎

We are now equipped to prove the main theorem.

Proof of
6.1
.

The algorithm that we refer to in the statement of
6.1
, prepares a data structure as follows.

1.

First, run the preprocessing of
4.21
with precision parameter
\xi=\phi^{2}/(20^{4}\eta^{2}k)
(so as to meet the requirement of
Table

1
). This produces a data structure that we can be queried with pairs
V\times V
to obtain access to our approximate inner product function
DP
.

2.

Next, run
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
using
DP
as
\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\text{apx}}
(and implementing access to it via the data structure from the previous step), and obtain vectors
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}
7
7

7

Again, in reality these are vertices
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
that indicate the embedding that should be used for the corresponding approximate cluster mean. We write
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}
for readability.
.

3.

Additionally, generate a table
r
of
\lceil 450\log n\rceil\times\lceil L\rceil
(recall the definition of
L
from
Table

1
) uniformly random integers in
[2d]
. The table
r
is then used as seed that we use to generate lazy random walks in
Algorithm

4

RobustConn
. More particularly, whenever we need to generate the
j
-th (out of
\lceil L\rceil
) step of the
i
-th (out of
\lceil 450\log n\rceil
) random walk starting from a vertex
u
, we take the
r_{i,j}
-th neighbor of the
(j-1)
-st vertex of the
i
-th walk in the graph cross graph
G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}
. If the value
r_{i,j}
is greater than
d
, then the
j
-th step of the
i
-th walk is a self-loop.

With the data structure resulting from these three steps, we answer a query
u\in V
by running on it
Algorithm

3
with
DP
as
\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\text{apx}}
and feeding it
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}
.

Denote by
\nu
the randomness involved in the preprocessing, in particular
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3}
is the randomness used in the first, second, and third step above repsectively. Note that for fixed
\sigma
,
Algorithm

3
is deterministic after the preprocessing. Then, for fixed
\nu
and
\sigma
, let
\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]
be the mapping of a vertex to cluster id output by
Algorithm

3
with this realization of the processing randomness. We want to show that with high constant probability over the realizations of
\nu
and
\sigma
, we have an
\alpha
that is wrong on at most
O(\epsilon\delta/\phi^{6}\cdot\log(1/\delta))
fraction of vertices.

First, we condition on the randomness
\nu_{1}
: by
4.21
, we know that the data structure produced by the first step does give access to a legitimate approximate inner product function
DP
as per
Definition

4.1
with high probability, so let us assume that the realization of
\nu_{1}
meets this condition.

Then, we condition on the internal randomness
\nu_{2}
of
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
: by
Lemma

4.24
, with probability
0.97
over
\nu_{2}
, one has that
\widetilde{\mu}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mu}_{k}
meet the condition of
Definition

4.4
with probability
0.999
over
\sigma
, so let us assume that the realization of
\nu_{2}
meets this condition. As we noted in the proof of
5.2
, at this point the vectors
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}
are determined up to permutation
\pi
, which is computed by
Algorithm

8
as a function of
\sigma
.

As in the proof of
5.2
, we consider an event
\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi(\sigma))
. However, here we do not want to directly relate this event to the misclassification rate of
\alpha
, since the latter also depends on the randomness
\nu_{3}
use to generate the seed for
Algorithm

4
. Hence, we define
\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi(\sigma))
to be the event such that, permuting the
k
vectors given by
Algorithm

7
with
\pi(\sigma)
, one of the following happens:

1.

the number of vertices
u\in V
such that
\tau(u)\in\{*,\sigma(u)\}
and
\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
is more than
2\cdot 10^{9}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot{\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{4}}n
;

2.

the number of vertices
u\in V
such that
\tau(u)\notin\{\sigma(u),*\}
,
\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
, and
u\not{\perp}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
is more than
2\cdot 10^{15}\eta^{5}{\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{6}}\cdot\log({1}/{\delta})\cdot n
;

3.

the number of vertices
u\in V
such that
\tau(u)\notin\{\sigma(u),*\}
,
\tau(u)\neq\iota(u)
, and
u\not{\rightsquigarrow}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
is more than
5\cdot 10^{12}\eta^{2}{\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{6}}\cdot\log({1}/{\delta})\cdot n
.

As in the proof of
5.2
, we can bound

\Pr_{\sigma}[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi(\sigma))]\leq 2\Pr_{\sigma}[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi^{*})]+10^{-3}\,,

where
\pi^{*}
denotes the correct permutation for the set of vectors produced by
Algorithm

7
.
Conditioned on the success of
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
, the approximate cluster means
(\widetilde{\mu}_{i})_{i\in[k]}
, the approximate inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
, and the approximate distance
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{apx}}
satisfy the assumptions in
Table

1
, so we are in the setting of
Table

1
. Therefore,
Lemma

4.16
,
Lemma

6.27
,
Lemma

6.35
apply, and, using Markov’s inequality, give
\Pr_{\sigma}[\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi^{*})]\leq 10^{-4}
.

Finally, we condition on
\sigma
such that
\textsc{Fail}(\sigma,\pi(\sigma))
does not occur. Now, for any
\nu_{3}
, we can make the following observations about the number of vertices misclassified by
Algorithm

3
.

1.

For this choice of
\sigma
, from the first condition in the definition of the
Fail
event, it follows that
at most
2\cdot 10^{9}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot{\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{4}}n
vertices can be misclassified in line (
4
) or line (
6
).

2.

For a vertex to be misclassified in line (
9
), we must have
\tau(u)\notin\{\sigma(u),*\}
,
\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
and
RobustConn
must return “yes”. For there to be more than
2\cdot 10^{15}\eta^{5}{\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{6}}\cdot\log({1}/{\delta})\cdot n
such vertices, it must be the case that there is a vertex
u\perp_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
for which
RobustConn
returns “yes” (since, by our choice of
\sigma
, the number of vertices
u\in V
such that
\tau(u)\notin\{\sigma(u),*\}
,
\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)
, and
u\not{\perp}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
is at most
2\cdot 10^{15}\eta^{5}{\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{6}}\cdot\log({1}/{\delta})\cdot n
).

3.

For a vertex to be misclassified in line (
11
), we must have
\tau(u)\notin\{\sigma(u),*\}
,
\tau(u)\neq\iota(u)
and
RobustConn
must return “no”. For there to be more than
5\cdot 10^{12}\eta^{2}{\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{6}}\cdot\log({1}/{\delta})\cdot n
such vertices, it must be the case that there is a vertex
u\rightsquigarrow_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
for which
RobustConn
returns “no” (since, by our choice of
\sigma
, the number of vertices
u\in V
such that
\tau(u)\notin\{\sigma(u),*\}
,
\tau(u)\neq\iota(u)
, and
u\not{\rightsquigarrow}_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
is at most
5\cdot 10^{12}\eta^{2}{\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{6}}\cdot\log({1}/{\delta})\cdot n
).

By
Lemma

6.11
, having fixed
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\sigma
as above, we have the following: for any fixed vertex
u
such that
u\rightsquigarrow_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, the probability over
\nu_{3}
that
RobustConn
outputs “no” is at most
2/n^{5}
; for any fixed vertex
u
such that
u\perp_{G_{\tau(u),\sigma(u)}}\mathcal{X}\cup\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{X})^{*}
, the probability over
\nu_{3}
that
RobustConn
outputs “yes” is at most
2/n^{5}
. By a union bound, we get that the probability over
\nu_{3}
that

2\cdot 10^{15}\eta^{5}{\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{6}}\cdot\log({1}/{\delta})\cdot n
vertices misclassified in line (
9
) or more than
5\cdot 10^{12}\eta^{2}{\delta\epsilon}/{\phi^{6}}\cdot\log({1}/{\delta})\cdot n
vertices misclassified in line (
11
), is at most
4/n^{4}
.

Combining our different rounds of conditioning, we get that with probability
0.95
over
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3}
and
\sigma
, there exists a set
B
of size
O(\epsilon\delta/\phi^{6}\cdot\log(1/\delta))
such that our data structure answers correctly all the queries for
u\in V\setminus B
.

Preproccessing time and space complexity

The preprocessing stage of
Algorithm

3
is the same as that of the Spectral Dot Oracle from
4.21
. The space complexity of
Algorithm

3
is dominated by the size of the data structure from
4.21
which is computed in the preprocessing stage.

Query time.

In lines (
4
), (
6
) we compute the approximate distance between
f_{u}
and each of the
\{\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
generated by
DP
, as per
Remark

4.3
. This requires
O(k)
calls to the function
DP
. The remainder of the query time comes from running
RobustConn
. By
Lemma

6.12
,
RobustConn
requires
O(d\cdot L\cdot k\cdot\log(n))
many calls to the function
DP
. Letting
Q
denote the time of one call to
DP
, we get that the total query time of
Algorithm

3
is

O(d\cdot L\cdot k\cdot\log(n)\cdot Q)=O(d\cdot\mathrm{poly}(k)\cdot n^{1/2+O(\epsilon/\phi^{2})}\cdot\mathrm{polylog}(n)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(1/\phi)\cdot\log(1/\delta)).

∎

7
Refining communities

In this section, we show how to round our classifier into a
(k,\widetilde{O}(\epsilon\delta),\phi)
-clusterable graph, without significantly increasing the misclassification rate. Specifically, we prove the following.

Theorem 7.1

(Refining communities)
.

Let
G,\epsilon,\delta,\phi,k,\eta
as per
Table

1
and assume that these parameters satisfy the relation
10^{16}\eta^{5}\epsilon\delta\log(1/\delta)/\phi^{9}\leq(100\eta k)^{-1}
.
Then, there is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given as input
G,\eta,\phi,\delta,k
and
\sigma
as per
Table

2
, computes a weight function
w:E\rightarrow[0,1]
on the edges of
G
such that, with probability
0.9
over the randomness of the algorithm and the draw of
\sigma
, the weighted graph
G^{\prime}=(V,E,w,\ell)
(with self-loops
\ell
to preserve
d
-regularity) admits a
(k,O({\epsilon\delta/\phi^{7}\cdot\log(1/\delta)}),\Omega({\phi^{3}}/{k}))
-clustering
C_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,C_{k}^{\prime}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}^{\prime}\triangle C_{i}|\leq O({\epsilon\delta/\phi^{7}\cdot\log(1/\delta)})n
.

We show this via a more general result: given a clusterable graph
G
and a labeling of its vertices
\alpha
, we can improve the clusterability of
G
all the way up to matching the misclassification rate of
\alpha
. From this,
7.1
follows trivially by combining with
6.1
. Hence, our goal for the rest of the section is to prove the more general result. A complete list of the objects and parameters that we use and assumptions we make on them is presented in
Table

3
.

•

G=(V,E)
— regular graph

•

d\geq 3
— degree of
G

•

n=|V|
— number of vertices in
G

•

\eta=O(1)
— upper bound on
\max_{i,j\in[k]}\frac{|C_{i}|}{|C_{j}|}

•

\epsilon,\phi\in(0,1)
— conductance parameters

•

k\geq 2
— number of communities

•

\{C_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
—
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering of
G
(Def.
1.2
)

•

\mathcal{L}
— normalized Laplacian of
G

•

v_{1},\dots v_{n}
– norm. eigenvectors of
\mathcal{L}
ordered by ascending eigenvalue

•

P=I-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}
– projection onto top
n-k
eigenvectors of
\mathcal{L}

•

\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]
— the given labeling

•

\gamma\in(0,1)
— misclassification rate of the labeling
\alpha

•

\theta=\phi^{2}/5
– threshold used in
Algorithm

5

•

\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{6}}\leq\frac{10^{-5}}{\eta^{4}},\phi^{2}\eta<10^{-3}

•

k\leq\frac{\phi^{5}}{10^{6}\epsilon^{1/2}}

•

\gamma\leq{\phi^{3}}({100\eta k})

Table 3:
Parameters and objects used in
Section

7
.

In this setting, we prove the following result.

Theorem 7.2

(Refining communities, general)
.

There is a polynomial-time algorithm (Algorithm
5
) that given
G,\phi
and
\alpha
as per
Table

3
computes a weight function
w:E\rightarrow[0,1]
on the edges of
G
such that the weighted graph
G^{\prime}=(V,E,w,\ell)
(with self-loops
\ell
to preserve
d
-regularity) admits a
(k,3\gamma/\phi,{\phi^{3}}/({480k}))
-clustering
C_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,C_{k}^{\prime}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}^{\prime}\triangle C_{i}|\leq\frac{4\gamma}{\phi}n
.

We start by showing how to obtain
7.1
from
7.2
.

Proof of
7.1
.

Let
\alpha
be the classifier given by
6.1
, and apply
7.2
to the classifier
\alpha
. By
6.1
, with probability
0.95
over the internal randomness of the algorithm and the draw of
\sigma
, the misclassification rate of
\alpha
is
O(\epsilon\delta\log(1/\delta)\frac{1}{\phi^{6}}n)
. So applying
7.2
, given the classifier
\alpha
, gives the result.
∎

Having established
7.1
, we now focus on proving
7.2
for the rest of the section. Ideally, in order to sharpen the communities, we would like to down-weight the cross edges
E_{\mathrm{cross}}
without touching the internal edges
E_{\mathrm{in}}
, as defined below.

Definition 7.3

(Internal and cross edges)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

3
. We define the set of
internal edges
of
G
, denoted
E_{\mathrm{in}}
, to be the edges with both endpoints belonging to the same cluster, that is

E_{\mathrm{in}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}E(C_{i})\,.

We define the set of
cross-edges
of
G
, denoted
E_{\mathrm{cross}}
, to be the edges whose endpoints belong to different clusters, that is

E_{\mathrm{cross}}\coloneqq E\setminus E_{\mathrm{in}}\,.

Furthermore, we let
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{cross}}
denote the normalized Laplacians of the graphs
(V,E_{\mathrm{in}},\ell)
and
(V,E_{\mathrm{cross}},\ell^{\prime})
respectively, where the self-loops
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
ensure that these graphs are
d
-regular.

The challenge is of course that we do not have access to the target clustering
C_{1},\ldots C_{k}
, and in particular, we do not know which edges belong to
E_{\mathrm{cross}}
and which belong to
E_{\mathrm{in}}
. Instead, we use the given classifier
\alpha
to identify
flagged
edges, which are edges that we believe are likely to be cross-edges.

Definition 7.4

(Flagged edges)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

3
. We say that an edge
(u,v)\in E
is
flagged
if
\alpha(u)\neq\alpha(v)
. Furthermore, we will use
F
and
E^{+}
to denote the set of flagged and non-flagged edges, that is

F\coloneqq\{e=\{u,v\}\in E:\alpha(u)\neq\alpha(v)\}\quad\text{and}\quad E^{+}\coloneqq E\setminus F\,.

The following claim shows that the flagged edges are indeed a good proxy for the cross edges
E_{\mathrm{cross}}
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Consider the setting of
Table

3
. Then

|F\triangle E_{\mathrm{cross}}|\leq d\gamma n\,.

Proof.

Let
Q\subseteq V
denote the set of vertices misclassified by
\alpha
. We have
|Q|\leq\gamma n
, by assumption. Note that the edges in
E_{\mathrm{in}}
can only be flagged if either of their endpoints is misclassified, and analogously the edges in
E^{+}
can only be crossing if either of their endpoints is misclassified, i.e.

F\triangle E_{\mathrm{cross}}\subseteq\{(u,v)\in E:u\in Q\text{ or }v\in Q\}\subseteq\mathrm{N}_{G}(Q)\,.

Hence, we get

|F\triangle E_{\mathrm{cross}}|\leq d|Q|\leq d\gamma n\,.

∎

We are now equipped to present our algorithm. The idea is to use a semidefinite program to reweight the edges so as to minimize their weight while preserving the expansion properties of the clusters.
Lemma

7.5
suggests that down-weighting the edges in
F
should be enough, so this is exactly what we do.

Algorithm 5

Refining communities via SDP

1:

Input:

G,\phi
and
\alpha
as per
Table

3

2:

Output:
weight function
x^{*}:E\rightarrow[0,1]

3:

F\leftarrow\{e=\{u,v\}\in E:\alpha(u)\neq\alpha(v)\}

\triangleright
flag edges disagreeing endpoints, see
Definition

7.4

4:

E^{+}\leftarrow E\setminus F

5:

x^{*}\leftarrow\arg\min\left\{\sum_{e\in F}x_{e}\,:\,\begin{array}[]{l}x\in[0,1]^{E},\\
x\geq\mathbbm{1}_{E^{+}},\\
P(\mathcal{L}_{x}-\theta I)P\succeq 0\end{array}\right\}
\triangleright

\mathcal{L}_{x}
as per Definition
7.7

6:

return

x^{*}

\triangleright
down-weight flagged edges subject to PSD constraint

Remark 7.6

(Computing the projection matrix
P
)
.

Our SDP
5
assumes that we have access to the projection matrix
P=I-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}
projecting onto the top
n-k
eigenvectors of
\mathcal{L}
. Such a matrix can be computed in polynomial time to precision
1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, which is sufficient for our purposes. See Appendix
A
for more details.

We now need to analyze the clusterability and spectral properties of a graph obtained by weights such as those produced by
Algorithm

5
. Hence, we now formally define how we obtain a weighted graph from a weight function
x\in[0,1]^{E}.

Definition 7.7

(
G_{x}
and
\mathcal{L}_{x}
)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

3
, and let
x:E\rightarrow[0,1]
. We let

G_{x}=(V,E,w,\ell)
be the weighted graph with
w_{e}=x(e)
for all
e\in E
and
\ell(v)=d-\sum_{(u,v)\in E}x_{e}
(note that
w_{v}\geq 0
since
x\in[0,1]^{E}
). Furthermore, we let
\mathcal{L}_{x}
be the normalized Laplacian of
G_{x}
.

We now prove that the SDP
5
has a high objective value, i.e., that it down-weights almost all of the flagged edges. This will be useful for showing that the graph
G_{x^{*}}
obtained from the optimal solution
x^{*}
has few edges across clusters. First, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 7.8
.

Consider the setting of
Table

3
. Let
v_{1},\dots v_{k}
and
v_{1}^{*},\dots v_{k}^{*}
denote the bottom
k
eigenvectors of
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}
, respectively.
Let
P=I-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}
and
P^{*}=I-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}^{*}{v_{i}^{*}}^{\top}
. Then

\|P-P^{*}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 8k^{1/2}\frac{\epsilon^{1/4}}{\phi^{1/2}}\,.

We defer the proof of
Lemma

7.8
to
Section

C.4
.

Lemma 7.9

(Existence of a good SDP solution)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

3
. Let
\mathrm{OPT}
denote the optimum value of the SDP in Algorithm
5
. Then

\mathrm{OPT}\leq d\gamma n.

Proof.

Consider the solution given by

x_{e}=\begin{cases}0\qquad\text{if $e\in F\cap E_{\mathrm{cross}}$}\\
1\qquad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

To prove the lemma, we show that this is a feasible solution with objective value at most
d\gamma n
.

Objective value.

The objective value of the solution
x
is

\sum_{e\in F}x_{e}=|F\setminus E_{\mathrm{cross}}|\leq d\gamma n\,,

where the last inequality follows by
Lemma

7.5
.

Feasibility.

We clearly have
x\in[0,1]^{E}
, by definition of
x
. Furthermore,
x_{e}=1
for all
e\in E^{+}=E\setminus F
, so the condition
x\geq\mathbbm{1}_{E^{+}}
also holds. It remains to check the constraint
P(\mathcal{L}_{x}-\theta I)P\succeq 0
. Since
x
only down-weights cross edges and leaves the internal edges
E_{\mathrm{in}}
unchanged, it follows that
\mathcal{L}_{x}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{in}}\succeq 0
. This is because
\mathcal{L}_{x}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{in}}
is the degree-
d
normalized Laplacian of the subgraph graph
(V,E_{\mathrm{cross}},w)
where the weights are given by
w(e)=x_{e}
for all
e\in E_{\mathrm{cross}}
. Hence, we have

P(\mathcal{L}_{x}-\theta I)P\succeq P(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}-\theta I)P\,.

We know that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{in}}
has
k
orthonormal eigenvectors
v_{1}^{*},\dots,v_{k}^{*}
with eigenvalue
0
. Let
P^{*}=I-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}^{*}{v_{i}^{*}}^{\top}
, and define

A=P^{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}-\theta I)P^{*}\quad\text{and}\quad B=P(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}-\theta I)P\,.

We conclude the proof by showing that
B\succeq 0
. Since
Bv_{i}=0
for
i=1,\dots,k
, the matrix
B
has at least
k
zero eigenvalues. Therefore, to rule out any negative eigenvalues, it suffices to show that
\lambda_{k+1}(B)>0
. Indeed, if
\lambda_{k+1}(B)>0
, then the
k
zero eigenvalues must be
\lambda_{1}(B)=\lambda_{2}(B)=\dots=\lambda_{k}(B)=0
(otherwise
\lambda_{i}(B)=0<\lambda_{k+1}(B)
for some
i\geq k+1
, which is impossible), and in particular
B
only has non-negative eigenvalues. Therefore, our task reduces to proving
\lambda_{k+1}(B)>0
.

First, we show that
\lambda_{k+1}(A)\geq\phi^{2}/2-\theta
. The matrix
P^{*}
projects out the
k
bottom eigenvectors
v_{1}^{*},\ldots,v_{k}^{k}
of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}
, so for any vector
x\in\mathrm{span}(\{v_{i}^{*}\}_{i=1}^{k})
, it holds that
Ax=0
, and for any vector
x\in\mathrm{span}(\{v_{i}^{*}\}_{i=1}^{k})^{\perp}
, it holds that
x^{\top}Ax=x^{\top}(\mathcal{L}^{in}-\theta I)x\geq(\lambda_{k+1}(\mathcal{L}^{in})-\theta)\|x\|^{2}_{2}\geq(\phi^{2}/2-\theta)\|x\|_{2}^{2}
. Here the last inequality is using that
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}
is the Laplacian of a
(k,0,\phi)
-clusterable graph, so by
Lemma

3.2
, it holds that
\lambda_{k+1}(\mathcal{\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}})\geq\phi^{2}/2.

So

\lambda_{k+1}(A)\geq\phi^{2}/2-\theta\,.

(7.1)

Next, we have

\displaystyle\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\|P^{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}-\theta I)P^{*}-P(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}-\theta I)P\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\|(P^{*}-P)(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}-\theta I)P^{*}+P(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}-\theta I)(P^{*}-P)\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\|P^{*}-P\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}-\theta I\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|P^{*}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|P-P^{*}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}-\theta I\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|P\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq 4\|P^{*}-P\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq 40\cdot k^{1/2}\frac{\epsilon^{1/4}}{\phi^{1/2}},

where the second inequality uses
\|\mathcal{L}-\theta I\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\max_{i}|\lambda_{i}(\mathcal{L})-\theta|\leq 2
since
\lambda_{i}(\mathcal{L})\in[0,2]
for all
i
, and the last inequality follows from
Lemma

7.8
.
By Weyl’s inequality, we have

\lambda_{k+1}(B)\geq\lambda_{k+1}(A)-\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq\phi^{2}/2-\theta-40\cdot k^{1/2}\frac{\epsilon^{1/4}}{\phi^{1/2}}>0,

where the second inequality follows by (
7.1
), and the last inequality follows by the assumption that
k\leq{\phi^{5}}/({10^{6}\epsilon^{1/2}})
.
∎

From this, we show that the optimal solution removes almost all of the weight from the cross edges
E_{\mathrm{cross}}
.

Lemma 7.10

(The optimal SDP solution puts little weight on the cross edges)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

3
, and let
x^{*}\in[0,1]^{E}
be the optimal solution to SDP (
5
). Then,
w(E_{\mathrm{cross}})=\sum_{e\in E_{\mathrm{cross}}}x^{*}_{e}\leq 2d\gamma n
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{e\in E_{\mathrm{cross}}}x^{*}_{e}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in E_{\mathrm{cross}}\setminus F}x^{*}_{e}+\sum_{e\in E_{\mathrm{cross}}\cap F}x^{*}_{e}

\displaystyle\leq|E_{\mathrm{cross}}\setminus F|+\sum_{e\in F}x^{*}_{e}

since
0\leq x^{*}_{e}\leq 1
for all
e\in E

\displaystyle\leq d\gamma n+\sum_{e\in F}x^{*}_{e}

by
Lemma

7.5

\displaystyle=d\gamma n+\mathrm{OPT}

by optimality of
x^{*}

\displaystyle\leq 2d\gamma n

\displaystyle\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:OPT_SDP_k}}.

∎

Next, we need to show that the graph
G_{x^{*}}
obtained from the optimal solution
x^{*}
of SDP
5
can be partitioned into
k
clusters, each of which has a high internal conductance. We will show a stronger statement, namely that this is true for
any
feasible solution
x
of the SDP
5
.
Before we proceed, we introduce the notion of
k
-way expansion
.

Definition 7.11

(
k
-way expansion)
.

Recall the notion of conductance from
Definition

1.1
. Define the
k
-way expansion
of the graph
G
as

\rho_{k}(G):=\min_{\text{disjoint }A_{1},\dots,A_{k}}\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\Phi_{G}(A_{i}).

Theorem 7.12

(Theorem 1.1 in
[
23
]
)
.

For a graph
G
, let
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{n}
be the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{L}
, where
\mathcal{L}
is the normalized Laplacian of
G
. We have that for every
k
,

\frac{\lambda_{k}}{2}\leq\rho_{k}(G)\,.

In order to lower bound the internal conductance of the clusters of
G_{x}
, we want to bound the
(k+1)
-way expansion
\rho_{k+1}(G_{x})
. Motivated by
7.12
, we start by showing that any feasible solution
x
of the SDP satisfies
\lambda_{k+1}(\mathcal{L}_{x})\gtrsim{\phi^{2}}
.

Lemma 7.13

(
\lambda_{k+1}(\mathcal{L}_{x})\geq\theta
)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

3
.
For all weight assignments
x:E\rightarrow[0,1]
satisfying
x\geq\mathbbm{1}_{E^{+}}
and
P(\mathcal{L}_{x}-\theta I)P\succeq 0
, it holds that

\lambda_{k+1}(\mathcal{L}_{x})\geq\theta=\frac{\phi^{2}}{5}\,.

Proof.

Let
v_{1},\dots v_{n}
denote the eigenvectors of the Laplacian
\mathcal{L}
of
G
corresponding to eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{L})\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{L})
. Let

S\coloneqq\mathrm{span}\{v_{k+1},\dots v_{n}\}
be the
n-k
dimensional subspace spanned by the top
n-k
eigenvectors of
\mathcal{L}
.
Since
Pz=z
for all
z\in S
, we get that for all for all
z\in S
it holds that

\displaystyle z^{\top}\mathcal{L}_{x}z

\displaystyle=z^{\top}P\mathcal{L}_{x}Pz

\displaystyle=z^{\top}P(\mathcal{L}_{x}-\theta I)Pz+\theta\|z\|_{2}^{2}

by adding and substracting
\theta z^{\top}PIPz=\theta\|z\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\theta\|z\|_{2}^{2}

by assumption that
P(\mathcal{L}_{x}-\theta I)P\succeq 0
.

In particular,

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in S:\\
\|z\|_{2}=1\end{subarray}}z^{\top}\mathcal{L}_{x}z\geq\theta\,.

Therefore, from the Courant-Fischer Theorem, we get that

\displaystyle\lambda_{k+1}(\mathcal{L}_{x})

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}S^{\prime}\subseteq V:\\
\dim(S^{\prime})=n-k\end{subarray}}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in S^{\prime}:\\
\|z\|_{2}=1\end{subarray}}z^{\top}\mathcal{L}_{x}z

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in S:\\
\|z\|_{2}=1\end{subarray}}z^{\top}\mathcal{L}_{x}z

\displaystyle\geq\theta.

∎

From
Lemma

7.13
and
7.12
, we can infer that for any feasible solution
x
, there
exists
a clustering
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots C_{k}^{\prime}
of
G_{x}
. However, a priori it is not clear why this partitioning would be close to the target partitioning
C_{1},\dots C_{k}
. We will argue the existence of a good refined partitioning
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots C_{k}^{\prime}
that is close to the target partitioning
C_{1},\dots C_{k}
as follows: we start by trying the target partitioning
C_{1},\dots C_{k}
. If a cluster
C_{i}
has too low expansion in the reweighted graph
G_{x}
, then we will remove the portion violating the expansion and reassign it to another cluster. The following lemma shows that after removing the largest violating portion
S\subseteq C_{i}
, the rest of the cluster,
C_{i}\setminus S
, has a good expansion.

Lemma 7.14

(Lemma 7.2 in
[
35
]
)
.

Let
G=(V,E,w)
be a graph and let
\psi\leq 1
. Let

C\subseteq V
and let
S\subset C
be a set maximizing
\mathrm{vol}(S)
among those satisfying

1.

\mathrm{vol}(S)\leq\mathrm{vol}(C)/2

2.

\frac{w(S,C\setminus S)}{\mathrm{vol}(S)}\leq\psi

If
\mathrm{vol}(S)\leq r\cdot\mathrm{vol}(C)
for
r\leq 1/3
, then

\Phi(G\{C\setminus S\})\geq\psi\cdot\frac{1-3r}{1-r}\,.

We now show that the largest set
S\subseteq C_{i}
violating the expansion of a cluster
C_{i}
has to be small. This will be useful for arguing that our clustering
C_{1}^{\prime},\ldots C_{k}^{\prime}
obtained by reassigning the violating sets will be close to the target partitioning
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
.

Lemma 7.15

(Sparse cuts are small)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

3
, and let
x\in[0,1]
be a feasible solution to SDP
5
. Let
i\in[k]
, and let
\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq C_{i}
such that
\Phi_{G_{x}\{C_{i}\}}(S)<\phi/2
and
|S|\leq|C_{i}|/2
. Then,
|S|\leq\frac{2}{d\phi}|F(C_{i})|
, where
F(C_{i})
denotes the set of edges in
F
with both endpoints in
C_{i}
.

Proof.

Any feasible solution
x
to the SDP
5
must satisfy
x\geq\mathbbm{1}_{E^{+}}
, i.e. it can only down-weight edges in
F
. In particular, we have
w(S,C_{i}\setminus S)\geq|E(S,C_{i}\setminus S)|-|F(S,C_{i}\setminus S)|
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\phi}{2}d|S|

\displaystyle>w(S,C_{i}\setminus S)

\displaystyle\geq|E(S,C_{i}\setminus S)|-|F(S,C_{i}\setminus S)|

\displaystyle\geq|E(S,C_{i}\setminus S)|-|F(C_{i})|\qquad\text{since $S\subseteq C_{i}$}

\displaystyle\geq\phi d|S|-|F(C_{i})|,

where the last inequality holds because
C_{i}
is a
\phi
-expander.
Rearranging we get the lemma.
∎

Finally, we need to show that the removed sets can be reassigned to other clusters without breaking their expansion.
The following (possibly exponential time) algorithm shows how one can achieve that, and obtain the required
(k,O(\gamma/\phi),\mathrm{poly}(\phi)/k)
-clustering
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{k}^{\prime}
of
G_{x}
.
Algorithm

6
below is based on Algorithm 2 in
[
12
]
.

Algorithm 6

Construction of
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{k}^{\prime}

1:

Input:
graph
G_{x}=(V,E,w)
, partition
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}

2:

Output:
partition
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{k}^{\prime}

3:

for

i=1
to
k

do

4:

X_{i}\leftarrow\arg\max\left\{|S|:\emptyset\neq S\subsetneq C_{i},\ \Phi_{G_{x}\{C_{i}\}}(S)<\phi/2,\ |S|\leq|C_{i}|/2\right\}

5:

C_{i}^{\prime}\leftarrow C_{i}

6:

end

for

7:

while
there exist
i\neq j\in[k]
and
S\subseteq C_{i}^{\prime}\cap\left(\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{k}X_{\ell}\right)
such that
w(S,C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)<w(S,C_{j}^{\prime})

do

8:

Update
C_{i}^{\prime}\leftarrow C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S

9:

Update
C_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow C_{j}^{\prime}\cup S

10:

end

while

In other words,
Algorithm

6
does the following: We start by selecting the largest non-expanding subset
X_{i}
from each cluster
C_{i}
. By
Lemma

7.15
, the remaining “core”
C_{i}\setminus X_{i}
is an induced expander. It remains to decide how to reassign the vertices from the sets
X_{i}
so that they don’t break the expansion of the cores. We start by trying
C^{\prime}_{1}=C_{1},\ldots,C^{\prime}_{k}=C_{k}
, and do the following “greedy” updates: while there exists a subset
S\subseteq\cup_{l=1}^{k}X_{l}
inside of a cluster
C^{\prime}_{i}
that expands more to a different cluster
C^{\prime}_{j}
than to its own, we simply move it to
C^{\prime}_{j}
.

Note that the algorithm always terminates in a finite number of steps. This is because for each iteration of the while loop, the total weight
w(\cup_{i\neq j\in[k]}E(C^{\prime}_{i},C^{\prime}_{j}))
across the partitions decreases by at least
\Delta\coloneqq\min_{e\in E:w(e)>0}w(e)
, which is a constant positive amount. Furthermore, we have two important properties.

Claim 7.16

(Fact 2.4 in
[
12
]
)
.

The output of Algorithm
6
satisfies the following:

1.

C_{i}\setminus X_{i}\subseteq C_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in[k]

2.

For every
i\in[k]
and every
S\subseteq C_{i}^{\prime}\cap(\cup_{\ell=1}^{k}X_{\ell})
, we have

w(S,C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)\geq\frac{1}{k}w(S,V\setminus S).

Moreover, the induced subgraphs
G_{x}\{C_{i}\setminus X_{i}\}
are expanders.

Lemma 7.17
.

Consider the setting of
Table

3
, let
x
be a feasible solution for SDP
5
, and let
G_{x}
be obtained from
x
as per
Definition

7.7
. Let
X_{i}\subseteq C_{i}
be the sets obtained in line
4
of Algorithm
6
on input
G_{x}
. Then
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|X_{i}|\leq 2\gamma n/\phi
and

\forall\,i\in[k],\,\,\,\Phi(G_{x}\{C_{i}\setminus X_{i}\})\geq\frac{\phi}{6}\,.

Proof.

By its the definition,
X_{i}
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

7.15
for all
i\in[k]
. Therefore, we conclude that

\sum_{l=1}^{k}|X_{l}|\leq\frac{2}{d\phi}\sum_{l=1}^{k}|F(C_{l})|.

Note that
F(C_{l})\cap E_{cross}=\emptyset
and all
F(C_{l})
are disjoint since all
C_{l}
are disjoint. By
Lemma

7.5
we have that

\sum_{l=1}^{k}|F(C_{l})|\leq|F\setminus E_{cross}|\leq d\gamma n,

which gives
\sum_{l=1}^{k}|X_{l}|\leq 2\gamma n/\phi
as desired. Since
G_{x}
is a
d
-regular graph, we have that, for any
i

\frac{\mathrm{vol}(X_{i})}{\mathrm{vol}(C_{i})}=\frac{|X_{i}|}{|C_{i}|}\leq\frac{2\eta\gamma k}{\phi},

where the last inequality follows from
|X_{i}|\leq\sum_{l=1}^{k}|X_{l}|\leq 2\gamma n/\phi
and
|C_{i}|\geq\frac{n}{\eta k}
as per
Table

3
. By
Lemma

7.14
, applied to
G=G_{x}
,
C=C_{i}
,
S=X_{i}
,
\psi=\phi/2
and
r={2\eta\gamma k}/{\phi}\leq{1}/{4}
we get that

\Phi(G_{x}\{C_{i}\setminus X_{i}\})\geq\frac{\phi}{2}\cdot\frac{1-(3/4)}{1-(1/4)}=\frac{\phi}{6}\,.

∎

We are now ready to show that any feasible solution
x
of the SDP
5
gives rise to a clustering
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots C_{k}^{\prime}
. Note that the quality of the clustering depends on how much weight
x
puts on the cross-edges.

Lemma 7.18

(Existence of a good
k
-clustering on
G_{x}
)
.

Consider the setting of
Table

3
, let
x
be a feasible solution for SDP
5
, let
G_{x}
be obtained from
x
as per
Definition

7.7
, and assume that
\lambda_{k+1}(\mathcal{L}_{x})\geq\frac{\phi^{2}}{10}
. Then, there exists a partitioning
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C^{\prime}_{k}
of
V
such that:

1.

\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C^{\prime}_{i}\triangle C_{i}|\leq\frac{4\gamma n}{\phi}
;

2.

\Phi(G\{C_{i}^{\prime}\})\geq\frac{\phi^{3}}{480k}
for
i\in[k]
;

3.

w\left(\bigcup_{i,j\in[k]:i\neq j}E(C^{\prime}_{i},C^{\prime}_{j})\right)\leq w(E_{\mathrm{cross}})+2d\gamma n/\phi
.

Proof.

Let
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{k}^{\prime}
be the output of
Algorithm

6
given the input
G_{x}
and
C_{1},\dots C_{k}
. We now show that
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{k}^{\prime}
satisfies the three conditions in the lemma statement.

Condition 1:

We have

C_{i}\triangle C_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq\cup_{\ell=1}^{k}X_{\ell}
, by construction of
C_{1}^{\prime},\dots,C_{k}^{\prime}
, and
\left|\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{k}X_{\ell}\right|\leq\frac{2\gamma n}{\phi},
by
Lemma

7.17
.
So

\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}\triangle C_{i}^{\prime}|=\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}\setminus C_{i}^{\prime}|+\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus C_{i}|\leq 2\left|\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{k}X_{\ell}\right|\leq\frac{4\gamma n}{\phi},

where the first inequality follows from the fact that the sets in
\{C_{i}\setminus C_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i\in[k]}
are pairwise disjoint, and similarly the sets in
\{C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus C_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
are pairwise disjoint.

Condition 2:

Let
R_{i}=C_{i}^{\prime}\cap(\cup_{\ell=1}^{k}X_{\ell})
be the additional vertices that got reassigned to
C_{i}^{\prime}
outside of the “core”
C_{i}\setminus X_{i}
.

Given
S\subseteq C_{i}^{\prime}
with
|S|\leq\frac{|C_{i}^{\prime}|}{2}
, let
S_{core}=S\setminus R_{i}
, and let
S_{out}=S\cap R_{i}
. Note that since
C_{i}\setminus X_{i}=C^{\prime}_{i}\setminus(\cup_{\ell=1}^{k}X_{\ell})=C^{\prime}_{i}\setminus R_{i}
, we have that
S_{core}=S\setminus R_{i}=S\cap(C_{i}\setminus X_{i})
(see
Figure

4
for an illustration).

C_{i}^{\prime}
S
S_{\text{core}}
S_{\text{out}}
C_{i}\setminus X_{i}
R_{i}

Figure 4:
The cluster
C_{i}^{\prime}
can be partitioned into the “core”
C_{i}\setminus X_{i}
and the “reassigned” vertices
R_{i}
. For
S\subseteq C_{i}^{\prime}
, we let
S_{core}=S\setminus R_{i}
and
S_{out}=S\cap R_{i}
.

We need to show that

\frac{w(S,C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)}{d|S|}\geq\frac{\phi^{4}}{2000k}.

We consider two cases.

Case 1:

|S_{core}|\geq|S_{out}|
. We have that
|S|\leq 2|S_{core}|
. From the above derivation,
(C_{i}\setminus X_{i})\setminus S_{core}=C^{\prime}_{i}\setminus(R_{i}\cup S_{core})\subseteq C^{\prime}_{i}\setminus S
, so

\displaystyle\frac{w(S,C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)}{d|S|}\geq\frac{w(S_{core},C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)}{2d|S_{core}|}\geq\frac{w(S_{core},(C_{i}\setminus X_{i})\setminus S_{core})}{2d|S_{core}|}\geq\frac{\phi}{12},

since
G_{x}\{C_{i}\setminus X_{i}\}
is a
\frac{\phi}{6}
-expander, by
Lemma

7.17
. Since
k\geq 1
and
\phi\leq 1
,
\frac{\phi}{12}\geq\frac{\phi^{3}}{480k}
, which concludes the proof.

Case 2:

|S_{core}|<|S_{out}|
. We have that
|S|\leq 2|S_{out}|
.
Then

\displaystyle\frac{w(S,C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)}{d|S|}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{w(S_{out},C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)+w(S_{core},C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)}{2d|S_{out}|}

\displaystyle\text{since $|S|\leq 2|S_{out}|$}

(7.2)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{w(S_{out},C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)+w(S_{core},(C_{i}\setminus X_{i})\setminus S_{core})}{2d|S_{out}|}

\displaystyle\text{since $(C_{i}\setminus X_{i})\setminus S_{core}\subseteq C^{\prime}_{i}\setminus S$}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{w(S_{out},C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)+(\phi/12)\cdot d|S_{core}|}{2d|S_{out}|}

\displaystyle\text{since $G_{x}\{C_{i}\setminus X_{i}\}$ is a $\phi/12$-expander by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:expanders}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{w(S_{out},C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)+(\phi/12)\cdot w(S_{core},S_{out})}{2d|S_{out}|}

\displaystyle\text{since $d|S_{core}|\geq w(S_{core},V)\geq w(S_{core},S_{out})$}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi}{24}\cdot\frac{w(S_{out},C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)+w(S_{core},S_{out})}{d|S_{out}|}

\displaystyle\text{since $\phi\leq 1$}

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi}{24}\frac{w(S_{out},C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S_{out})}{d|S_{out}|}

\displaystyle\text{since $C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S_{out}=(C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)\cup S_{core}$}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi}{24k}\frac{w(S_{out},V\setminus S_{out})}{d|S_{out}|}

\displaystyle\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:simpleprop_k}.}

To continue, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{G_{x}}(C_{\ell}\setminus X_{\ell})

\displaystyle\leq\Phi_{G}(C_{\ell}\setminus X_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\frac{|E(C_{\ell}\setminus X_{\ell},V\setminus(C_{\ell}\setminus X_{\ell}))}{d|C_{\ell}\setminus X_{\ell}|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|E(C_{\ell},V\setminus C_{\ell})|+|E(X_{\ell},V\setminus X_{\ell})|}{d\left(|C_{\ell}|-|X_{\ell}|\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon dn+d|X_{\ell}|}{d\left(|C_{\ell}|-|X_{\ell}|\right)}

by assumption that
C_{1},\dots C_{k}
is a
(k,\epsilon,\phi)
-clustering

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon n+2\gamma n/\phi}{|C_{\ell}|-|X_{\ell}|}

since
|X_{\ell}|\leq 2\gamma n/\phi
