by
Lemma

7.17

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon+2\gamma/\phi}{1-|X_{\ell}|/|C_{\ell}|}\cdot\frac{n}{|C_{\ell}|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon+2\gamma/\phi}{3/4}\cdot\frac{n}{|C_{\ell}|}

since
|X_{\ell}|\leq 2\gamma n/\phi\leq\frac{n}{4k\eta}\leq\frac{|C_{i}|}{4}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon+2\gamma/\phi}{3/4}\cdot k\eta

since
\min_{i}|C_{i}|\geq\frac{n}{k\eta}
by
Table

3

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{25}

since
\epsilon,\gamma/\phi\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{100\eta k}
by the setting of
Table

3
.

On the other hand, by the assumption that
\lambda_{k+1}(\mathcal{L}_{x})\geq\phi^{2}/10
, by
7.12
we get
\rho_{k+1}(G_{x})\geq\phi^{2}/20
.

Since
S_{out},C_{1}\setminus X_{1},\dots,C_{k}\setminus X_{k}
are
k+1
disjoint sets and since
\Phi_{G_{x}}(C_{\ell}\setminus X_{\ell})\leq\phi^{2}/25<\rho_{k+1}(G_{x})
for all
\ell\in[k]
, it must hold that
\Phi_{G_{x}}(S_{out})\geq\rho_{k+1}(G_{x})
, by Definition
7.11
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{w(S,C_{i}^{\prime}\setminus S)}{d|S|}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi}{24k}\frac{w(S_{out},V\setminus S_{out})}{d|S_{out}|}\qquad\text{by Equation \eqref{eq:SvsSout}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi}{24k}\rho_{k+1}(G_{x})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\phi^{3}}{480k}.

Condition 3:
Again, for
i\in[k]
, let
R_{i}=C_{i}^{\prime}\cap(\cup_{\ell=1}^{k}X_{\ell})
be the additional vertices that got reassigned to
C_{i}^{\prime}
outside of the “core”
C_{i}\setminus X_{i}
. In other words,
R_{i}=C^{\prime}_{i}\setminus(C_{i}\setminus X_{i})
. Then, we have

\displaystyle w(\cup_{i\neq j\in[k]}E(C^{\prime}_{i},C^{\prime}_{j}))

\displaystyle\leq w\left(\cup_{i\neq j\in[k]}E(C_{i}\setminus X_{i},C_{j}\setminus X_{j})\right)+w\left(\{e\in E:e\cap(\cup_{i\in[k]}R_{i})\neq\emptyset\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq w(E_{\mathrm{cross}})+d\left|\cup_{\ell\in[k]}X_{\ell}\right|\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{since $\cup_{\ell\in[k]}R_{\ell}\subseteq\cup_{\ell\in[k]}X_{\ell}$}

\displaystyle\leq w(E_{\mathrm{cross}})+2d\gamma n/\phi\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:expanders}.}

∎

Finally, we can combine
Lemma

7.18
above together with the fact that the optimal solution
x^{*}
to the SDP in
Algorithm

5
puts little weight on the cross edges (see
Lemma

7.15
), so show that
x^{*}
gives rise to the required refined clustering.

Proof of Theorem
7.2
.

Let
x
be the SDP solution returned by Algorithm
5
, and let
G_{x}
be the weighted graph obtained from it. By
Lemma

7.13
, we have
\lambda_{k+1}(\mathcal{L}_{x})\geq{\phi^{2}}/{10}
, and so we can apply Lemma
7.18
to
G_{x}
.
By Lemma
7.18
item (
3
), the obtained partitioning
C_{1}^{\prime},\ldots C_{k}^{\prime}
satisfies

\displaystyle w(\cup_{i\neq j\in[k]}E(C^{\prime}_{i},C^{\prime}_{j}))

\displaystyle\leq w(E_{\mathrm{cross}})+2d\gamma n/\phi

by Lemma
7.18
, item (
3
)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{OPT}+2d\gamma n/\phi

by the assumption that
x
is an optimal SDP solution

\displaystyle\leq d\gamma n+2d\gamma n/\phi

by
Lemma

7.9

\displaystyle\leq 3d\gamma n/\phi.

Furthermore, by Lemma (
7.18
) item (
2
), the partitioning
C_{1}^{\prime},\ldots C_{k}^{\prime}
satisfies
\Phi(G\{C_{i}^{\prime}\})\geq{\phi^{4}}/({2000k})
for
i\in[k]
.
So
C_{1}^{\prime},\ldots C_{k}^{\prime}
is a
(k,{3\gamma n}/\phi,{\phi^{3}}/({480k}))
-clustering. Finally, by
Lemma

7.18
item (
1
), we have
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C^{\prime}_{i}\triangle C_{i}|\leq{4\gamma n}/{\phi}
.
∎

Appendix A
Obtaining approximate cluster means

In this section, we prove two results stated in
Section

4.2
without proof: namely,
Remark

4.19
and
Lemma

4.24
.

See
4.19

Proof of
Remark

4.19
.

For matrices with orthonormal columns
Q,\widehat{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
define

\sin\Theta(Q,\widehat{Q})\coloneqq(I-QQ^{T})\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{T}.

From
[
15
, Theorem 2.5.1]
and
[
3
, Section 3]
we have

\|\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{T}-QQ^{T}\|_{2}=\|\sin\Theta(Q,\widehat{Q})\|_{2};

see also
[
11
, Lemma 1.5]
.

Let
M=I+\frac{1}{2d}A
be the lazy random walk matrix in
G
. Observe that
M
is a symmetric matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda^{M}_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda^{M}_{n}\geq 0
and, by
Lemma

3.2
,
\alpha:=\frac{\lambda^{M}_{k+1}}{\lambda^{M}_{k}}\leq\frac{1-\phi^{2}/4}{1-\epsilon}
. Let
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
be a matrix whose columns form an orthonormal basis for the top-
k
dimensional eigenspace
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
of
M
. The matrix
U_{[k]}
from
Definition

3.1
is an example of such a matrix
Q
. Note that the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{Q}
is the Gram matrix of vectors in
Q^{\top}
:

P_{\mathcal{Q}}=QQ^{T}.

Let
\Omega\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
be a standard Gaussian matrix and let
q\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\widehat{Q}
be an orthonormal basis for the range of
M^{q}\Omega
. Results on subspace iteration
[
31
,
4
,
32
]
show that there exist two matrices
\Omega_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-k\times k}
and
\Omega_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
which deterministically depend on
\Omega
and

\|\sin\Theta(Q,\widehat{Q})\|_{2}\leq\alpha^{q}\|\Omega_{2}\Omega_{1}^{-1}\|_{2}

for all possible realizations of
\Omega
. Under the randomness of
\Omega
,
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
are independent standard Gaussian matrices. Consequently, with probability
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
over the sampling of
\Omega
, we have that

\|\Omega_{1}^{-1}\|_{2}\leq O\left(\mathrm{poly}(n)\cdot\sqrt{k}\right)\text{ and }\|\Omega_{2}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{k}+\sqrt{n-k}+\sqrt{O(\log n)}.

where the first is shown in
[
33
, Theorem 3.3]
and the second in shown in
[
16
, Proposition 10.1 and 10.3]
. Hence, if
q\geq C\cdot\frac{\log(n)}{\log(1/\alpha)}
for a sufficiently large constant
C
, then

\|\widehat{Q}\widehat{Q}^{T}-QQ^{T}\|_{2}\leq 1/\mathrm{poly}(n).

It remains to note that
1/\alpha\geq\frac{1-\epsilon}{1-\phi^{2}/4}\geq 1+\phi^{2}/4
by the setting of parameters in
Table

1
, and therefore it suffices to take
q=O\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\log(1+\phi^{2}/4)}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{(1+\phi^{2}/4)\log(n)}{\phi^{2}/4}\right)
. The algorithm for finding
\widehat{Q}
consists of three steps:

•

Sample
\Omega
(takes
O(n\cdot k)
operations);

•

Compute
M^{q}\Omega
(takes
O(n^{2}\cdot k\cdot q)
operations);

•

Find an orthonormal basis of
M^{q}\Omega
(takes
O(n\cdot k^{2})
operations).

∎

Lemma

4.24
, restated below for the convenience of the reader, states that we can compute a set of approximate cluster means
\left(\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\right)_{i\in[k]}
that satisfy
Definition

4.4
.
See
4.24

The algorithm for computing the approximate cluster means
\left(\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\right)_{i\in[k]}
consists of two parts. First, in
Algorithm

7
, we obtain a set
\{\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
of approximate cluster means, where each approximate cluster mean
\widetilde{\mu}_{i}
is close to some true cluster mean
\mu_{j}
in the sense that
\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{j}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}^{2}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{1600\eta}\|\mu_{j}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}^{2}
. This part of the algorithm is very similar to Algorithm 1 in
[
34
]
. For completeness, we include our own analysis of it.

Although the cluster means output by
Algorithm

7
are indexed as
(\widetilde{\mu}_{i})_{i\in[k]}
, the ordering is arbitrary and does not necessarily correspond to the true cluster labels.
To resolve this, we use a second algorithm (described in
Algorithm

8
) to recover the correct ordering on the approximate cluster means. That is, we find the permutation
\pi
such that each
\widetilde{\mu}_{i}
is close to the cluster mean
\mu_{\pi(i)}
. That way, we can relabel the cluster means, so that they satisfy
Definition

4.4
.

We begin by presenting
Algorithm

7
, which outputs the set of approximate cluster means (up to a possible permutation).

Algorithm 7

Approximate cluster means

1:

Input:

d
-regular graph
G=(V,E)

2:

Output:
vertices
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots

\triangleright

u_{i}
defines
i
-th approximate cluster center:
\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\coloneqq f_{u_{i}}

3:

T\leftarrow

10\eta\cdot k\log k
vertices sampled i.i.d. from
\mathrm{unif}(V)

4:

H\leftarrow\emptyset

\triangleright

H
is the same-cluster graph induced by
T

5:

for all

x,y\in T

do

6:

if

\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\text{apx}}\geq 0.9\cdot\|f_{x}\|_{\text{apx}}^{2}

then

7:

H\leftarrow H\cup\{(x,y)\}

8:

end

if

9:

end

for

10:

i\leftarrow 1

11:

while

H\neq\emptyset

do

12:

Select arbitrary vertex
x
from
H

\triangleright
Select a representative of a new spectral cluster

13:

u_{i}\leftarrow x

14:

H\leftarrow H\setminus\{(x,y):y\in N_{H}(x)\}

\triangleright
Remove all vertices of this spectral cluster

15:

i\leftarrow i+1

16:

end

while

17:

return

u_{1},u_{2},\ldots

We now outline the intuition behind
Algorithm

7
. The algorithm relies on two key properties of the spectral embeddings
\{f_{v}\}_{v\in V}
: Firstly, for almost of all vertices,
f_{v}
is well-concentrated around the corresponding cluster mean
\mu_{i}
, and secondly, the cluster means
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
are nearly orthogonal. Therefore, the inner product
\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
is large when
u
and
v
belong to the same cluster, and small when they belong to different clusters.

Using this observation, the algorithm samples a set of vertices uniformly at random and constructs a similarity graph
H
by connecting pairs
u,v
if
\langle f_{u},f_{v}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
is large. We will show that with a good probability, this graph consists of
k
disjoint cliques, each corresponding to a single cluster.

To select the approximate cluster means, we simply select one representative vertex
u_{i}
from each clique and use
f_{u_{i}}
as a proxy for
\mu_{i}
. To ensure we select only one representative per cluster, the algorithm iteratively picks an arbitrary vertex from the remaining graph and removes all its neighbors before repeating.

We now analyze the algorithm more formally.

Note that our definitions of
\mathcal{X}
,
\Gamma
and
\mathrm{SpecCluster}
from
Section

4
assume that we already have the cluster means. Therefore, to analyze the algorithm, we introduce notation
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
,
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{SpecCluster}^{\prime}
to refer to the objects defined with exact cluster means instead of approximate, with different parameters and with
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
taken as
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
. Namely,

Definition A.1
.

\mathrm{SpecCluster}^{\prime}(i)\coloneqq\left\{x\in V\colon\|f_{x}-\mu_{i}\|^{2}_{2}<\frac{\phi^{2}}{1600\eta}\|\mu_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\right\},

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\coloneqq V\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i\in[k]}\mathrm{SpecCluster}^{\prime}(i)\right),

\Gamma^{\prime}(i,j)\coloneqq\mathrm{SpecCluster}^{\prime}(i)\cap C_{j}.

At least intuitively, these objects have the same properties as
\mathrm{SpecCluster},\mathcal{X},\Gamma
since the exact cluster means satisfy the properties of approximate cluster means, and changing the radius in the definition of
\mathrm{SpecCluster}
by a factor of constant does not influence the properties of
\mathrm{SpecCluster}
. We will still provide proofs whenever we use any particular properties.

With the definitions of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
,
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{SpecCluster}^{\prime}
in place, we can now show that the number of sampled vertices in
Algorithm

7
is sufficient to hit all of the clusters while avoiding the small set of “bad” vertices that do not concentrate around their cluster means.

Claim A.2
.

Let
T
be a set of
10\eta\cdot k\log k
vertices from
V
sampled uniformly at random. Then,

1.

With probability 0.99,
T
does not contain any vertices from
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}
, i.e.
\Pr[T\cap(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime})=\emptyset]\geq 0.99
.

2.

With probability 0.99,
T
contains representatives from every cluster, i.e.
\Pr[T\cap C_{i}\neq\emptyset\ \forall i]\geq 0.99
.

Proof.

We first show the statement
1
, and then we show the statement
2
.

1.

We start by bound the size
|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}|
. The argument is similar to
Lemma

4.16
. From
Definition

A.1
, for all
u\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}
, it holds that
\|f_{u}-\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\phi^{2}}{1600\eta}\|\mu_{\iota(u)}\|^{2}_{2}\geq\frac{\phi^{2}}{3200\eta^{2}}\cdot\frac{k}{n}
, where the second inequality follows by
Remark

3.7
. Summing over all
u\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}
, we get

\sum_{u\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}}\|f_{u}-\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}^{2}\geq|\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{X}^{\prime}|\cdot\frac{\phi^{2}}{3200\eta^{2}}\cdot\frac{k}{n}.

On the other hand, by
Lemma

3.9
, we have

\sum_{u\in\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\|f_{u}-\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\sum_{u\in V}\|f_{u}-\mu_{\iota(u)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{4\epsilon k}{\phi^{2}}.

Combining the above two equations, we get

|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}|\leq 10^{5}\eta^{2}\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n.

We now have

\mathbb{E}_{T}[|(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime})\cap T|]=10\eta\cdot k\log k\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}|}{n}\leq 10^{6}\eta^{3}\cdot k\log k\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}\leq 10^{-7},

where the second inequality follows from
\eta^{3}k\log k\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{6}}\leq 10^{-9}
as per
Table

1
. Therefore, with probability 0.99 by Markov’s inequality
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime})\cap T
is empty.

2.

Fix
i
. A vertex sampled from
G
uniformly at random is not from
C_{i}
with probability
1-\frac{|C_{i}|}{n}\leq 1-\frac{1}{\eta k}.

Therefore, since
T
is a set of
10\eta\cdot k\log k
vertices sampled independently uniformly at random, we get

\Pr[T\cap C_{i}=\emptyset]\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{\eta k}\right)^{|T|}=\left(1-\frac{1}{\eta k}\right)^{10\eta\cdot k\log k}\leq\frac{1}{k^{10}}.

By the union bound argument, we get

\Pr[T\cap C_{i}=\emptyset\quad\forall i]\leq k\cdot\frac{1}{k^{10}}=\frac{1}{k^{9}}\leq 0.01,

setting
k\geq 2
as per
Table

1
.
∎

Definition A.3

(Same-cluster graph)
.

Given a set of vertices
T
, we say that
H=(T,E_{H})
is the same-cluster graph induced by
T
if the set of edges
E_{H}
is exactly the set of pairs of vertices in
T
belonging to the same cluster, i.e.

E_{H}=\{(x,y)\in T\times T:\iota(x)=\iota(y)\}.

Lemma A.4

(Correctness of
Algorithm

7
)
.

With probability
0.98
, Algorithm
7
stops at
i=k+1
and returns
k
vertices
u_{1},\ldots u_{k}
such that, for some permutation
\pi^{\prime}:[k]\to[k]
, for every
i\in[k]
it holds that

\|\mu_{\pi^{\prime}(i)}-f_{u_{i}}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{\pi^{\prime}(i)}\|_{2}.

We use notation
\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\coloneqq f_{u_{i}}
and refer to
\{\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
as approximate cluster centers.

Proof.

We first show in
A.5
that conditioned on the event
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime})\cap T=\emptyset
, the graph
H
computed in
Algorithm

7
is the same-cluster graph.

Claim A.5

(
H
is the same-cluster graph induced by
T
)
.

If
(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime})\cap T=\emptyset
, then
H=\{(x,y)\in T\times T:\iota(x)=\iota(y)\}.

Proof.

The main idea is to use that for all
x,y\notin\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}
,
\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}
is very close to
\|f_{x}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}
if and only if
x
and
y
belong to the same cluster.

We start by proving upper bounds on
|\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}-\langle\mu_{\iota(x)},\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle|
and
|\|f_{x}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}|
.

We have that

\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle=\langle f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)},f_{y}-\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle+\langle f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)},\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle+\langle\mu_{\iota(x)},f_{y}-\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle+\langle\mu_{\iota(x)},\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle.

From here, by
4.21
,

\displaystyle\left|\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}-\langle\mu_{\iota(x)},\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle-\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}\right|+|\langle f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)},f_{y}-\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle|

(A.1)

\displaystyle+|\langle f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)},\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle|+|\langle f_{y}-\mu_{\iota(y)},\mu_{\iota(x)}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\xi}{n}+|\langle f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)},f_{y}-\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle|+|\langle f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)},\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle|+\ |\langle f_{y}-\mu_{\iota(y)},\mu_{\iota(x)}\rangle|.

Since
x\notin\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
, by
Definition

A.1
,
x
is spectrally close to some cluster center, and since
x\notin\Gamma^{\prime}
, by
Definition

A.1
again,
x
can only be spectrally close to its own cluster center. More formally, we have

\|f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{1600\eta}\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{800}\cdot\frac{k}{n},

where the last inequality follows since
\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{2\eta k}{n}
, by
Remark

3.7
. Similarly,
\|f_{y}-\mu_{\iota(y)}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{800}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}
. Combining this with a stronger bound
\|f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{1600\eta}\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}
above we get

|\langle f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)},f_{y}-\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle|\leq\|f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2}\cdot\|f_{y}-\mu_{\iota(y)}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{800}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}\cdot\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2},

(A.2)

and

|\langle f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)},\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle|,\ |\langle f_{y}-\mu_{\iota(y)},\mu_{\iota(x)}\rangle|\leq\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2}\cdot\|\mu_{\iota(y)}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\phi}{20}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}\cdot\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2},

(A.3)

where the last inequality follows from
\|\mu_{\iota(y)}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{2\eta k}{n}
, by
Remark

3.7
.
Substituting
Equation

A.2
and
Equation

A.3
into
Equation

A.1
, we get

\displaystyle\left|\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}-\langle\mu_{\iota(x)},\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle\right|\leq\frac{3\phi}{20}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}\cdot\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2}+\frac{\xi}{n}\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{\phi}{\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}\cdot\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\phi\cdot\sqrt{\eta}\cdot\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2},

(A.4)

where the second inequality follows by setting
\frac{\xi}{n}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{800\eta}\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}
as per
Table

1
, and the last inequality follows since
\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2}\geq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\eta}}
, by
Remark

3.7
.

Next, we have

\left|\|f_{x}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}\right|\leq\left|\|f_{x}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|f_{x}\|^{2}_{2}\right|+\left|\|f_{x}\|^{2}_{2}-\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}\right|\leq\frac{3\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\cdot\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}+\frac{\xi}{n}\leq\frac{\phi}{2\sqrt{\eta}}\cdot\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}.

(A.5)

Here, the second inequality follows from
Remark

4.3
and

\|f_{x}\|_{2}\leq\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2}+\|f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2}\leq\left(1+\frac{\phi}{10\sqrt{\eta}}\right)\cdot\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2}

by
Lemma

4.6
, and the last inequality follows by setting
\frac{\xi}{n}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{800\eta}\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}
as per
Table

1
.

If
\iota(x)=\iota(y)
then

\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}\geq\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}-\left|\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}-\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}\right|\geq(1-\phi\sqrt{\eta})\cdot\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}\geq\frac{1-\phi\sqrt{\eta}}{1+\phi/(2\sqrt{\eta})}\|f_{x}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}\geq 0.9\cdot\|f_{x}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}},

where the second inequality follows from
Equation

A.4
, the third inequality follows from
Equation

A.5
, and the last inequality follows from
\phi^{2}\eta\leq 10^{-3}
as per
Table

1
.

If
\iota(x)\neq\iota(y)
then

\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}\leq\langle\mu_{\iota(x)},\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle+\left|\langle f_{x},f_{y}\rangle_{\mathrm{apx}}-\langle\mu_{\iota(x)},\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle\right|\leq 2\phi\sqrt{\eta}\cdot\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{2\phi\sqrt{\eta}}{1-\phi/(2\sqrt{\eta})}\|f_{x}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}}\leq 0.8\cdot\|f_{x}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{apx}},

where the second inequality follows from
Equation

A.4
and

|\langle\mu_{\iota(x)},\mu_{\iota(y)}\rangle|\leq\frac{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\cdot\frac{\eta k}{n}\leq\frac{10^{-1}\phi}{\eta}\cdot\frac{k}{n}\leq 10^{-1}\phi\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|^{2}_{2},

the third inequality follows from
Equation

A.5
, and the last inequality follows from
\phi^{2}\eta\leq 10^{-3}
as per
Table

1
.

∎

By
A.5

H
is a spectral proximity graph, and by the second item of
A.2
it has exactly
k
cliques. Every time the line (
12
) is triggered, we pick a vertex
x
, declare it an approximate cluster center by setting
\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\coloneqq f_{x}
, and remove the rest of the vertices from
C_{\iota(x)}
from
H
.

Because
x\notin\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}
,
x
is spectrally close to the center of its own cluster, which by the definition means:

\|\widetilde{\mu}_{i}-\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2}=\|f_{x}-\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\phi}{40\sqrt{\eta}}\|\mu_{\iota(x)}\|_{2},

as desired.

∎

Lemma

A.4
shows that we can compute cluster means
\{\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
up to permutation. We use Algorithm
8
to recover the correct permutation.

Algorithm 8

Obtaining the permutation

1:

Input:

d
-regular graph
G=(V,E)
, labeling
\sigma
as per
Table

1
,

2:

u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\coloneqq
output of
Algorithm

7

\triangleright

\exists\pi:[k]\to[k]
:
C_{\pi(i)}
:
\widetilde{\mu}_{\pi(i)}\coloneqq f_{u_{i}}

3:

Output:
permutation
\pi:[k]\to[k]

4:

S\leftarrow 50\eta\cdot k\log k
vertices sampled independently
\sim\mathrm{unif}(V)

5:

for all

u

do

6:

S_{u}\leftarrow\{v\in S:\|f_{v}-f_{u}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}^{2}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{400\eta}\|f_{u}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}^{2}\}

\triangleright

S_{u}
are the samples that are spectrally close to
u

7:

\pi(u)\leftarrow\arg\max_{j\in[k]}\left|\{v\in S_{u}:\sigma(v)=j\}\right|

\triangleright
Predict the label
\pi(u)
by majority voting

8:

end

for

9:

return

\pi

Given the cluster means
\{\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
,
Algorithm
8
simply samples
\widetilde{O}(k)
vertices at random and uses the labels
\sigma
of the sampled vertices to determine the permutation. Specifically, for each cluster mean
\widetilde{\mu}_{i}
, we identify the sampled vertices that are spectrally close to
\widetilde{\mu}_{i}
, and do a majority vote over these vertices in to determine the label we assign to
\widetilde{\mu}_{i}
.

Finally, we prove
Lemma

4.24
, which proves the guarantee of
Algorithm

8
.

Remark A.6
.

In the proof of
Lemma

4.24
, we will refer to the spectral objects
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
,
\mathcal{X}
and
\Gamma(i,j)
, as defined in
Section

4
. Note that these objects are defined in terms of the approximate cluster means
\{\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
. Conditioned on the success of
Algorithm

7
, the spectral objects
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
,
\mathcal{X}
and
\Gamma(i,j)
exist and satisfy all of the properties from
Section

4
. Note that without knowing the correct labeling on
\{\widetilde{\mu}_{i}\}_{i\in[k]}
, we cannot verify if a given vertex belongs to a particular
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(i)
or
\Gamma(i,j)
. However, we remark that our analysis below only uses the existence of these objects, but not access to them.

Proof of
Lemma

4.24
.

Note that all of the vertices in
G
, except for
\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma
, are spectrally close to their own cluster mean. First, we would like to show that with high constant probability none of the sampled vertices in
S
belong to
\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma
, and that
S
contains sufficiently many vertices from each of the clusters. In that case, it is possible to partition
S\cup\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\}
into
k
groups of spectrally close vertices, and all of the vertices in every group belong to the same cluster. Since every group is sufficiently large, and
\sigma
errs on every vertex with bounded probability
\delta
, it suffices to declare
\pi(u)
for every vertex
u\in\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\}
to be the most common
\sigma
label in the group which
u
belongs to.

Now we state the proof more formally. Let
\pi^{*}
be the correct permutation, which exists by
Lemma

A.4
. The goal of the analysis, thus, is to show that
\pi
constructed by
Algorithm

8
coincides with
\pi^{*}
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be the event that
S\cap(\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma)=\emptyset
. From line (
6
), we have that for all
u\in\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\}
, the set
S_{u}
is defined by
S_{u}\leftarrow\{v\in S:\|f_{v}-f_{u}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}^{2}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{400\eta}\|f_{u}\|_{\mathrm{apx}}^{2}\}
.
In other words,
S_{u_{i}}
is precisely the set of vertices sampled from
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\pi^{*}(i))
, as per
Definition

4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be the event that
S_{u_{i}}\setminus\Gamma\geq 20\log k
simultaneously for all
i\in[k]
. As mentioned in the proof outline above, we would want to show that event
\bar{\mathcal{E}_{1}}\cup\bar{\mathcal{E}_{2}}
takes place with small constant probability. The randomness of each of these events is over the choice of
S
but not over
\sigma
. To bound
\Pr_{S}[\bar{\mathcal{E}_{1}}]
, note that by Markov’s inequality

\displaystyle\Pr_{S}[\bar{\mathcal{E}_{1}}]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{S}[|S\cap(\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma)|\geq 1]

\displaystyle\leq\E_{S}[|S\cap(\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma)|]

\displaystyle=|S|\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{X}\cap\Gamma|}{n}

\displaystyle\leq 50\eta k\log k\cdot 2\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}\frac{n}{n}\qquad\qquad

by Lemma
4.16

\displaystyle\leq 10^{-3}\qquad

by the assumptions in
Table

1
.

Next, we bound
\Pr_{S}[\bar{\mathcal{E}_{2}}].
Fix a
i\in[k].
We have

\displaystyle\E_{S}\left[|S_{u_{i}}\setminus\Gamma|\right]

\displaystyle=|S|\cdot\frac{|\mathrm{SpecCore}(\pi^{*}(i))|}{n}\qquad

since
\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\pi^{*}(i))\setminus\Gamma=\mathrm{SpecCore}(\pi^{*}(i))

\displaystyle\geq 50\eta k\log k\cdot\frac{|C_{\pi^{*}(i)}|-|\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma|}{n}\qquad

since
C_{\pi^{*}(i)}\setminus(\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma)\subseteq\mathrm{SpecCore}(\pi^{*}(i))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{50\eta k\log k}{n}\left(\frac{1}{\eta}\frac{n}{k}-2\cdot 10^{4}\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{\phi^{4}}n\right)\qquad

by definition of
\eta
and
Lemma

4.16

\displaystyle\geq 40\log k\qquad

by the assumptions in
Table

1
.

So by Chernoff bounds, we get

\Pr_{S}[|S_{u_{i}}\setminus\Gamma|\leq 20\log k]\leq\exp(-50\log k/12)\leq\exp(-3\log k)\leq\frac{10^{-3}}{k}.

By a union bound over all
i\in[k]
, we get
\Pr_{S}[\bar{\mathcal{E}_{2}}]\leq 10^{-3}
.

Now, condition on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
as well as on the success of
Algorithm

7
, and note that these events are independent of the labels
\sigma
. The probability of these events over internal randomness of
Algorithm

7
and
Algorithm

8
is bounded by

0.02+\Pr_{S}[\mathcal{E}_{1}]+\Pr_{S}[\mathcal{E}_{2}]\leq 0.03.

Since we are conditioning on the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, we have that

S_{u_{i}}=S_{u_{i}}\setminus\Gamma=S\cap(\mathrm{SpecCluster}(\pi^{*}(i))\setminus\Gamma)=S\cap\mathrm{SpecCore}(\pi^{*}(i)).

By the definition of
\pi
in line (
7
), we have that
\pi(i)
can be different from
\pi^{*}(i)
only if at least half of the vertices in
S_{u_{i}}
are mislabeled. Fix
i\in[k]
, and recall from
Definition

4.12
the set
\mathrm{\Lambda}
of mislabeled vertices.
Note that
|S_{u_{i}}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|
is the number of successes in the Bernoulli process with
|S_{u_{i}}|
many trials and success probability
\delta
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pr_{\sigma}\left[|S_{u_{i}}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|\geq\frac{1}{2}|S_{u_{i}}|\right]\leq\exp(-|S_{u_{i}}|/(20\delta)),

since
\delta\leq 1/100
as per
Table

1
.
Since we are conditioning on the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
we have that
|S_{u_{i}}|\geq 20\log k
, and so

\displaystyle\Pr_{\sigma}\left[|S_{u_{i}}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|\geq\frac{1}{2}|S_{u_{i}}|\right]\leq\exp(-|S_{u_{i}}|/(20\delta))\leq\exp(\log k/\delta)\leq\frac{10^{-3}}{k},

since
\delta\leq 1/100
.

By the union bound argument, with probability at east
1-10^{-3}
it holds that
|S_{u_{i}}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}|<\frac{1}{2}|S_{u_{i}}|
for all
i\in[k]
, which implies

\Pr_{\sigma}\left[\exists i\in[k]:\pi(i)\neq\pi^{*}(i)\right]\leq 10^{-3}.

From here, we get

\Pr_{\sigma}\left[\forall i\in[k]:\pi(i)=\pi^{*}(i)\right]=1-\Pr_{\sigma}\left[\exists i\in[k]:\pi(\widetilde{\mu}_{i})\neq i\right]\geq 1-10^{-3}.

Finally, if
\pi=\pi^{*}
then, by
Lemma

A.4

\|f_{u_{i}}-\mu_{\pi(i)}\|^{2}_{2}=\|f_{u_{i}}-\mu_{\pi^{*}(i)}\|^{2}_{2}\leq\frac{\phi^{2}}{1600\eta}\|\mu_{\pi^{*}(i)}\|^{2}_{2}=\frac{\phi^{2}}{1600\eta}\|\mu_{\pi(i)}\|^{2}_{2},

as desired.
∎

Appendix B
Simple classifier for
k=2
and
\delta\leq\epsilon

We now describe a more nuanced variant of the vanilla majority voting scheme introduced in
Section

2.1
which we shall call “majority-voting++”, still in the simple case of
k=2
. The key idea is to make the decision rule less brittle for vertices whose neighborhoods are dominated by the opposite cluster. The algorithm classifies a vertex
u\in V
as follows.

Algorithm 9

Majority-voting++

1:

Input:

G
,
\sigma
, and a vertex
u\in V

2:

Output:
a cluster id in
\{1,2\}

3:

if

|\{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u):\,\sigma(v)=1\}|\leq\frac{2}{3}\phi d

then

\triangleright

u
is probably in
C_{2}

4:

return

2

5:

else

if

|\{v\in\mathrm{N}_{G}(u):\,\sigma(v)=2\}|\leq\frac{2}{3}\phi d

then

\triangleright

u
is probably in
C_{1}

6:

return

1

7:

else
\triangleright
for both
i=1,2
,
u
has
>\frac{2}{3}\phi d
neighbors
v
with
\sigma(v)=i

8:

return

\sigma(u)

9:

end

if

For simplicity, we only consider the setting where
d
is bigger than a large enough constant (as a function of
\phi
) and
\delta\ll\phi^{2}
. To analyze the misclassification rate of this algorithm, we bound how many vertices in
C_{1}
can be misclassified. By symmetry, the same reasoning will apply to
C_{2}
, giving the overall misclassification rate. We consider two types of vertices in
C_{1}
: those with at least
(1-\phi/10)d
neighbors in
C_{1}
, and those with more than
\phi/10\cdot d
neighbors in
C_{2}
. We first analyze the vertices of the first kind.

Lemma B.1
.

Any vertex of
C_{1}
with at least
\left(1-\frac{\phi}{10}\right)d
neighbors in
C_{1}
is misclassified with probability
\approx\delta^{2}
.

Proof sketch.

Any such vertex
u
is misclassified only in one of these two situations:

•

in line (
4
), if at least
\left(1-2/3\cdot\phi-\phi/10\right)d=\Omega(d)
of its neighbors are wrongly labeled as
\sigma(v)=2
, which happens with probability at most
\approx\Omega(\delta)^{(1-O(\phi))d}\leq\delta^{2}
. This follows by an application of Chernoff bounds, as we only expect to see
\delta
fraction of the neighbors mislacssified and, by above, at least
(1-O(\phi))
has to be mislassified;

•

in line (
8
), if
u
is wrongly labeled as
\sigma(u)=2
, which happens with probability
\delta
, and more than
(2/3-1/10)\cdot\phi\cdot d
of its neighbors in
C_{1}
are also wrongly labeled, which happens with probability
\approx\delta^{\Omega(\phi d)}\leq\delta
, by application of Chernoff bounds. Since the two events are independent, they occur simultaneously with probability
\leq\delta^{2}
.

Overall, any vertex of this type is misclassified with probability
\delta^{2}
.
∎

Considering now the second type of vertices, we have the following.

Lemma B.2
.

Any vertex of
C_{1}
with more than
\phi/10\cdot d
neighbors in
C_{2}
, of which there at most
\approx\epsilon/\phi\cdot n
, is misclassified with probability
\approx\delta
.

Proof.

There can only be
\approx\epsilon/\phi\cdot n
of them, otherwise there would be too many edges between
C_{1},C_{2}
; second, every vertex in
C_{1}
must still have at least
\phi d
neighbors in
C_{1}
, since the singleton cuts must
\phi
-expand. Therefore, a vertex
u
of the this type is misclassified only in one of these two situations:

1.

in line
4
, if all but
2\phi/3\cdot d
of its neighbors are wrongly labeled as
\sigma(v)=2
, which happens with probability at most
\delta^{\Omega(\phi d)}\leq\delta
. This is similar to the proof of
Lemma

B.1
– note that
u
has at least
\phi d
neighbors in its own cluster, which means that at least a
1/3
fraction of its neighbors have to be mislassified, while we only expect to see
\delta
fraction of the neighbors mislassified;

2.

in line
8
, if
u
is wrongly labeled as
\sigma(u)=2
, which happens with probability
\delta
.

Overall, any vertex of this type is misclassified with probability
\delta
.
∎

Combining the failure probabilities of the two types of vertices with the fact that there are only
\approx\epsilon/\phi\cdot n
vertices of the second type, we get a misclassification rate of
\approx\delta^{2}+\epsilon\delta/\phi
.

This simple algorithm, gives the sought-after rate of
\approx\epsilon\delta
whenever
\delta\leq\epsilon
, or more generally
\delta^{\Omega(\phi d)}\leq\epsilon\delta
. Even if this already provides a partial answer to our initial question, we do not want to make any such assumptions on
\delta
and
d
. A natural attempt to artificially increase the degree, in order to bring
\delta^{\Omega(\phi d)}
below
\epsilon\delta
, is the following: power the graph
G
for
t
times, until
\delta^{\Omega(\phi d^{t})}\leq\epsilon\delta
, and apply the algorithm above to the powered graph
G^{t}
. However, there is an issue with this plan: we do not know how to argue that
G^{t}
remains
(2,\epsilon,\phi)
-clusterable with respect to
C_{1},C_{2}
. To illustrate this, consider the following example graph, where
d\geq 1/\phi
: the cluster
C_{1}
contains two sets
S,T
of size
|S|=\epsilon/10\cdot n
and
|T|=\phi d\cdot|S|
. Connect the sets
S
and
T
so that
\mathrm{N}_{C_{1}}(S)=T
, so each vertex in
S
has
\phi d
neighbors in
C_{1}
. Additionally, connect the set
T
to the rest of
C_{1}
so that every vertex in
T
has
2\phi d
neighbors in
C_{1}\setminus(S\cup T)
. Every other vertex in
C_{1}
has all but
1
of its neighbors in
C_{1}\setminus(S\cup T)
, and
G[C_{1}\setminus(S\cup T)]
induces a
2\phi
-expander. One can see that this construction does not violate the requirement that
G
is clusterable. On the other hand, the number of vertices in
C_{1}
in the two-hop neighborhood of
u\in S
is only
\phi d+2\phi d\cdot\phi d\leq 3\phi^{2}d^{2}
. This means that we would need to change the thresholds in the algorithm to be on the order of
\phi^{2}d^{2}
, while we would have liked to use
\phi d^{2}
. Hence, the misclassification rate would be at least suffer the term
\delta^{\Omega((\phi d)^{2})}
: this is much worse than the loss
\delta^{\Omega(\phi d^{2})}
we were wishing for.

Appendix C
Deferred proofs

C.1
Proof of
5.6

Recall that
5.6
is a technical tool for proving
Lemma

5.4
. Before we revisit the statement of the Claim, recall notation used in the proof of
Lemma

5.4
. Let
S_{1}
be the set of neighbors of cross and impostor vertices, i.e.

S_{1}=\mathrm{N}_{G}(\mathcal{X}\cup\Gamma)\,.

For a fixed labeling
\alpha
, for all
i\in[n]
we let
S_{i+1}(\alpha)
be the set of neighbors in
G
of the vertices mislabeled vertices in
S_{i}(\alpha)
that are not in any previously defined sets, i.e.

S_{i+1}(\alpha)=\mathrm{N}_{G}(\{u\in S_{i}(\alpha):\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\})\setminus\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}S_{j}(\alpha)\right)\,.

Note that the definition of the set
S_{1}
does not depend on the labeling
\alpha
but, for the sake of uniformity of notation, we will still write
S_{1}(\alpha)
. For convenience, we let
\widehat{S}_{i}(\alpha)
be the set of vertices in
S_{i}(\alpha)
with a wrong label, i.e.

\widehat{S}_{i}(\alpha)=\{u\in S_{i}(\alpha):\,\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}\,,

and let
{S}_{\leq i}(\alpha)
be the set of vertices in any of
S_{1}(\alpha),\dots,S_{i}(\alpha)
, i.e.

S_{\leq i}(\alpha)=\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}S_{j}(\alpha)\,,

so we can rewrite

S_{i+1}(\alpha)=\mathrm{N}_{G}(\widehat{S}_{i}(\alpha))\setminus S_{\leq i}(\alpha)\,.

Similarly, we also let
\widehat{S}_{\leq i}(\alpha)
be the set of vertices in any of
\widehat{S}_{1}(\alpha),\dots,\widehat{S}_{i}(\alpha)
, i.e.

\widehat{S}_{\leq i}(\alpha)=\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}\widehat{S}_{j}(\alpha)\,,

See
5.6

Proof.

Consider
i
fixed everywhere in this proof. Let

\mathcal{T}=\{\mathbf{T}=(T,\widehat{T})\in((2^{V})^{i})^{2}:\forall\,j\in[i],\,\widehat{T}_{j}\subseteq T_{j}\text{ and }\forall\,j\in[i-1],\,T_{j+1}=\mathrm{N}_{G}(\widehat{T}_{j})\setminus(\cup_{h=1}^{j}T_{h})\}.

In other words, any
\mathbf{T}
is a pair of two sequences
T=T_{1},\ldots,T_{i}
,
\widehat{T}=\widehat{T}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{T}_{i}
, where for each
j\in[i]
both
T_{j}
and
\widehat{T}_{j}
are subsets of
V
. The sequence
T
is defined by its first element
T_{1}
and the sequence
\widehat{T}
.
\mathcal{T}
is the set of all possible pairs of sequences
\mathbf{T}
. Note that for any
\alpha
,
((S_{j}(\alpha))_{j=1}^{i},(\widehat{S}_{j}(\alpha))_{j=1}^{i})\in\mathcal{T}
.
\mathcal{T}
is thus designed to contain all
((S_{j}(\alpha))_{j=1}^{i},(\widehat{S}_{j}(\alpha))_{j=1}^{i})
for all possible realizations of
\sigma
.

Define
\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})=\mathrm{N}_{G}(\widehat{T}_{i})\setminus\cup_{j=1}^{i}T_{j}
for all
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}
. Note that
\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})
is defined by
T=T_{1},\ldots,T_{i}
and
\widehat{T}=\widehat{T}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{T}_{i}
in the same way as
T_{j+1}
is defined by
T_{1},\ldots,T_{j}
and
\widehat{T}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{T}_{j}
for any
j<i
, so it’s meant to be the
next
object in the sequence
T
. In particular, if
\mathbf{T}=((S_{j}(\alpha))_{j=1}^{i},(\widehat{S}_{j}(\alpha))_{j=1}^{i})
then
\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})=S_{i+1}(\alpha)
.

For any
\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]
, let
\text{seq}(\alpha,i)=((S_{j}(\alpha))_{j=1}^{i},(\widehat{S}_{j}(\alpha))_{j=1}^{i})
. Also, for any
Q\subseteq V
, and labelings
\gamma:Q\rightarrow[k]
,
\beta:V\setminus Q\rightarrow[k]
, we denote by
\gamma\|\beta:V\rightarrow[k]
the labeling that assigns
\gamma(u)
to each
u\in Q
and assigns
\beta(u)
to each
u\in V\setminus Q
. With this notation in place, we rewrite

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma}\left[\left|\widehat{S}_{i+1}(\sigma)\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]}\left|\widehat{S}_{i+1}(\alpha)\right|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\alpha]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]\text{ s.t.}\\
\text{seq}(\alpha,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}|\widehat{S}_{i+1}(\alpha)|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\alpha]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k],\\
\beta:V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]\\
\text{s.t. }\text{seq}(\gamma\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}\left|\{u\in\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}):\gamma(u)\neq\iota(u)\}\right|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\gamma\|\beta]\,.

In more details, in the last line we decouple the labeling
\alpha
into
\gamma
and
\beta
. As mentioned before, if
\text{seq}(\alpha,i)=\mathbf{T}
then
S_{i+1}(\alpha)=\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})
. By the definition of
\widehat{S}_{i+1}(\alpha)
we have that
\widehat{S}_{i+1}(\alpha)\subseteq S_{i+1}(\alpha)
is the set of all vertices in
S_{i+1}(\alpha)
misclassified by
\alpha
. Since
\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})
is the domain of
\gamma
, we have that
\widehat{S}_{i+1}(\alpha)=\{u\in\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}):\gamma(u)\neq\iota(u)\}
.

Note that for any
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}
and
\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]
,
\beta:V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]
one has

\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\gamma\|\beta]=\Pr_{\sigma(\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\gamma]\cdot\Pr_{\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\beta]\,,

(C.1)

since random variables
\sigma(u)
and
\sigma(v)
are independent for any
u\neq v
. Next, we argue that for any
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}
and
\beta:V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]
, one has that

\text{either}\quad\forall\,\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k],\,\text{seq}(\gamma\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}\quad\text{or}\quad\forall\,\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k],\,\text{seq}(\gamma\|\beta,i)\neq\mathbf{T}\,.

(C.2)

To show this, we prove that if there exists
\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]
such that
\text{seq}(\gamma\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}
, then one has
\text{seq}(\gamma^{\prime}\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}
for all
\gamma^{\prime}:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]
. To this end, we fix
\gamma^{\prime}:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]
and argue by induction on
j\in[i]
that for all
h\in[j]
one has
S_{h}(\gamma^{\prime}\|\beta)=S_{h}(\gamma\|\beta)
and
\widehat{S}_{h}(\gamma^{\prime}\|\beta)=\widehat{S}_{h}(\gamma\|\beta)
. Let
\alpha=\gamma\|\beta
and
\alpha^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime}\|\beta
for brevity. For the base case
j=1
, we have

S_{1}(\alpha^{\prime})=S_{1}=S_{1}(\alpha)

and

\widehat{S}_{1}(\alpha^{\prime})=\{u\in S_{1}:\alpha^{\prime}(u)\neq\iota(u)\}=\{u\in T_{1}:\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}=\{u\in S_{1}:\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}=\widehat{S}_{1}(\alpha)\,,

since
T_{1}=S_{1}
,
T_{1}\cap\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})=\emptyset
, and therefore
T_{1}
lies entirely in the domain of
\beta
. For the inductive step, let
j\in[i-1]
and assume that for all
h\in[j]
one has
S_{h}(\alpha^{\prime})=S_{h}(\alpha)
and
\widehat{S}_{h}(\alpha^{\prime})=\widehat{S}_{h}(\alpha)
. Then, we have

S_{j+1}(\alpha^{\prime})=\mathrm{N}_{G}(\widehat{S}_{j}(\alpha^{\prime}))\setminus\left(\bigcup_{h=1}^{j}{S}_{h}(\alpha^{\prime})\right)=\mathrm{N}_{G}(\widehat{S}_{j}(\alpha))\setminus\left(\bigcup_{h=1}^{j}{S}_{h}(\alpha)\right)=S_{j+1}(\alpha)\,,

and

\widehat{S}_{j+1}(\alpha^{\prime})=\{u\in{S}_{j+1}(\alpha^{\prime}):\alpha^{\prime}(u)\neq\iota(u)\}=\{u\in T_{j+1}:\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}=\{u\in{S}_{j+1}(\alpha):\alpha(u)\neq\iota(u)\}=\widehat{S}_{j+1}(\alpha)\,,

since
{S}_{j+1}(\alpha)={S}_{j+1}(\alpha)=T_{j+1}
,
T_{j+1}\cap\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})=\emptyset
, and therefore
T_{j+1}
lies entirely in the domain of
\beta
. The inductive proof is thus concluded.

For a fixed
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}
, we use (
C.2
) to write

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k],\\
\beta:V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]\\
\text{s.t.}\\
\text{seq}(\gamma\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}|\{u\in\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}):\gamma(u)\neq\iota(u)\}|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\gamma\|\beta]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta:V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]\\
\text{s.t.}\\
\forall\,\gamma^{\prime}:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k],\,\text{seq}(\gamma^{\prime}\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]}|\{u\in\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}):\gamma(u)\neq\iota(u)\}|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\gamma\|\beta]\,.

Now, for any
\beta:V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]
such that
\text{seq}(\gamma^{\prime}\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}
for all
\gamma^{\prime}:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]
, we use (
C.1
) to write

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]}|\{u\in\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}):\gamma(u)\neq\iota(u)\}|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\gamma\|\beta]

(C.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pr_{\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\beta]\sum_{\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]}|\{u\in\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}):\gamma(u)\neq\iota(u)\}|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma(\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\gamma]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pr_{\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\beta]\cdot\E_{\sigma(\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}\left[|\{u\in\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}):\sigma(u)\neq\iota(u)\}|\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pr_{\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\beta]\cdot\delta\cdot|\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\delta d\cdot|\widehat{T}_{i}|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\beta],

where the last inequality follows from the fact that
|\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})|\leq|\mathrm{N}_{G}(\widehat{T}_{i})|\leq d\cdot|\widehat{T}_{i}|
. From
Equation

C.1
and
Equation

C.2
we have that for all
\mathbf{T}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta:V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]\\
\text{s.t.}\forall\gamma\\
\text{seq}(\gamma\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}\Pr_{\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\beta]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]\text{ s.t.}\\
\text{seq}(\alpha,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\alpha]

(C.4)

since

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta:V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]\\
\text{s.t.}\forall\gamma\\
\text{seq}(\gamma\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}\Pr_{\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\beta]=\qquad\qquad\text{since}\sum_{\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]}\Pr_{\sigma(\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\gamma]=1

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta:V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]\\
\text{s.t.}\forall\gamma\\
\text{seq}(\gamma\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]}\Pr_{\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\beta]\cdot\Pr_{\sigma(\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))}[\sigma(\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}))=\gamma]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]\text{ s.t.}\\
\text{seq}(\alpha,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\alpha]

By summing the inequality
C.3
over all
\beta
such that for all
\gamma

\text{seq}(\gamma\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}
, and applying
Equation

C.4
we get that for any
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k],\\
\beta:V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]\\
\text{s.t.}\\
\text{seq}(\gamma\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}|\{u\in\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}):\gamma(u)\neq\iota(u)\}|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\gamma\|\beta]\leq\delta d\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]\text{ s.t.}\\
\text{seq}(\alpha,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}|\widehat{T}_{i}|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\alpha]\,,

which implies

\displaystyle\E_{\sigma}\left[|\widehat{S}_{i+1}(\sigma)|\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma:\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k],\\
\beta:V\setminus\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow[k]\\
\text{s.t.}\\
\text{seq}(\gamma\|\beta,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}|\{u\in\text{nxt}(\mathbf{T}):\gamma(u)\neq\iota(u)\}|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\gamma\|\beta]

\displaystyle\leq\delta d\sum_{\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]\text{ s.t.}\\
\text{seq}(\alpha,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}|\widehat{T}_{i}|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\alpha]

\displaystyle=\delta d\sum_{\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{T}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]\text{ s.t.}\\
\text{seq}(\alpha,i)=\mathbf{T}\end{subarray}}|\widehat{S}_{i}(\alpha)|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\alpha]

\displaystyle=\delta d\sum_{\alpha:V\rightarrow[k]}|\widehat{S}_{i}(\alpha)|\cdot\Pr_{\sigma}[\sigma=\alpha]

\displaystyle=\delta d\cdot\E_{\sigma}\left[|\widehat{S}_{i}(\sigma)|\right]\,.

∎

C.2
Proof of
6.34

See
6.34

In the above,
\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p_{S}^{t}[H]
describes
\bf{w}
as a
t
-step lazy random walk in
H
starting in
S
, as per
Definition

6.4
. We first describe the high-level idea. Imagine that
H[S]
is a subgraph of a sufficiently big expander
\widehat{H}
. Then, for
t
large enough, the endpoint of a lazy random walk of length
t
starting in some vertex
u\in S
, is equally likely to end at any vertex in
\widehat{H}
, since random walks mix rapidly on expanders. In particular, the probability that the endpoint is in
S
is small.

Proof.

Recall that
H
is a graph with self-loops, whose edge set excluding the self-loops is denoted by
E^{\prime}
, and whose self-loops are reflected by the function
\ell^{\prime}:V\to\mathbb{N}
.

We construct a weighted graph
\widehat{H}=(U,\widehat{E},\widehat{\ell},\omega)
defined as follows: its vertex set is
U=S\cup T
, where
T
is a set of dummy vertices disjoint from
S
of size
|T|=20\lceil\exp(\psi^{2}t/36)\rceil\cdot|S|
; its edge set
\widehat{E}
contains all the edges in
E^{\prime}(S)
and an edge
(u,v)
for every
u,v\in U
such that at least one of
u,v
is in
T
; its self-loops are copied over
S
and set to
0
everywhere else, i.e.
\widehat{\ell}(u)=\ell(u)
for all
u\in S
and
\widehat{\ell}(u)=0
for all
u\in T
; its weight function
\omega:F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is

\omega(u,v)=\begin{cases}1\,,&\quad\text{if }(u,v)\in E^{\prime}(S)\\
\frac{1}{|T|}|E^{\prime}(\{u\},V\setminus S)|\,,&\quad\text{if }u\in S,v\in T\\
\frac{1}{|T|-1}\left(d-\frac{1}{|T|}|E^{\prime}(S,V\setminus S)|\right)\,,&\quad\text{if }u,v\in T\\
\end{cases}\,.

First, one can observe the following.

Claim C.1
.

The graph
\widehat{H}
is
d
-regular, i.e
\widehat{\ell}(u)+\sum_{v:u\neq v\in U}\omega(u,v)=d
for all
u\in U
.

Proof.

For every
u\in S
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\ell}(u)+\sum_{v:u\neq v\in U}\omega(u,v)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\ell}(u)+\sum_{v:u\neq v\in S}\omega(u,v)+\sum_{v\in T}\omega(u,v)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\ell}(u)+\left(|E^{\prime}(\{u\},S\setminus\{u\})|+|E^{\prime}(\{u\},V\setminus S)|\right)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\ell}(u)+\left(d-\ell(u)\right)\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{since $H$ is $d$-regular}

\displaystyle=d\,.

For every dummy vertex
u\in T
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\ell}(u)+\sum_{v:u\neq v\in U}\omega(u,v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in S}\omega(u,v)+\sum_{v:u\neq v\in T}\omega(u,v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in S}\frac{1}{|T|}|E^{\prime}(\{v\},V\setminus S)|+d-\frac{1}{|T|}|E^{\prime}(S,V\setminus S)|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|T|}|E^{\prime}(S,V\setminus S)|+d-\frac{1}{|T|}|E^{\prime}(S,V\setminus S)|

\displaystyle=d\,.

∎

C.1
allows us to conveniently talk about the distribution of random walks in
\widehat{H}
, and in particular allows us to show the following.

Claim C.2
.

One has

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[H]}\left[\forall\,r\in[t],\,w_{r}\in S\right]=\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[\widehat{H}]}\left[\forall\,r\in[t],\,w_{r}\in S\right]\,.

Proof.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{V\times V}
and
\widehat{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{U\times U}
be the adjacency matrix of
H
and
\widehat{H}
respectively, and define the corresponding lazy random walk transition matrices as
M=\frac{1}{2}I+\frac{1}{2d}A
and
\widehat{M}=\frac{1}{2}I+\frac{1}{2d}\widehat{A}
(where we used that
\widehat{H}
is
d
-regular, by
C.1
). Observe that
M_{u,v}=\widehat{M}_{u,v}
for all
u,v\in S
. We also let
x
and
\widehat{x}
be
\mathbbm{1}_{S}
in
\mathbb{R}^{V}
and
\mathbb{R}^{U}
respectively, and define
P=\text{diag}(x)
,
\widehat{P}=\text{diag}(\widehat{x})
. Then, we have

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[H]}\left[\forall\,r\in[t],\,w_{r}\in S\right]=x^{\top}(PM)^{t}x\frac{1}{|S|}\,,

and

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[\widehat{H}]}\left[\forall\,r\in[t],\,w_{r}\in S\right]=\widehat{x}^{\top}(\widehat{P}\widehat{M})^{t}\widehat{x}\frac{1}{|S|}\,.

To prove the statement, it then suffices to show

\mathrm{supp}((PM)^{t}x),\ \mathrm{supp}((\widehat{P}\widehat{M})^{t}\widehat{x})\subseteq S\quad\text{ and }\quad\forall\,u\in S,\,\left((PM)^{t}x\right)_{u}=\left((\widehat{P}\widehat{M})^{t}\widehat{x}\right)_{u}\,.

(C.5)

We argue this by induction on
t
. For the base case
t=1
, one has

\left(PMx\right)_{u}=\sum_{v\in S}M_{u,v}=\sum_{v\in S}\widehat{M}_{u,v}=\left(\widehat{P}\widehat{M}\widehat{x}\right)_{u}

for all
u\in S
. Moreover,
\mathrm{supp}(Pz)\subseteq S
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
and
\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{P}\widehat{z})\subseteq S
for all
\widehat{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{U}
, so one in particular has
\mathrm{supp}(PMx),\ \mathrm{supp}(\widehat{P}\widehat{M}\widehat{x})\subseteq S
. For the inductive step, let us assume that (
C.5
) holds for
t
. Again, since
\mathrm{supp}(Pz),\ \mathrm{supp}(\widehat{P}\widehat{z})\subseteq S
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{V},\widehat{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{U}
, we have
\mathrm{supp}((PM)^{t+1}x)
,
\mathrm{supp}((\widehat{P}\widehat{M})^{t+1}\widehat{x})\subseteq S
. Then, we are left with task of showing the second condition, so we fix
u\in S
and write

\left((PM)^{t+1}x\right)_{u}=\left(PM(PM)^{t}x\right)_{u}=\sum_{v\in S}M_{u,v}\left((PM)^{t}x\right)_{v}\,,

where the last equality uses that
\mathrm{supp}((PM)^{t}x)\subseteq S
by the inductive hypothesis. Since by the inductive hypothesis we also know
\left((PM)^{t}x\right)_{v}=\left((\widehat{P}\widehat{M})^{t}\widehat{x}\right)_{v}
for all
v\in S
, we continue from above and get

\left((PM)^{t+1}x\right)_{u}=\sum_{v\in S}M_{u,v}\left((PM)^{t}x\right)_{v}=\sum_{v\in S}\widehat{M}_{u,v}\left((\widehat{P}\widehat{M})^{t}\widehat{x}\right)_{v}\,.

Now, from the inductive hypothesis we also know
\mathrm{supp}((\widehat{P}\widehat{M})^{t}\widehat{x})\subseteq S
, so we can continue from above to conclude

\displaystyle\left((PM)^{t+1}x\right)_{u}=\sum_{v\in S}\widehat{M}_{u,v}\left((\widehat{P}\widehat{M})^{t}\widehat{x}\right)_{v}=\left(\widehat{P}\widehat{M}(\widehat{P}\widehat{M})^{t}\widehat{x}\right)_{u}=\left((\widehat{P}\widehat{M})^{t+1}\widehat{x}\right)_{u}\,.

∎

By
C.2
, we have

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[H]}\left[\forall\,r\in[t],\,w_{r}\in S\right]=\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[\widehat{H}]}\left[\forall\,r\in[t],\,w_{r}\in S\right]\leq\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[\widehat{H}]}\left[\text{$\bf{w}$}\text{ ends in }S\right]\,.

(C.6)

To bound the right-hand side above, we want to resort to the mixing properties of expander. One can indeed argue that
\widehat{H}
is an expander graph.

Claim C.3
.

The graph
\widehat{H}
is a
\psi/3
-expander, i.e
\omega(Q,U\setminus Q)\geq\psi/3\cdot d|Q|
for all
Q\subseteq U
with
|Q|\leq|U|/2
.

Before proving this claim, we show that the statement of
6.34
now easily follows from (
C.6
). Let
\widehat{A},\widehat{\mathcal{L}},\widehat{M}
be the adjacency matrix, normalized Laplacian, and lazy random walk transition matrix of
\widehat{H}
respectively, i.e.
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}=I-\frac{1}{d}\widehat{A}
and
\widehat{M}=\frac{1}{2}I+\frac{1}{2d}\widehat{A}=I-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
. Also, let
0=\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{|U|}
be the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
, and let
x_{1},\dots,x_{|U|}
be the corresponding eigenvectors, where
x_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|U|}}\mathbbm{1}
. Then, one has

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[\widehat{H}]}\left[\text{$\bf{w}$}\text{ ends in }S\right]=\mathbbm{1}_{S}^{\top}\widehat{M}^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{S}\frac{1}{|S|}=\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i=1}^{|U|}\left(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{2}\right)^{t}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{S},x_{i}\rangle^{2}=\frac{|S|}{|U|}+\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i=2}^{|U|}\left(1-\frac{\lambda_{i}}{2}\right)^{t}\langle\mathbbm{1}_{S},x_{i}\rangle^{2}\,.

Here the last equality follows from the fact that
\lambda_{1}=0
and
x_{1}=\mathbbm{1}_{U}/\sqrt{|U|}
. By Cheeger’s inequality, we know that
\lambda_{2}\geq(\psi/3)^{2}/2
, so we continue from above and conclude

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[H]}\left[\forall\,r\in[t],\,w_{r}\in S\right]\leq\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[\widehat{H}]}\left[\text{$\bf{w}$}\text{ ends in }S\right]\leq\frac{|S|}{|U|}+\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i=1}^{|U|}\exp\left(-\frac{\psi^{2}}{36}t\right)\langle\mathbbm{1}_{S},x_{i}\rangle^{2}=\frac{|S|}{|U|}+\exp\left(-\frac{\psi^{2}}{36}t\right)\,,

where the first inequality uses (
C.6
). Recalling that
|U|\geq\lceil\exp(\psi^{2}t/36)\rceil\cdot|S|
, we get the statement of
6.34
. We finish by showing
C.3
.

Proof of
C.3
.

Given a set
Q\subseteq U
with
Q\leq|U|/2
, split
Q
into
Q_{S}=Q\cap S
and
Q_{T}\coloneqq Q\cap T
. We note that

\displaystyle\omega(Q,U\setminus Q)

\displaystyle=\omega(Q_{S},U\setminus Q_{S})-\omega(Q_{S},Q_{T})+\omega(Q_{T},U\setminus Q)

(C.7)

\displaystyle\geq\omega(Q_{S},U\setminus Q_{S})-\omega(Q_{S},Q_{T})+\omega(Q_{T},T\setminus Q_{T})\,.

We now bound the three terms, starting with
\omega(Q_{T},T\setminus Q_{T})
.
By construction of
H
, we have

\omega(Q_{T},T\setminus Q_{T})=|Q_{T}|\cdot|T\setminus Q_{T}|\cdot\frac{1}{|T|-1}\left(d-\frac{1}{|T|}|E^{\prime}(S,V\setminus S)|\right)\,.

(C.8)

By the assumption that
t,\psi\geq 0
, we get
|T|\geq 20(\exp(\psi^{2}t/36))|S|\geq 20|S|
, which rearranges to

|S|\leq\frac{|T|}{20}.

(C.9)

Thus,

|E^{\prime}(S,V\setminus S)|\leq d\cdot|S|\leq d\cdot\frac{|T|}{20}
, which together with Equation (
C.8
) gives

\omega(Q_{T},T\setminus Q_{T})\geq 0.94\cdot|Q_{T}|\cdot|T\setminus Q_{T}|\cdot\frac{d}{|T|}\,.

Using Equation (
C.9
) again, and noting that
|Q_{T}|\leq|Q|\leq|U|/2=(|S|+|T|)/2
, we have

|T\setminus Q_{T}|\geq|T|-\frac{1}{2}(|S|+|T|)=\frac{1}{2}|T|-\frac{1}{2}|S|\geq\frac{19}{40}|T|
. Hence,

\omega(Q_{T},T\setminus Q_{T})\geq 0.94\cdot\frac{19}{40}d\cdot|Q_{T}|>\frac{\psi}{3}d\cdot|Q_{T}|

(C.10)

where the last inequality used the assumption that
\psi\leq 1
. Next, we bound the two terms
\omega(Q_{S},U\setminus Q_{S})-\omega(Q_{S},Q_{T})
together. By construction of
\widehat{H}
, we get

\omega(Q_{S},Q_{T})=\sum_{u\in Q_{S}}\frac{1}{|T|}|E^{\prime}(\{u\},V\setminus S)|\cdot|Q_{T}|=|E^{\prime}(Q_{S},V\setminus S)|\cdot\frac{|Q_{T}|}{|T|}\leq|E^{\prime}(Q_{S},V\setminus Q_{S})|\cdot\frac{2}{3}\,,

(C.11)

where the last inequality uses
Q_{S}\subseteq S
and
\frac{|Q_{T}|}{|T|}\leq\frac{|S|+|T|}{2|T|}\leq\frac{21}{40}|T|
as per
Equation

C.9
. The definition of
\omega
for edges incident on
S
, in conjunction with the expansion property of
S
in
H
, gives

\omega(Q_{S},U\setminus Q_{S})=|E^{\prime}(Q_{S},V\setminus Q_{S})|\geq\psi d|Q_{S}|\,.

(C.12)

Combining (
C.12
) with (
C.11
), we get

\omega(Q_{S},U\setminus Q_{S})-\omega(Q_{S},Q_{T})\geq|E^{\prime}(Q_{S},V\setminus Q_{S})|-|E^{\prime}(Q_{S},V\setminus Q_{S})|\cdot\frac{2}{3}\geq\frac{\psi}{3}d|Q_{S}|\,.

(C.13)

Now we plug (
C.10
) and (
C.13
) into (
C.7
) and get

\omega(Q,U\setminus Q)\geq\frac{\psi}{3}d\cdot|Q_{S}|+\frac{\psi}{3}d\cdot|Q_{T}|=\frac{\psi}{3}d\cdot|Q|\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

∎

C.3
Proof of
6.33

See
6.33

Proof of
6.33
.

Let
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
be the adjacency matrix of
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
respectively, and define the corresponding lazy random walk transition matrices as
M_{i}=\frac{1}{2}I+\frac{1}{2d}A_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Then, letting
P=\text{diag}(\mathbbm{1}_{S})
we have

\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t}_{S}[H_{i}]}\left[\forall\,r\in[t],\,w_{r}\in S\right]=\mathbbm{1}_{S}^{\top}(PM_{i})^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{S}\frac{1}{|S|}\,.

By virtue of this rewriting, it suffices to show

\mathbbm{1}_{S}^{\top}(PM_{1})^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{S}\frac{1}{|S|}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{S}^{\top}(PM_{2})^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{S}\frac{1}{|S|}+t\frac{|E_{2}\setminus E_{1}|}{2d|S|}\,.

(C.14)

Consider the matrix
M_{1}-M_{2}
. Since both
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are
d
-regular, the sum of values in any row of
M_{1}-M_{2}
is 0. Moreover,
(M_{1}-M_{2})_{u,v}=-1/(2d)
for all
u\neq v
if
(u,v)\in E_{2}\setminus E_{1}
and 0 otherwise, which means that
(M_{1}-M_{2})_{u,u}=(l_{1}(u)-l_{2}(u))/(2d)=|\{v\in V:(u,v)\in E_{2}\setminus E_{1}\}|/(2d)
.

Let
H=(V,E_{2}\setminus E_{1})
be the graph induced by the edges
E_{2}\setminus E_{1}
. Observe that

M_{1}-M_{2}=\frac{1}{2d}L_{H},

where
L_{H}
is the unnormalised Laplacian of
H
. We apply
C.5
with
X=PM_{1}
and
Y=PM_{2}
, and get

(PM_{1})^{t}=(PM_{2})^{t}+\frac{1}{2d}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}(PM_{2})^{i}PL_{H}(PM_{1})^{t-i-1}\,.

In particular, this gives

\mathbbm{1}_{S}^{\top}(PM_{1})^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{S}\frac{1}{|S|}=\mathbbm{1}_{S}^{\top}(PM_{2})^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{S}\frac{1}{|S|}+\frac{1}{2d|S|}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\mathbbm{1}_{S}^{\top}(PM_{2})^{i}PL_{H}(PM_{1})^{t-i-1}\mathbbm{1}_{S}\,.

We use the following claim to bound each term in the sum above.

Claim C.4
.

For all
i=0,\dots,t-1
, one has

\mathbbm{1}_{S}^{\top}(PM_{2})^{i}PL_{H}(PM_{1})^{t-i-1}\mathbbm{1}_{S}\leq|E_{2}\setminus E_{1}|\,.

Proof of
C.4
.

Let
i=0,\dots,t-1
, and define
x=(PM_{1})^{t-i-1}\mathbbm{1}_{S}
and
y=P(M_{2}P)^{i}\mathbbm{1}_{S}=(PM_{2})^{i}\mathbbm{1}_{S}
. We note that
x,y
are vectors with support contained in
S
. Observe that
L_{H}=B_{H}^{T}B_{H}
, where
B_{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{|E_{2}\setminus E_{1}|\times|V|}
is the incidence matrix of
H
, where, just to formally define
B_{H}
, edges in
H
are directed arbitrarily. Since
E_{2}\setminus E_{1}\subseteq E(S,V\setminus S)
, one has

y^{\top}L_{H}x=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(u,v)\in E_{2}\setminus E_{1};\\
u\in S,\ v\in V\setminus S\end{subarray}}(y_{u}-y_{v})(x_{u}-x_{v})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(u,v)\in E_{2}\setminus E_{1};\\
u\in S,\ v\in V\setminus S\end{subarray}}y_{u}x_{u}\,,

where the last equation follows from the fact that
x
and
y
are both 0 at any coordinate
v
such that
v\notin S
.
To continue, let us fix
u\in S
and expand out the definition of
x,y
to write

\displaystyle y_{u}x_{u}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{u}^{\top}(PM_{2})^{i}\mathbbm{1}_{S}\mathbbm{1}_{u}^{\top}(PM_{1})^{t-i-1}\mathbbm{1}_{S}

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in S}\mathbbm{1}_{u}^{\top}(PM_{2})^{i}\mathbbm{1}_{z}\mathbbm{1}_{u}^{\top}(PM_{1})^{t-i-1}\mathbbm{1}_{S}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbbm{1}_{u}^{\top}(PM_{2})^{i}\mathbbm{1}_{u}\right)\left(\mathbbm{1}_{u}^{\top}(PM_{1})^{t-i-1}\mathbbm{1}_{S}\right)\,.

We now bound both factors above by
1
: if
i=0
, then trivially
\mathbbm{1}_{u}^{\top}(PM_{2})^{i}\mathbbm{1}_{u}=1
, otherwise we can write

\mathbbm{1}_{u}^{\top}(PM_{2})^{i}\mathbbm{1}_{u}=\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{i}_{u}[H_{2}]}\left[w_{i}=u\text{ and }\forall\,r\in[i],\,\text{$\bf{w}$}\in S\right]\leq 1\,,

and analogously if
i=t-1
we trivially have
\mathbbm{1}_{u}^{\top}(PM_{1})^{t-i-1}\mathbbm{1}_{S}=1
, otherwise we bound

\mathbbm{1}_{u}^{\top}(PM_{1})^{t-i-1}\mathbbm{1}_{S}=\Pr_{\text{$\bf{w}$}\sim p^{t-i-1}_{u}[H_{1}]}\left[\forall\,r\in[i],\,\text{$\bf{w}$}\in S\right]\leq 1\,.

Hence, every term in (
C.3
) is bounded by
1
and we can conclude.
∎

One can see that
C.4
gives (
C.14
), and the statement is proven.
∎

Fact C.5
.

Let
X,\Delta
be real square matrices, let
Y=X-\Delta
, and let
t\geq 1
be an integer. Then, one has

X^{t}=Y^{t}+\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}Y^{i}\Delta X^{t-i-1}\,.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction on
t
. The base case for
t=1
is trivial as
X=Y+\Delta
. For the inductive step, let us assume that

X^{t}=Y^{t}+\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}Y^{i}\Delta X^{t-i-1}\,.

Then, we have

X^{t+1}=X^{t}\cdot X=\left(Y^{t}+\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}Y^{i}\Delta X^{t-i-1}\right)X

by the inductive hypothesis. We distribute
X
and obtain

X^{t+1}=Y^{t}X+\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}Y^{i}\Delta X^{t-i-1}X=Y^{t+1}+Y^{t}\Delta+\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}Y^{i}\Delta X^{(t+1)-i-1}=Y^{t+1}+\sum_{i=0}^{t}Y^{i}\Delta X^{(t+1)-i-1}\,,

which proves the identity for
t+1
.
∎

C.4
Proof of Lemma
7.8

See
7.8

Proof of Lemma
7.8
.

For every real symmetric matrix
A
, it holds that
\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(A^{\top}A)}=\max_{i\in[n]}\{|\lambda_{i}(A)|\}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\|P-P^{*}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\max_{\|x\|_{2}=1}\|(P-P^{*})x\|_{2}

(C.15)

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in[n]}\{|\lambda_{i}(P-P^{*})|\}\qquad\text{ since $P-P^{*}$ is symmetric}

\displaystyle=\max_{\|x\|_{2}=1}|x^{\top}(P-P^{*})x|

\displaystyle=\max_{\|x\|_{2}=1}\left|x^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)x\right|\,.

Claim C.6
.

For all
x\in\mathrm{span}(\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k})
, it holds that

\left|x^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)x\right|\leq\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\,.

Proof.

Suppose
x\in\mathrm{span}(\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k})
. Then
x=U_{[k]}\alpha
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
We will now show that

x^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\right)x=\alpha^{\top}\left(I-\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right)\alpha\,.

First, note that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}=U_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{\top}\,.

(C.16)

Furthermore, note that
\{(1/\sqrt{|C_{i}|})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{C_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{k}
is a set of
k
orthonormal eigenvectors of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}
with eigenvalue
0
, and therefore they span the eigenspace of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{in}}
corresponding to eigenvalue
0
(otherwise, the orthogonal component gives an additional
(k+1)
-th
0
-eigenvalue, which is impossible as
\lambda_{k+1}\geq\phi^{2}/2
by
Lemma

3.2
). Consequently, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}^{*}{v_{i}^{*}}^{\top}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}\mathbbm{1}_{C_{i}}\mathbbm{1}_{C_{i}}^{\top}\,.

We also have that

\displaystyle\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}\sum_{u\in C_{i}}f_{u}=\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}\sum_{u\in C_{i}}U_{[k]}^{\top}\mathbbm{1}_{u}=\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}U_{[k]}^{\top}\sum_{u\in C_{i}}\mathbbm{1}_{u}=\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}U_{[k]}^{\top}\mathbbm{1}_{C_{i}}\,,

which gives

\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}U_{[k]}^{\top}\mathbbm{1}_{C_{i}}\mathbbm{1}_{C_{i}}^{\top}U_{[k]}=U_{[k]}^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{|C_{i}|}\mathbbm{1}_{C_{i}}\mathbbm{1}_{C_{i}}^{\top}\right)U_{[k]}=U_{[k]}^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}^{*}{v_{i}^{*}}^{\top}\right)U_{[k]}\,.

(C.17)

Combining (
C.16
) and (
C.17
), and recalling that
x=U_{[k]}\alpha
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we obtain

\displaystyle x^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}^{*}{v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\right)x

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\top}U_{[k]}^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\right)U_{[k]}\alpha

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\top}U_{[k]}^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)U_{[k]}\alpha-\alpha^{\top}U_{[k]}^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}^{*}{v_{i}^{*}}^{\top}\right)U_{[k]}\alpha

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\top}U_{[k]}^{\top}U_{[k]}U_{[k]}^{\top}U_{[k]}\alpha-\alpha^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right)\alpha

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\top}\left(I-\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right)\alpha\,,

where the last equality follows by the fact that
U_{[k]}^{\top}U_{[k]}=I_{k}.

Thus, applying Lemma
3.10
, we obtain

\left|x^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)x\right|=\left|\alpha^{\top}\left(I-\sum_{i=1}^{k}|C_{i}|\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}\right)\alpha\right|\leq\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|\alpha\|^{2}_{2}=\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|U_{[k]}^{\top}x\|^{2}_{2}=\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\,.

∎

Claim C.7
.

For all
x\in\mathrm{span}\left(\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}\right)^{\perp}
, it holds that

\left|x^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)x\right|\leq k\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\,.

Proof.

Suppose not, and let
x\in\mathrm{span}(\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k})^{\perp}
be a unit norm vector with
|x^{\top}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top})x|>k{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}/{\phi}
. Since
x\perp v_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,k
, we have

k\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}<\left|x^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)x\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle x,v_{i}^{*}\rangle^{2}.

(C.18)

Furthermore,
v_{1},\dots,v_{k},x
are
(k+1)
orthonormal vectors, so we can extend them to an orthonormal basis
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k},x,u_{k+2},\ldots u_{n}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some vectors
u_{i}
.
Expanding
v_{1}^{*},\ldots,v_{k}^{*}
with respect to this basis, we obtain

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|v_{i}^{*}\|^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k}\langle v_{i}^{*},v_{j}\rangle^{2}+\langle v_{i}^{*},x\rangle^{2}+\sum_{j=k+2}^{n}\langle v_{i}^{*},u_{j}\rangle^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\langle v_{i}^{*},v_{j}\rangle^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle v_{i}^{*},x\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle>\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}^{*}{v_{i}^{*}}^{\top}\right)v_{j}+k\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\qquad\text{by~\eqref{eq:xvi*}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}{v_{i}}^{\top}\right)v_{j}-\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|v_{i}\|^{2}_{2}+k\frac{4\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\qquad\text{by applying \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:inspan} to $v_{1},\dots,v_{k}$}

\displaystyle=k\,,

which is a contradiction.
∎

To complete the proof of the lemma, we need to show that
\max_{\|x\|_{2}=1}|x^{\top}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top})x|\leq 8\sqrt{k{\sqrt{\epsilon}}/{\phi}}
. Given any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\|x\|_{2}=1
, decompose
x=u+w
for vectors
u\in\mathrm{span}(\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k})
,
w\in\mathrm{span}(\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k})^{\perp}
. Applying
C.6
and
C.7
to
u
and
w
, respectively, we get

\displaystyle\left|x^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)x\right|

\displaystyle=\left|(u+w)^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)(u+w)\right|

(C.19)

\displaystyle\leq\left|u^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)u\right|+\left|w^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)w\right|

\displaystyle+2\left|u^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)w\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|u\|^{2}_{2}+4k\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|w\|^{2}_{2}+2\left|u^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)w\right|\,.

To bound the term
2|u^{\top}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top})w|
, note that

\displaystyle\left|u^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)w\right|

\displaystyle=\left|u^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}\right)w\right|\qquad\text{since $w\perp v_{i}$ for all $i\in[k]$}

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle u,v_{i}^{*}\rangle\langle w,v_{i}^{*}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle u,v_{i}^{*}\rangle^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle w,v_{i}^{*}\rangle^{2}\right)}\qquad\text{by Cauchy-Schwarz}

\displaystyle\leq\|u\|_{2}\sqrt{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle w,v_{i}^{*}\rangle^{2}\right)}

\displaystyle=\|u\|_{2}\sqrt{w^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)w}\qquad\text{since $w\perp v_{i}$ for all $i\in[k]$}

\displaystyle\leq\|u\|_{2}\|w\|_{2}\cdot 2k^{1/2}\frac{\epsilon^{1/4}}{\phi^{1/2}}\qquad\text{by Claim \ref{claim:inspanperp}.}

Substituting back into (
C.19
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|x^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)x\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|u\|^{2}_{2}+4k\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|w\|^{2}_{2}+2\left|u^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\right)w\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|u\|^{2}_{2}+4k\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}\|w\|^{2}_{2}+4\|u\|_{2}\|w\|_{2}\cdot k^{1/2}\frac{\epsilon^{1/4}}{\phi^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq 8\max\left\{k\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi},\sqrt{k\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}}\right\}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\qquad\text{using the fact that $\|u\|^{2}_{2},\|w\|^{2}_{2}\leq\|x\|^{2}_{2}$}

\displaystyle=8\sqrt{k\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\qquad\text{since $k\leq\frac{\phi}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}$, by the lemma assumption.}

Finally, combining with (
C.15
), we get

\|P-P^{*}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\max_{\|x\|_{2}=1}|x^{\top}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{v_{i}^{*}v^{*}_{i}}^{\top}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top})x|\leq 8\sqrt{k\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi}}
.
∎
